Any resemblance to actual persons, living or dead, actual events, or suspiciously familiar situations is probably not coincidental. In fact, if you see yourself in these pages, you may want to spend a few minutes reflecting on the life choices that brought you here.

One Last Thing



Author's Note
This novel is the first in The Relativistic Investigation Bureau series. The physics discussed herein is real—relativity, time dilation, spacetime geometry, and holographic duality are genuine concepts from modern physics. The crimes and investigations are fictional. The jokes are mine.
If you find yourself questioning whether time is fundamental, whether causality can be violated, or whether a sufficiently incompetent filing system could create paradoxes... congratulations. You're thinking like an investigator.
The Bureau appreciates your attention to detail.

How to Read This Book
This is a satirical mystery with real relativity underneath. You need no physics to follow the case. The comedy is the bait. The geometry is the trap.
The novel is Case 1047: a murder that has not happened yet, filed early by a Bureau that confuses event labels with corpses. Derek Gent investigates his own future death while trying not to erase his daughter from a doorway she should never have stood in.
The course lives in the Appendix as sixteen lessons. Most chapters end the same way: a Rule, and a single link to your lesson. Follow it. Each lesson should survive the hot-chair test: say the idea plainly to someone who has never heard the jargon — no phone, no notes, no hiding behind Greek letters.
Three honest ways to read this book.
For the story alone: read the chapters and stop at each Rule. No plot point lives only in the lessons.
To take the class: read each chapter, then its lesson, in order. Lesson 1 assumes nothing; Lesson 16 assumes you have been paying attention.
If you are impatient: skim the COURSE page and Lesson 16. You will still meet the brass clock.
If you take the class out of order, take Lesson 2 before you argue with Chapter 3, and Lesson 14 before you board the train. The novel will not punish you. The algebra might.
One promise: where Newton cannot host this mystery, the book says so. Absolute 'now' makes Case 1047 gibberish. Relativity makes the misfiling possible — and the fear of it real.
A companion list of films lives at the end of each lesson. None of them are required. All of them were chosen because they teach the same idea the chapter just used as a joke.
Three interactive demos — drag simultaneity, curvature, and cosmic expansion around until they behave — live at the book's companion demo site, built to be broken gently rather than read.
PROLOGUE
In Which the Universe Refuses to Behave
The murder had not happened yet. This would have been unremarkable, except that Derek Gent was already dead on paper—and someone had remembered to file the necessary forms.
The time of death had been entered. The location had been entered. The victim had been identified.
The victim was Derek Gent. Derek was alive. He was also the sort of man who noticed discrepancies in official paperwork. This particular discrepancy was rather difficult to ignore. 
The paperwork predicted his death a day before it was due to occur. Derek therefore did what any sensible investigator would do.
He checked the date, then the time, then the old clock.
Then the clock again, because the first check had produced a result he found professionally inconvenient. 11:17. The mysterious clock had stopped.
Then there was Sherlock, a dolphin who worked remotely as an occasional Bureau consultant and was paid in fish. Nobody had yet found this arrangement stranger than the cases he commented on. He had never been asked to sit the capstone, which was probably why he passed it.
The Bureau's official consultant-fee schedule still listed dolphins under Miscellaneous Marine. Accounts had queried it twice. Both queries had been answered, correctly, with a receipt for mackerel.
For the same reason, when Derek found a murder notice announcing his own future death, he did not immediately conclude that he was being hunted by a time traveller, a quantum ghost or an unusually vindictive neighbour. 
The case would eventually force an even less comfortable possibility: that the Bureau's own computer was not merely recording the crime, but mis-filing a future event as one.
He began with the evidence. 
A stopped clock.
A future date. 
A murder that had not happened. 
And, eventually, a distant, mysterious train.
There was only one sensible response. Investigate. 
Preferably before 11:17. 
Rule: A murder that has not happened yet is still a filing. Treat the paperwork as evidence.

-> After Chapter 1, take Lesson 1 - Special Relativity Is Rude
Chapter 1
The Surprise in the Morning
At precisely 9:17 on a Tuesday morning, Derek Gent received an email informing him that he was dead. His daughter, Sophie, was twelve years old. He checked the time on his phone and decided that one of these facts might be wrong. 
Derek had always suspected that death would arrive by email. In this, as in so many other matters, he had been only slightly ahead of the general population — a position he found both comforting and faintly depressing, which was, on balance, better than the alternatives. The alternatives were rarely an improvement, a fact Derek had verified more than once, usually at personal cost and never with a sense of novelty.
Not because he was especially pessimistic, but because nearly everything important seemed to arrive by email these days. Electricity bills. Convenient ways to re-enter bank account numbers for verification. Invitations to life-changing webinars. A unique opportunity to cooperate with a prince in Nigeria. Notifications that someone had changed a password Derek had never used. Insurance recommendations. Death was simply the logical next step. What surprised him was the sender.
The message had come from the Derek Gent Relativistic Investigation Bureau. Derek stared at the screen. The Bureau was not supposed to send him emails. Derek had written the rule himself, which made its violation particularly irritating.
It was his Bureau. More precisely, it was a small private investigative organisation occupying two rooms on the second floor of an old office building in London, plus a corridor which the landlord insisted was a communal area and Derek insisted belonged to his Bureau whenever he needed somewhere to dry his brolly. The official paperwork still said two rooms. The official paperwork, Derek had long ago concluded, was aspirational. The whole floor smelled faintly of instant coffee, old carpet, and the particular dust that only accumulates in buildings old enough to have opinions about it. Radiators clanked on a schedule nobody had ever agreed to, and the single working lightbulb in the corridor buzzed at a frequency Trevor had once measured out of boredom and never mentioned again. The Bureau dealt primarily with unusual cases.
This was partly because Derek liked unusual cases and partly because ordinary cases paid terribly. The sign on the frosted glass door read:
DEREK GENT
RELATIVISTIC INVESTIGATION BUREAU
Underneath, in smaller letters:
CAUSALITY, CHRONOLOGY &
OTHER INCONVENIENCES
And underneath that, added by Penny several years earlier:
NO MURDERS BEFORE 10 A.M.
WITHOUT AN APPOINTMENT
Derek had objected. Penny had noted that they had never actually received a case before ten.
Derek had said that this was beside the point.
Penny had stated that this was precisely the point.
Derek had lost that argument years ago and had, as a matter of policy, continued to lose it every morning since. The sign was still right. The email was still early.
He was now looking at an email from the Bureau. The subject line was:
CASE UPDATE: DEREK GENT
He opened it.
Dear Mr Gent,
We regret to inform you that you have been
murdered.
The incident will occur tomorrow at 11:03 a.m.
The perpetrator has been identified. It is you.
Kind regards,
Derek Gent
Relativistic Investigation Bureau.
Derek read it again.
Then a third time. He glanced at the sender's address. It was correct.
Then he rapped his knuckles against the Bureau's computer. It was his computer.
Everything appeared to be where it ought to be. Derek did not find this reassuring.
The filing cabinet was locked. The kettle was where the kettle normally was, which was not necessarily where it ought to be.
Outside, a bus pulled away from the kerb with a noise suggesting that several of its components had voted against the decision. Rain tapped the window. Somewhere downstairs, the landlord was drilling into something which, judging by previous work, would shortly become the Bureau's responsibility.
Derek gazed at the email again, with the particular stillness of a man who has just been handed a problem that refuses to sit neatly in any of the usual categories. The Bureau had categories for almost everything. This was not among them. A new category would no doubt be invented once the damage was complete.
There was, he decided, a useful distinction between the things a man could act on and the things he could merely be informed of. Whether he died tomorrow fell into the second category, and no amount of staring at the screen would move it into the first. Whether Sophie was safe did not. That one, at least, he could still do something about — he was already reaching for the phone before he'd finished the thought — and it seemed the more efficient use of the morning.
Derek was thirty-eight, greying at the temples in a way he found more alarming than the murder notice, and built like a man who had once been reasonably athletic and had since made his peace with filing cabinets instead. He had a habit, under stress, of straightening things that were already straight — a stapler, a mug, the corner of a report nobody would ever read — as though order in small objects might eventually negotiate order in larger ones. "That's inconvenient," he commented, as if the matter were primarily a filing error. He did not yet know that the joke was the correct diagnosis — and that the email was not a warning so much as Exhibit Zero, delivered early.
Being confronted with "Apparently I've been murdered" made Derek feel considerably less useful. Being confronted with a Bureau category that did not yet exist made him feel worse.
Across the empty room, Trevor Boltzman looked up. Trevor was twenty-eight, tall in the specific way that seemed designed to make doorframes personal, with the kind of permanently rumpled sandy hair that suggested he had once owned a comb and lost the argument with it. He was highly intelligent, technically qualified, and possessed of the unfortunate tendency to think about one problem at a time — a trait that made him excellent at mathematics and intermittently disastrous at life. When thinking deeply, he carried himself like a frozen Windows system before reboot. 
His voice usually carried a rising lift to it, like someone perpetually one sentence from an interesting discovery — the same lift that vanished entirely under real strain, replaced by something quieter and more careful. He sped up when he was anxious and slowed abruptly when he was certain, the gear-change audible before he had finished a sentence.
He had a sister, Tabitha, who was generally considered the more dangerous of the two, a fact Trevor mentioned only when cornered, and sometimes not even then.
Derek, for his part, had Sophie — twelve, precise, already suspicious of adult explanations — currently at school, which was supposed to be the safest sentence in London.
Sophie's mother, Tuppence — a name whose irony Derek had once pointed out, to no discernible improvement in the conversation — had left three years earlier, for a man named Julian who had made an improbable fortune manufacturing clothes hangers. She had told him, with the specific clarity she reserved for important announcements, that he did not make enough money for her needs — a diagnosis Derek found difficult to dispute, given that the Bureau's ordinary cases paid terribly and its unusual ones paid worse. When Derek had attempted to point out that the Bureau offered a more relativistic kind of reward, she had considered this for exactly as long as it deserved. "Relativistic, my arse," she said, and was gone by Thursday. Derek had filed the entire matter under Processed — Do Not Reopen, which worked well enough, most days.
Tuppence had been, by every account Derek retained without particularly wanting to, immaculately put together — the sort of competence that read as polish, heels chosen for effect rather than comfort, opinions delivered as though already finalised by committee. It was, he privately conceded, an entirely reasonable set of qualities to want from a life. He had simply never managed to embody them himself.
None of which made her a bad mother. She rang Sophie every Sunday without fail, remembered the names of Sophie's friends better than Derek generally managed, and had never once, in three years, made Sophie feel like an inconvenience to either household. Whatever she and Derek had failed to be for each other, she had not extended the failure to their daughter.
At his desk, Trevor removed his glasses — a small, unhelpful tic, giving no reliable clue whether insight or complete bafflement was about to follow. "What?"
Derek had stopped trying to predict it years ago. "I've been murdered."
"Recently?"
"Tomorrow."
"Tomorrow?"
"Yes."
Trevor considered this carefully. "You're telling me you've been murdered tomorrow?"
"Yes."
Trevor inclined his head. "Ah."
Derek waited. Trevor put his glasses back on. 
Derek continued waiting, "Why did you say 'ah'?"
"I wasn't sure what else to say. 'Ah' seemed to cover the available options."
"How about 'that's impossible'? It has a certain traditional appeal."
"I thought you'd already know that. You usually do."
Derek peered at him. "Trevor, I have been working in this field for seventeen years."
"Yes."
"I know the difference between something being impossible and something being highly inconvenient."
"Which is this now?"
Derek held up the screen. "I'm not sure yet."
Trevor stood. "Tea?"
"Yes."
"Milk?"
"No."
"Sugar?"
"One."
Trevor accepted this and walked towards the kitchen. Derek called after him, "Two."
Trevor stopped. "Two?"
"Yes."
Trevor continued. A moment later there was a knock at the door.
Before Derek could answer, the door opened. Penny Gent, Derek's cousin, stepped in without waiting for an invitation. She had never been much given to waiting for invitations, a trait that had served her well and other people less so. She also had the disconcerting ability to look as if she had somewhere more interesting to be, even when she was standing in a room full of stopped clocks.
She was also one of those people who always appeared to have been designed specifically to make other people lose their train of thought. She was a striking brunette, though Derek had never used the word in her presence because he valued his life and had no particular desire to become the subject of a police report — or worse, a footnote. She wore a dark coat, carried a handbag, and had the calm expression of someone who had already dealt with several irritating people before breakfast and was prepared, if necessary, to deal with several more.
Her voice was usually calm, almost amused, right up until she was impatient — which was often — at which point it went quick and flat, agreement and sarcasm nearly indistinguishable until you knew her well enough to hear the difference.
She eyed Derek. "Morning."
"Morning."
She put her handbag down. "Why do you look like that?"
"Like what?"
"Like you've just discovered the Inland Revenue has found you."
"I've been murdered."
Penny paused. "Already?"
"Apparently."
"Who did it?"
"I did."
She conceded. "Suppose so."
"You're taking this remarkably well."
"It's Tuesday."
Derek crossed his arms. "That doesn't explain anything."
"It explains most things."
She walked over to the computer. "Show me."
Derek turned the monitor towards her. Penny read the email. Her expression changed.
Only slightly. Penny was clever. Very clever.
Unlike Trevor, who approached every new piece of information as if it might require a spreadsheet, Penny seemed to absorb things almost before anyone had spoken. She read the mysterious message again. "Tomorrow at eleven-oh-three."
"Yes."
"Not approximately eleven."
"No."
"Eleven-oh-three."
"Yes."
"That's very specific."
"I thought so."
"And the murderer is you."
"Apparently."
Penny regarded Derek. "Are you planning to murder yourself?"
"Not consciously."
"Good."
She pulled out a chair. "I'd hate to think you'd become predictable."
He grinned lightly, somewhat against his own will. Trevor came back carrying three mugs.
"Milk?"
"None," Derek noted.
Trevor handed Penny her mug. "Thanks."
Then he handed Derek the other one. Derek took a sip before looking sharply at Trevor. "Where's yours?"
"I didn't make one."
"Why not?"
"I thought there were only two of us."
Penny met his gaze. Trevor looked at Penny. Then at Derek.
"Oh." Derek sighed softly.
"Trevor."
"Yes?"
"That's Penny."
"I know."
"My cousin."
Trevor replied, "I know."
"She's been here for approximately ninety seconds."
"Yes."
"And yet somehow you've already forgotten that she exists," countered Derek.
"I haven't forgotten she exists."
"You made two cups of tea."
"I thought you didn't want one."
"I have one."
Trevor stared silently at Derek's mug. "Right."
Penny grinned, clearly amused. "Don't worry. He's usually this bright."
"I'm sitting right here."
"We know."
Derek turned back to the computer. Something was still bothering him, and it was not the murder itself.
The date was the problem. He clicked the attachment.
A photograph appeared. For a moment Derek stopped breathing. The room felt briefly smaller, as if the air itself had taken an interest. He recognised the feeling: it was the same one that arrived just before a theory collapsed, or a relationship, or both. Somewhere beneath the professional irritation, something colder and more personal had stirred — the quiet recognition that the face in the photograph was not merely a case, but a deadline. 
Derek had never liked deadlines that looked back at him. Most deadlines had the decency to remain abstract. 
Derek filed the sensation under 'to be examined later' and continued working. He was good at filing things under 'later'. It was one of his more reliable skills, and possibly one of his more dangerous ones. 
Trevor noticed. 
Penny asked, "What?"
Derek turned the monitor towards them. The photograph showed their office. Not a similar office.
Not a room that resembled it. The actual room. The same battered desk.
The same green filing cabinet. The same window overlooking the street. The same framed photograph of Derek's grandfather standing beside a Morris Minor in 1964.
And on the floor, face-down beside the familiar desk, was Derek. He was face-down, his head turned sufficiently towards the camera for identification. His right arm was beneath his chest. Blood had spread across the back of his shirt. In the doorway behind him stood a girl of twelve, frozen mid-step, holding a school bag as if she had opened the wrong door into the wrong Tuesday. Sophie. His daughter. Who was, at that moment, supposed to be safely at school.
Trevor went very still.
Penny leaned closer. "That's you. And that's Sophie."
Penny's voice went flat and fast. "Then we're not debating photographs. We're finding out where she actually is. Now."
"Yes." Derek's voice had gone thin. "She isn't in this room."
He was already dialling. School office. Two rings. "Mr Gent? Sophie's in double maths. Shall I fetch her?" "No," he managed, too quickly. "Just — is she there? Can you see her?" A pause. Paper rustle. "She's at the second desk from the window. Looking bored, if that helps." Derek's grip on the phone tightened until his fingernails bit crescents into his palm. His breath came shallow. "It helps. Don't tell her I called. She can't know I'm afraid." He hung up. His hand was not steady.
Penny watched him. "She's at school."
"She's at school," Derek repeated, as if repeating could keep the photograph from being true. "And she is also in that doorway. I will not choose which one is real until I understand the frame."
Trevor cleared his throat. "Shouldn't we ring the actual police? There's blood in that photograph. Real detectives exist. Scotland Yard, presumably, still answers its own telephone."
Penny didn't look up from the screen. "And tell them what? That you've been murdered tomorrow, the body is also you, and it's currently standing in this room complaining about the coffee? They'd section you before they'd file it." She tapped the timestamp. "This isn't a crime yet. It's a filing error with excellent production values. The moment it becomes an actual body in an actual location, we call. Until then, it's ours, because nobody else's paperwork has a drawer for it."
Trevor considered this. "That seems like a very convenient rule."
"It's the rule that keeps us out of a police interview room explaining reference frames to a sergeant. Pick your battles." Penny allowed herself a small, unkind smile. "Besides. Scotland Yard would spend the first six weeks establishing whose jurisdiction a murder that hasn't happened yet actually falls under, and would consider the question far too important to rush on your account. This office, whatever else is wrong with it, doesn't require a crime to have the decency of occurring before we're willing to investigate it."
Trevor, carefully not looking at either of them, zoomed the image. "There's something on the desk."
Derek peered at the screen. A small, silent brass railway clock stood beside the computer, looking entirely ordinary and entirely wrong, which was, in Derek's experience, the most dangerous combination. He had the sudden, unwelcome sense that the clock had been waiting for him.
"Fair enough."
Penny examined him. "You don't recognise it?"
"No."
"Neither do I. Not from anywhere real."
"What is it?"
"An old railway clock."
Derek looked again. The old clock showed 11:03. 
He inspected the clock on his own desk. 9:21.
He considered his computer: 9:21.
His phone and the wall clock both read 9:21.
He turned back to the photograph. 11:03.
Derek leaned back. "Interesting."
Penny's brow tightened. "What?"
"That photograph wasn't taken tomorrow."
Trevor frowned. "Then when was it taken?"
Derek indicated the picture. "The clock reads eleven-oh-three."
"So?"
"Tomorrow's murder is at eleven-oh-three."
"Yes."
"And the photograph was apparently taken at the moment of my alleged death."
"Yes."
Trevor waited. Derek glanced at him. 
Trevor pivoted back. "So?"
Derek looked at him. "Trevor, you really must learn to recognise a mystery when it is standing on the table waving its arms."
Trevor stared at the photograph. "Oh."
Penny smirked. "There he is."
Trevor leaned closer. "Hang on. If the clock in the photo reads eleven-oh-three and the clock in this room reads nine-twenty-one, then the photograph was taken two hours later." He wore the pleased expression of a man who had just solved something.
"No," Penny corrected. "It isn't."
Trevor's expression collapsed with the quiet dignity of a man who has just watched a carefully constructed theory meet reality and lose. It was a look Derek had come to recognise, and almost respect. "Oh."
Derek got up and walked to the window. 
Outside, London was grey and wet. A bus stopped at the kerb, and another stopped behind it.
Derek tapped a rhythm against the windowsill. London was, as usual, being mildly inconvenient.
Penny raised her eyes. "Your impending murder?"
"Other than that."
"Your Bureau?"
"Also other than that."
Trevor thought. "Your cousin?"
Penny looked at him. Trevor immediately reconsidered his life choices.
But Derek's mouth twitched. "No."
He tapped the photograph. "We have a clock reading eleven-oh-three and a room full of clocks reading nine-twenty-one. Before we call either one wrong, we need to ask what kind of number we are looking at."
Trevor's brow creased. "A time. Obviously. Times are what clocks show — until they aren't."
"Sometimes," Derek said. "Sometimes a clock is labelling an event. Those are not the same job."
Penny looked at Sophie in the photograph. "So the death is the event. Eleven-oh-three might only be a label. What I care about is that she is standing inside it."
"Yes. And a photograph records light arriving somewhere, not a view from nowhere. If this image came out of our own system, it may not be a camera at all."
Trevor looked at the clock, then at Penny, then at the maths he trusted more than either. "Then we test the labels. Carefully."
Derek inclined his head at the brass clock, not at either of them. "Measure. Then fight. In that order."
Penny enlarged the lower-right corner of the image. She did not speak for a long time. The three lines sat there like a spell the Bureau had not meant to cast: Death. Departure. Arrival.
There was something in the lower-right corner. A piece of paper.
Penny zoomed in. It contained three lines.
11:03 - DEATH
11:17 - DEPARTURE
12:04 - ARRIVAL
Below them:
DO NOT TRUST THE CLOCKS.
Rule: A clock can lie by telling the wrong kind of truth.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 1 - Special Relativity Is Rude
Chapter 2
Case 1047
For several seconds, nobody spoke. The three-line note sat on the screen like a timetable written by someone who already knew the journey.
Penny read it. "That's ominous."
"Very."
"Could 'departure' mean the train?"
"Perhaps."
"Arrival?"
"Perhaps."
"Death?"
"Almost certainly me."
Trevor shifted uneasily.
Penny faced him. "That's a surprisingly weak argument."
"It has served me well."
Derek zoomed in again. 
The silent clock in the photograph wasn't showing exactly 11:03. 
It showed 11:03:17.
Derek leaned in.
He looked at his own clock. 9:27:17.
He checked his phone. 9:27:17. 
The wall clock. 9:27:17. 
Derek tapped the clock face. "Seventeen seconds." 
He said it quietly, as if the number itself might change if spoken too loudly. 
"Same."
Derek allowed. "That's interesting."
Penny leaned closer. "Why?"
"Because if this were simply a badly set clock, there's no reason for the seconds to line up."
Trevor thought about that. "Could someone have set it deliberately?"
"Yes."
Penny fixed him with a look. 
Trevor agreed quickly. "Exactly."
Derek opened the Bureau's internal records. 
The system showed no record of the ancient brass clock. 
No purchase.
No inventory entry. No photograph. No mention of it.
Derek searched the building's security footage. Nothing. He checked the office camera.
The old desk was empty from 9:16 to 9:19. At 9:20, the ancient brass clock appeared. Trevor stared. "How?"
Derek replayed it. The clock simply suddenly appeared. There was no hand.
There was no person and no shadow. One frame showed nothing.
The next showed the clock. Penny watched Derek. "That's not normal."
"No."
"No."
"Could the camera have glitched?"
"Yes."
"Did it?"
"No."
"How do you know?"
"Because the clock casts a shadow."
Penny studied it again, more carefully this time. It did. The shadow moved normally as the light from the window changed.
Derek replayed the footage. The silent clock existed. Then it didn't.
Then it existed again. Trevor's expression deteriorated by degrees.
"Could someone have edited the footage?"
"Yes."
"Could they have edited the photograph?"
"Yes."
"So, how do we know anything is real?"
Derek met his eyes. "Now you're asking the right question."
Trevor didn't look reassured. Derek slowly opened another file. "Let's start with the clocks."
He took the ancient brass clock from the photograph and compared its mechanism, its hands, and its case against the Bureau's own clocks. All different, except the second hand: identical. Seventeen seconds.
Derek frowned. Penny noticed.
"Another problem?"
"Yes."
"How many are we up to?"
"Seven."
Trevor raised his eyebrows. "Seven?"
"At least."
Derek turned the clear photograph upside down. There was a small number written on the back. 1047.
He reviewed the case database. Case 1047 did not exist. He searched deleted cases.
Nothing. He searched archived cases. Nothing.
He searched future-dated cases. Nothing. 
Then the computer made a noise. A soft chime. A new case appeared.
CASE 1047
CLIENT: Derek Gent
STATUS: ACTIVE
Derek went quiet. Penny leaned over. "What does it say?"
Derek scrolled. 
The case contained only three pieces of information.
CLIENT: Derek Gent
SUBJECT: Murder
DATE: WEDNESDAY
TIME: 11:03:17
Derek clicked "Assigned Investigator."
The field was blank.
He clicked "Perpetrator."
Blank.
He clicked "Victim."
Derek Gent.
He clicked "Case Notes."
There was one note. Written in Derek's own handwriting.
DO NOT LET TREVOR NEAR THE STRANGE CLOCK.
Trevor read it. "I feel slightly insulted."
Derek faced him. "Don't."
"I wasn't going to."
"You were."
"I was."
Penny smiled. "This is becoming quite good."
Derek stared at the screen. "Something is using the Bureau."
"What?"
"Someone — or something — is creating cases inside our system."
"From where?"
"I don't know."
"Can we trace it?"
Derek checked. "No."
The cursor blinked on an empty field.
"Why?"
"Because the originating address is..."
He stopped. "What?"
Derek turned the monitor towards them. The address read:
LOCALHOST
Trevor's forehead creased. "That's the computer itself. So either someone here typed a murder into existence, or the system executed a transformation we have not written down yet. I prefer the second. People lie. Equations, when honest, merely disappoint."
"Or the machine filed a record that arrived without a person attached," Derek offered. "Localhost means the Bureau processed it. It does not mean one of us intended it."
"That's negotiable causality," Trevor said, and didn't much like how it sounded coming out of his own mouth. "Feelings aren't in the equations. If you want an argument, argue with the transform. Not with me."
"I'll argue with both," Penny declared. She tapped the doorway in the photo once, lightly, as if knocking. "She answers first."
Penny surveyed the room. There were three people in it. Derek.
Trevor. Penny. Nobody else.
She folded her arms. "Well."
Trevor met her gaze. "What?"
"You've got an interesting problem."
"Which one?"
"One of us is lying — or the filing cabinet is. Mrs Marsh keeps a form for Pending Geometry. I've never seen anyone use it."
Derek looked at the three chairs. Then the silent clock. Then the computer.
"Perhaps."
Penny smirked. "Or one of us hasn't done it yet."
Derek regarded her. The office suddenly felt colder. Not the ordinary London cold — something more precise, as if a draft had found its way in from a place that did not appear on any map. Trevor swallowed. "You mean..."
Derek didn't answer. The computer chimed again. All three studied the screen.
A new message suddenly appeared.
CASE UPDATE
PERPETRATOR IDENTIFIED
Derek opened it. The name appeared.
TREVOR BOLTZMAN
Trevor froze. "What?"
Somewhere down the corridor, the single working lightbulb buzzed on exactly as it always did, entirely unmoved by the fact that a computer had just accused someone of a murder that hadn't happened.
Trevor sat down without deciding to. "No — one thing at a time. Either I'm a suspect, or the case is wrong, or the frame is wrong. I need you to tell me which one I'm allowed to panic about first."
Derek gave him a measured look. 
Trevor and Penny exchanged a glance.
"That wasn't me."
"I know," Derek added.
"How do you know?"
"Because you're still here."
Trevor blinked. "That's your evidence?"
"For the moment."
Penny walked to the window. "Then who is framing him?"
Derek glanced at the clock. 9:31:17. 
He returned his attention to the photograph.
11:03:17. He returned to the case file. 
Then he looked at Trevor. "Maybe nobody. Maybe Case 1047 is not a confession. Maybe it is a misfiled event — and every seventeen on this desk is the same fingerprint wearing different gloves."
Trevor's brow creased. "What does that mean?"
Derek walked to the whiteboard. He wrote:
EVENT
Then:
OBSERVER
Then:
FRAME
Then:
TIME
He circled the first three.
"Before we worry about who did it, we need to know whether 'did it' is even the right verb. Event. Observer. Frame. Then time — if time is still the useful word."
Trevor dipped his head. "Right."
Penny regarded him carefully. "Do you understand?"
Trevor thought for a while. "I think so."
"Good."
Derek picked up his coat. The decision, once made, felt both inevitable and slightly unreal.
"Then we're going to need to leave."
"Where?"
Derek considered the photograph. "The clock."
Penny frowned. "The clock?"
"So, whoever put it here expected us to find it."
"What about it?"
"It doesn't belong here."
"So?"
Trevor stood. "All right, then, where are we going?"
Derek held up the photograph. "To the place where it came from."
Penny grabbed her coat. "And where is that?"
Derek turned the photograph over. On the back, beneath the number 1047, another line had suddenly appeared.
LONDON TERMINUS
PLATFORM 7
11:17
Penny read it. "That's tomorrow."
Derek nodded. "Yes."
Trevor surveyed the photograph. "And that's after you're dead."
"So it appears."
"So, how are we supposed to investigate it?"
Derek allowed himself a smile. "We'll have to be there."
Trevor looked concerned. "Tomorrow?"
"Yes."
"At eleven seventeen?"
"Yes."
"After you're murdered?"
"Presumably."
Trevor thought for a moment. "How do we know we'll get there?"
Derek opened the door. "Because someone has already told us that we will."
Penny followed him. Trevor remained where he was.
"Derek?"
"Yes?"
"What if the message is wrong?"
Derek turned back. "Then we'll have wasted a perfectly good morning."
"And if it's right?"
Derek grinned. "Then I suppose I'll have to solve my own murder. The words sounded almost reasonable until one considered them."
They stepped into the corridor. Mrs Marsh, the building superintendent, was already there, polishing a non-existent mark on the wall with the air of someone who had been waiting specifically to be inconvenient. She was a broad, grey-permed woman built along the lines of a filing cabinet that had decided to take up a hobby, and her voice, when it came, had the flat, unhurried carry of someone who had never once needed to raise it to be obeyed. She was widely regarded as the most immovable object in the building, and possibly in several adjacent dimensions. She did not look up. "You're tracking geometry in again," she observed.
Further down the corridor, Mr Pendleton from Facilities stood with a clipboard and the expression of a man who had already decided the answer was no. He was thin in the way of men who had spent decades standing rather than sitting, with a moustache trimmed to a precision his actual job never required, and a voice that always sounded slightly apologetic about the bad news it was about to deliver.
He specialised in a different kind of obstruction: the kind that required signatures, countersignatures, and the quiet insistence that whatever you needed was in a different section, a different building, or a different continuum. The heavy door closed behind them with the finality of a decision that had already been made somewhere else. Inside the empty office, the old brass clock appeared on Derek's desk as if it had been waiting for the room to be unoccupied.
Its second hand moved. One. Two.
Three. It reached seventeen. Stopped.
Then continued. But now the clock was showing 11:03. And outside the empty office, somewhere far away, a distant train whistle sounded.
There was no empty railway station anywhere near the building. The whistle sounded again. Then a third time.
The Bureau's computer switched itself on. 
A final message appeared.
CASE 1047
STATUS: ACTIVE
VICTIM: DEREK GENT
PERPETRATOR: TREVOR BOLTZMAN
WITNESS: PENNY
PHYSICIST: ALFRED WEINSTEIN
REFERENCE FRAME: UNKNOWN
And beneath it, in smaller letters:
DO NOT LET WEINSTEIN GET TO THE EMPTY STATION FIRST
The message disappeared. The screen went black. 
Then the computer displayed the date.
Wednesday. 11:03. 17 seconds.
The quiet office was empty. For the moment.

Rule: If the case file updates itself, you are not reading news. You are reading geometry.
-> Lesson for this chapter: 2 - Lorentz Finally Gets Involved
Interlude
Sophie
Sophie was small for twelve and treated this as a technicality rather than a fact, the way she treated most inconvenient data. She wore her dark hair scraped back hard enough to argue with, on the theory that anything loose was a distraction, and she had inherited her father's habit of going very still when she was thinking, which teachers mistook for daydreaming and Derek would have recognised instantly, had he been in the room. Sophie had noticed the clocks were lying. Not all of them, and not in ways that made sense. The brass one in Dad's study — the one he kept pretending he hadn't bought — was always exactly four minutes ahead of her phone. She'd tested it seventeen times. Seventeen times: four minutes, never more, never less. That wasn't a clock that was broken. That was a clock that was disagreeing on purpose. She'd started a notebook. Not because she wanted to solve anything. But because if the clocks were having an argument, someone should keep score.
She asked questions the way she asked for the salt: plainly, expecting an answer, mildly surprised when adults treated the asking as a performance.
Chapter 3
The Lorentz Transform
By Wednesday morning, Derek Gent had decided that there were two kinds of impossible. The first kind was the ordinary sort. A man could not be in two places at once.
A clear photograph could not normally exist before it was taken. A clock could not run backwards without either being broken or being subjected to a level of engineering that Derek considered unnecessarily ambitious. The second kind was considerably worse.
The second kind was something that was perfectly consistent with the laws of physics but completely inconsistent with common sense. Derek had spent most of his professional life dealing with the second kind. He found it much more expensive.
At 8:36 on Wednesday morning, he was standing in front of the whiteboard in the Bureau while Trevor sat at the old desk and Penny leaned against the window. The brass railway clock sat between them. It had arrived sometime during the night.
Nobody had seen it arrive. For a moment the air above the desk held a shimmer like heat over rails — then ordinary London reasserted itself, embarrassed.
Nobody knew where it had come from. 
And it was still showing 11:03:17.
Derek had written three words on the board.
EVENT
FRAME
TIME
He underlined the first.
"Stop explaining," Penny instructed. "Establish what happened — and do not call it a murder until the geometry confesses."
Trevor studied the photograph. "You mean the murder?"
"I mean the event. Murder is a noun we invented after we assumed a shared now. If that assumption is wrong, the noun is evidence of our superstition — not of a killer."
"Isn't that the same thing?"
"No."
Trevor considered this. "Not necessarily. You may be putting the cart before the flaming horse."
"Precisely."
Penny regarded the silent clock. "We have a photograph of you lying dead."
"Yes."
"We have a timestamp."
"Apparently."
"We have a second timestamp in the Bureau," Penny stated.
"Yes."
"And the two don't agree."
"Not yet."
Trevor grimaced. "Not yet?"
Derek faced him. "That's an important distinction."
"Why?"
"Because we don't know whether they are supposed to agree."
Trevor examined the clock. "They're both clocks."
"Yes."
"So, they measure time."
"Yes."
"And time is time," Trevor ventured — then winced. "No. That sentence is a superstition. I withdraw it before the whiteboard hears."
Penny smiled. Derek eyed Trevor. 
Trevor immediately raised both hands. "I know."
"You've just contradicted Special Relativity."
"I know. I withdraw it."
Derek returned to the board. "Let's start from the beginning."
He drew two points. "Two events. Which one are we dating — the murder, the photo, or the moment the Bureau decided they were the same Tuesday?"
He labelled one event A and the other B. "Four numbers each if you insist. I care about one question: can A and B be the same event under two honest clocks?"
He wrote:
 (x, y, z, t)
Trevor conceded. "Position and time — labels. Not gospel."
"Exactly."
"And different observers can assign different coordinates."
"Yes."
"Because the observers move," Derek said. "Or because we assumed they share a now they do not have."
"Yes."
Penny considered the photograph the way she used to consider unfinished sentences. "If she is inside the event, we are already late. If she is outside it, we can still choose the door."
"No."
"It's when Derek died according to which observer."
"I think so." Penny beamed. "Good. You've missed all my mystery lectures, and now one has finally landed."
Trevor glanced at Derek. "But the death itself either happened or it didn't."
"Of course."
"That part doesn't depend on the observer."
"Correct."
Trevor tipped his head. "Right."
Derek grinned. "You've just rescued metaphysics from physics."
"I do my best."
Penny picked up the photograph. "And this image?"
"Don't lecture light travel," Derek pressed. "Test it. If the stamp and the body disagree under every frame we try, the photo is not a camera — it is a filing."
Trevor was already measuring. "Light delay cannot buy two hours unless the camera is leaving the solar system in a huff."
"Then light travel is not the alibi," Penny concluded. "Good. One failed explanation. Next."
Trevor fell into thought. "So when we say the photograph was taken at 11:03, we are already smuggling in a relationship between clock and event that we have not earned."
Derek stared at him. "Yes."
Trevor smiled. "That one was easy."
"Don't get used to it."
Penny laughed. 
Derek walked to the old desk and picked up the ancient brass clock. He turned it over.
There was a small metal plate on the back. No manufacturer's name. No serial number.
Just three letters.
Trevor gazed at it.
"Any idea what that means?"
Derek shook his head.
"Not yet."
Penny took the silent clock. "May I?"
Derek handed it to her.
She examined it carefully. "This isn't a normal railway clock."
"I know."
"The mechanism is unusually precise."
"How precise?" asked Trevor.
"More precise than it has any right to be."
Trevor leaned in. "How do you know?"
Penny smirked. "Because I opened it."
Derek consulted the clock. "When?"
"Last night."
"You opened it?"
"Yes."
"Without telling me?"
"You were asleep."
"I was working."
"You were asleep at your desk."
"That was a temporary research posture."
Penny ignored him. "There are no batteries."
Derek frowned. "Mechanical?"
"Not exactly," she replied, handing the silent clock back. "It has a power source."
"What sort?" asked Derek.
"I don't know."
Trevor took it. He examined the back. "That's odd."
"What?"
"There isn't enough room."
"For what?"
"For a power source," clarified Trevor.
Derek took the clock from him. Trevor was right. The casing was too thin.
There was nowhere to put anything substantial. Derek turned it over again. The second hand moved.
The second hand swept forward. Once. Twice.
Seventeen sweeps for seventeen seconds. Which would have been unremarkable, if the clock made any sound at all.
"Why seventeen?"
Derek turned his attention to him. "I don't know."
"It's on the photograph too."
"Yes."
"And the Bureau clock."
"Yes."
"And yesterday's footage."
Derek inclined his head. "Yes."
Trevor thought. "So, seventeen is part of the message."
"Probably."
"Could it be a date? A reference to a page? An equation?" Trevor ran through the possibilities without much hope.
Derek shook his head. "None of them, probably. But I don't actually know yet."
Penny declared, "Or a velocity."
Derek surveyed her. "Explain."
"Seventeen kilometres per second. 
Seventeen percent of something. 
Seventeen metres per second. 
There's no shortage of seventeen in physics."
"True."
Trevor studied the clock again. "Could be 17 seconds of proper time."
Derek stopped. Penny regarded him.
Trevor realised he had announced something interesting, "That would make the difference meaningful."
"Yes."
Derek took the photograph. "If seventeen seconds is a proper-time interval..."
"Then we'd need to know which worldline we're talking about," Penny asserted.
Trevor agreed. "And the gravitational potential."
"Exactly."
Derek examined both of them. "Which means we're already getting ahead of ourselves."
Penny grinned. "That's what we do."
Derek put the photograph down. "Not yet."
He turned to the whiteboard. "Simplest trap first. If Special Relativity can make the photo and our clocks into one event in two frames, we keep hunting a murderer. If it can't — "
"Then the premise dies," Trevor announced. "Good. Premises should die in public."
"Yes."
Derek wrote:
x' = γ (x — vt)
t' = γ (t — vx/c²)
"Don't narrate the symbols," Penny urged. "Plug the numbers in and see what breaks."
x = γ (x' + v t')
t = γ (t' + v x' / c²)
He stared at the symmetry for a moment. "Right. Experiment one."
Trevor inspected the equations. "The Lorentz transformation."
"Yes."
Penny confirmed. "Weinstein."
"Yes."
Trevor met Derek's gaze. "Do you ever find it strange saying that?"
"What — yes? Only when I actually can't be arsed to say 'yes' properly."
"No, I mean, that you're using Weinstein's equations to investigate a case involving Weinstein's own theory."
Derek thought about it. "Only if the equations get it wrong. Then we're not investigating with his theory. We're investigating *against* it."
"Why not?"
"Because the universe may be strange, but it remains investigable. If the photo and our clocks cannot be one event in two inertial frames, the Lorentz transform fails as an alibi machine — and that failure is evidence."
"Fair."
"If gravity is rearranging the clocks, we need General Relativity. If neither works, we're not investigating a crime. We're investigating a filing. And someone filed Sophie inside it."
"And if it involves both?"
"Then you start charging by the hour."
Penny laughed. 
Trevor already had the input file open. "Assume one event. Assume flat spacetime. Assume the two-hour gap is a frame disagreement."
Derek allowed. "Run it."
"I am running it," Trevor protested. "You are hovering. Hovering is not mathematics."
Penny set the photograph flat on the desk and put her palm over Sophie's face. Not to hide her. To keep her from becoming a number we could solve. "She is not a variable."
"I'm not ignoring her," Trevor snapped. "I'm refusing to let her become an excuse to stop calculating."
Derek held up a hand. "Both of you shut up until the number comes back."
Trevor studied the screen. The cursor blinked like a guilty conscience.
"Well?" Penny asked.
"The transform wants a relative velocity," Trevor reported. "It is being extremely sincere about it."
"And?"
Derek tapped the photograph. "And that is the first thing I want to test: whether eleven-oh-three is a time of day or an event label wearing a clock's face."
He opened the Bureau's analysis software without giving a seminar. "Assume the stamp is genuine. Assume one event. Assume relative motion. Ask the machine what velocity makes the clocks agree."
Trevor leaned forward. "All right."
"Suppose the photograph corresponds to an actual event tomorrow at 11:03:17."
"Okay."
"And suppose the camera and the Bureau are moving relative to one another."
Penny nodded. "If that works, Sophie is standing in a frame disagreement. If it doesn't, she's standing in something worse."
"Yes."
Trevor clarified, "But unless they're moving at a substantial fraction of the speed of light, the difference should be tiny."
Derek's mouth twitched. "Exactly."
"How tiny?"
"Very."
"How tiny?"
"Small enough that London Underground timetables are generally a greater source of temporal uncertainty."
"Obviously," agreed Penny, smiling. "So, ordinary motion can't explain a difference of nearly two hours."
"No."
Derek entered the relevant numbers. 
The computer calculated. He studied the result.
Then he entered them again. Trevor watched. "What did you get?"
"Nothing."
"Nothing?"
"Exactly."
Derek deleted the calculation.
Penny came over. "What did you expect?"
"A result."
"Meaning?"
"A physically meaningful relative velocity."
"And you didn't get one?"
"I got one."
"Then what's wrong?"
Derek didn't answer immediately. He looked at the number a second time, as if it might have the decency to change.
Derek tapped the number. "It's impossible. And that means the premise is the suspect—not the universe."
"How much greater?"
"Enough."
Trevor leaned closer. "That's not a velocity."
"Correct."
Penny's jaw tightened. "So Special Relativity just failed the only test we gave it."
"Or we failed the premise," Derek countered. "Wrong frame. Wrong event. Wrong clocks. Wrong photograph. The algebra didn't lecture us. It laughed."
"Or the event."
"Or the clocks."
"Or the photograph."
"Yes."
Trevor slowly gazed at the equation. "Could the photograph be showing a different event?"
Derek pivoted, puzzled. "Explain."
"The photograph may be real, but we assume it shows Derek's death."
"Yes."
"What if it shows Derek at a different event that happens to look like his death?"
Derek considered this.
Penny smirked. "That would be rather inconvenient for Derek."
"Indeed."
Trevor continued. "Maybe the blood isn't blood. Maybe the body isn't Derek. Maybe he's unconscious, or the photograph's been staged."
Derek peered at him. "All possible."
Trevor brightened. "So, we don't actually know that the photograph is evidence of murder."
Derek conceded. "Why, right. I'd actually prefer that."
Trevor sat back. "Then why did you say you'd been murdered?"
Derek eyed him. "Because the Bureau told me."
Trevor paused. "Right."
Penny mentioned, "We're going to need to question the Bureau."
Derek admitted, "I was afraid of that."
Trevor had gone quiet in the specific way that meant the maths had already left the room ahead of him.
The mug on the windowsill was empty again. No one had drunk from it. Meeting minutes for a briefing that had not been scheduled appeared in the tray, timestamped seventeen seconds past eleven.
Derek stared at the minutes. "Evidence from a meeting that hasn't happened. That's not a joke. That's the Bureau filing the future again."
Trevor snatched the page. "If the timestamp is consistent with the seventeen-second motif, I'm keeping it. Experience can catch up later."
Penny took the phone out of his hands. On the lock screen was yesterday's school photo — Sophie mid-eye-roll, bag strap twisted. "Motifs don't have bag straps. Call her again tonight. Ordinary. Boring. Do it."
"I'm not — " Trevor began.
"You are," Derek admitted quietly. "And I am the one who has to decide whether chasing this geometry keeps her out of that doorway or writes her into it harder."
Mrs Marsh, passing the open door, glanced at the unauthorised minutes and allowed herself, uncharacteristically, half a second of visible concern before her professional face reasserted itself.
Mr Pendleton appeared long enough to declare that future paperwork was still not his form, then vanished with the dignity of a man fleeing a causal loop.
Outside, a red Mini Cooper contained more people than its volume should allow. Derek counted seven heads and one surplus hand. London, too, was failing conservation laws in public.
Mrs Marsh walked past without breaking stride. "If that is a singularity," she noted, "it is not registered to this address. Pending Geometry is drawer three. Murder is drawer one. Do try not to confuse them again."
Derek leaned down for a closer look. The extra hand, on inspection, belonged to a passenger who had apparently brought a second, somewhat smaller passenger of his own. Nobody in the car seemed remotely troubled by this. Trevor found that considerably more alarming than the singularity.
Since the paperwork had changed, certain older forms still referred to arrangements that no longer applied. These were kept in a drawer labelled Pre-Transition / Do Not Open. Derek had opened it once. The forms inside had the air of documents that knew they were out of date and intended to remain that way. The universe, he had noticed, had become slightly harder to interpret since the annexes stopped matching. He was becoming disturbingly skilled at scheduling problems for other days. It was one of the few forms of time travel the Bureau officially permitted. The unofficial ones were more interesting and strictly forbidden.
The telephone rang. All three watched it.
Derek answered. "Derek Gent."
A pause. Then a man's voice — dry, unhurried, with the faint Bavarian clip of someone who had learned his English from textbooks and refused, on principle, to soften it for anyone's comfort. "Good morning."
Herbert Matkowski had once been Alfred Weinstein's mathematics professor, a fact he mentioned rarely and clearly savoured. He held the private, frequently reconfirmed opinion that his former student was very, very smart and very, very lazy, and that grading his coursework decades earlier had been the first reliable evidence for both. Where Weinstein worked in leaps, Matkowski worked in steps, and considered this — even now — the more honest method. His own handwriting was neat enough that Trevor had once photographed a page of it purely out of professional jealousy.
Derek knew the voice. "Herbert."
"Yes," Matkowski said. "In Munich, since you'll ask."
Derek surveyed Penny. "Of course."
Trevor mouthed, "Who?"
Derek waved him quiet. "What do you want?"
"I understand you have been using the Lorentz transformation."
"Yes."
"And?"
"It doesn't work."
There was silence.
Then Matkowski responded:
"It works perfectly."
"Not on this problem."
"Then you're using it incorrectly."
Derek's expression tightened. "Are you telling me I don't understand Special Relativity?"
"I'm telling you that you are trying to make a flat-spacetime transformation solve a problem that may not be flat."
Derek met Penny's gaze. She immediately understood. Trevor did too.
"Gravity," Derek opined.
"Perhaps."
"We don't have evidence of gravity."
"You have clocks."
"We always have clocks."
"Not clocks behaving like yours."
Derek regarded the brass railway clock. "Where are you?"
"Munich."
"Why?"
"Because Alfred is here."
Derek paused. "Alfred Weinstein?"
The brass clock hummed faintly. "Yes."
"Doing what?"
"Being bored."
"Where?"
"In a patent attorney's office in Schwabing."
Derek examined Penny. Penny raised an eyebrow. "That's very Weinstein."
Matkowski continued, "He has been thinking about General Relativity."
"At the moment?"
"Yes."
Derek inspected the silent clock. "How convenient."
"It is not convenient."
"Why?"
"Because Alfred has just discovered that the clocks in his office are disagreeing."
Derek's expression changed.
"By how much?"
Matkowski gave him the figure. Derek stopped moving entirely. Penny noticed. "Same?"
Derek confirmed. "Same."
Trevor stood. "Same what?"
Derek covered the receiver. "Time difference."
Trevor's brow creased. "Between Munich and London?"
"No."
"Then what?"
Derek considered the clock. "The same anomaly."
Penny took the phone. "Matkowski."
"Yes."
"How accurate are Weinstein's clocks?"
"Extremely."
"Could they be synchronised?"
"They were."
"Could the difference be gravitational?"
"Not at this magnitude."
Penny glanced at Derek. "What about acceleration?"
Matkowski answered: "That is precisely the question."
Derek took the phone back. "Are you saying the clocks in Munich and London are showing the same relativistic anomaly?"
"I'm saying that if you transform the measurements into a common frame, they are related."
Derek thought. "How?"
"I don't know yet."
"Then why the call?"
"Because Alfred wants to speak to you."
Derek wearily sighed. "Put him on."
There was a click. Then a different voice. Older. Calmer. Slightly irritated.
"Derek Gent?"
"Yes."
"This is Alfred Weinstein."
"I know."
"Do we know each other?"
"Not personally."
"Hmm." A pause.
Weinstein replied, "You're the detective."
"Yes."
"The relativistic detective."
"Yes."
"I've been told you have a clock."
Derek stared at it. "I do."
"Where did you get it?"
"I don't know."
"That's unhelpful." "Then we should stop asking where and start asking *when*," Derek said. Weinstein paused. "Now you're interesting."
"I agree."
Weinstein asked, "Does it have a brass case?"
"Yes."
"Old railway style?"
"Yes."
"Does it stop at seventeen seconds?"
"Yes."
There was a pause. "Then you have a problem."
Derek smiled. "We had already established that."
"No. You have a very specific problem."
"What is it?"
Weinstein took a breath. "I've seen that clock. Tomorrow. And you were there. Alive. Dead. Simultaneously."
Derek's expression changed.
"Where?"
"In 2026."
Derek turned to Penny. Neither of them spoke. Trevor had gone very still.
Derek maintained: "We're in 2026."
"I know."
"Then what do you mean?"
"I mean I saw it earlier."
"When?"
Weinstein paused. "Tomorrow."
Nobody spoke. Trevor's tea went cold in his hand and he didn't notice, which was itself the more alarming data point. Derek slowly sat down.
"Yes?"
"Are you saying you have already seen the clock?"
"Yes."
"Where?"
"In London."
Penny met Derek's gaze, one eyebrow already halfway towards the ceiling. "Here?"
"I believe so."
Derek took the phone back. "Alfred, are you sure?"
"I am old enough to know my own memory, Mr Gent, but I am not senile."
"I didn't mean — "
"I know what you meant."
A pause. Then Weinstein affirmed:
"The interesting thing is that I remember seeing it."
"That's reassuring."
"No."
"Why not?"
"Because the Bureau told me."
Derek closed his eyes and, for a moment, let the phone hum quietly against his ear.
Trevor whispered: "That doesn't help."
Derek covered the receiver. "No."
He uncovered it. "Alfred, what exactly happened?"
"I was standing in the Bureau."
"When?"
"Tomorrow."
"What were you doing?" asked Derek.
"Looking at the clock."
"Anything else?"
"Yes!" Weinstein confirmed.
"What?"
Derek's grip tightened on the phone. Whatever came next, he already knew it wasn't going to be small.
"You were dead."
Derek watched Penny. Penny took a slow breath. Trevor looked as though he would quite like to leave the country.
Derek pressed: "Did you see who killed me?"
"No."
"Why not?"
"Because you were standing up."
Derek blinked. "What?"
Weinstein continued, "You were dead."
"Yes."
"But you were standing."
Derek surveyed Penny. Penny stared back.
Trevor insisted: "Alive and dead are not adjectives that transform. If the maths appears to say otherwise, the maths is describing two events and you are smuggling in one 'now' like a superstition. I will not baptise superstition as physics."
Derek covered the phone. "Thank you, Trevor."
"Sorry."
Derek uncovered it. "Alfred, are you sure?"
"Quite."
Derek continued. "Could it have been a relativistic effect?"
"Possibly."
"Could the photograph have been from another frame?"
"Perhaps."
"Could the event be displaced in time?"
"Possibly."
Derek tried once more. "Could I have been dead in one frame and alive in another?" He hated the sentence. It sounded like giving up on investigability.
There was a long silence.
Then Weinstein answered emphatically. "No."
Derek waited. "Why not?"
"Because that's nonsense," Weinstein declared. "I deal in geometry. You deal in arrests. Do not confuse my tools with your nouns."
Derek inclined his head. "Good."
"However..."
Derek closed his eyes.
Another voice injected, "Being bored."
Derek paused. "That sounds like Alfred."
Matkowski agreed, "He's a well-boiled icicle when he gets interested."
"You could be dead according to one description of an event and alive according to another if we're actually talking about different events."
"That's what I thought."
"Which means the photograph may be telling you the truth."
"Even if I'm not dead."
"Yes."
Derek considered that. "That's rather annoying."
"I agree."
Matkowski's voice came faintly from the background.
"Ask him about the train."
Derek heard him.
"What train?"
Weinstein said, "Ah."
"What?"
"There is a train."
"What train?"
"The one at the station."
"What station?"
"London Terminus."
Derek froze. "Platform seven?"
Weinstein was silent.
"Alfred?"
"Yes."
"Platform seven?"
"Yes."
"At 11:17?"
"Yes."
Derek studied Penny. Penny's expression had gone completely serious. Trevor was standing beside the whiteboard.
Derek said:
"We have a photograph."
"I know."
"How do you know?"
"Because I was there."
"When?"
"Tomorrow."
Derek sat back. "All right."
Weinstein remained silent. 
Derek asked: "Who else was there?"
Weinstein hesitated. "You."
"Alive?"
"Yes."
"Dead?"
"Also yes." Weinstein paused. "And a child was in the doorway. I assumed she was part of the Bureau. She did not look like staff."
Derek's hand tightened on the phone. Sophie. Tomorrow. Watching.
Trevor stared at Derek. 
Penny regarded the ceiling.
Nobody spoke.
Finally Derek stated:
"Alfred."
"Yes?"
"I think we have a problem."
Weinstein sighed. "I was hoping you'd say that."
The line went dead. 
Derek lowered the phone. 
Trevor spoke first. "How can you be alive and dead at the same time?"
Derek examined him. "I don't know."
Penny commented:
"That's not strictly what Weinstein noted."
Trevor studied her. "What did he say?"
"He observed Derek was alive and dead."
"That's the same thing."
"No."
"How?"
Penny wiped the whiteboard clean of Greek letters and wrote one word: SOPHIE. "Trevor gets the algebra. I get her Tuesday. If those disagree, we do not average them."
She erased the word TIME. 
Then wrote:
EVENTS
"He's seeing two descriptions."
Trevor considered. "Two frames?"
"Possibly."
Derek returned to the equations. "Not if the transformation requires a velocity greater than light."
Trevor wanted to arrest the number. Penny wanted to arrest the assumption. Derek wanted tea, and then the assumption. "v > c is not a confession," he said. "It is the universe declining to countersign our form."
Trevor thought. "Then perhaps the events are not connected the way we assume."
"Exactly."
Penny spun to face them. "So, we're back to the beginning."
"No."
Derek picked up the photograph. "We're further along."
"Then you have a problem."
"We know the train exists."
"We knew that from the photograph."
"We now know Weinstein saw it."
"And?"
"He saw it tomorrow."
Trevor frowned. "That doesn't narrow it down."
Something eased in Derek's face. "It does."
"How?"
"Because Alfred Weinstein doesn't make mistakes with relativity."
Trevor granted. "True."
"He might misunderstand an unfamiliar physical phenomenon."
"Yes."
"But he won't misunderstand Special Relativity."
"Agreed."
Derek indicated the silent clock. "So if Weinstein reads the clock is showing us two different events rather than one event seen from two frames..."
Penny finished: "Then we should believe him."
Derek agreed. "Yes."
Trevor glanced at the whiteboard. "And then?"
"Then we find the second event."
"How?"
"By following the clock."
Penny smirked. "That sounds simple."
"It won't be."
"Why?"
"Because the clock isn't telling us where to go."
Derek turned it over. The letters LGV were still stamped into the back. Trevor looked over his shoulder. "What does LGV mean?"
"I don't know."
Penny replied:
"Could be someone's initials."
"Could be."
"A model number."
"Possibly."
"A railway designation."
"Maybe."
"A lorry licence," Mrs Marsh said, not even pausing on her way past the door. "LGV. Large Goods Vehicle. My nephew has one. Drives a very ordinary lorry. Nothing gravitational about it." She left before anyone could ask why she knew that, or why the mysterious clock would need a heavy goods licence, which was, on reflection, no stranger a question than most of the others that week.
Trevor gazed at the silent clock face.
"Could it mean Local Gravitational Vector?"
Derek peered at him. Penny eyed him. Trevor shrugged. "What?"
Derek grinned. "That's actually rather good."
Trevor smiled, pleased. "Thank you."
Derek took out his phone. He searched the Bureau's records. Nothing.
He searched railway archives. Nothing. He searched scientific databases.
Nothing. Then the Bureau's computer chimed. A new case file had appeared.
Derek opened it.
CASE 1047
He stopped, mid-motion. The file was different now.
CLIENT: DEREK GENT
VICTIM: DEREK GENT
PERPETRATOR: TREVOR BOLTZMAN
WITNESS: PENNY
PHYSICIST: ALFRED WEINSTEIN
LOCATION: LONDON TERMINUS
PLATFORM: 7
REFERENCE FRAME: INERTIAL
Derek tapped the last line. Trevor leaned over. "Inertial."
Penny confirmed. "That's important."
"Why?"
"Because the Bureau is telling us which frame to use."
Derek allowed. "And it has chosen an inertial frame."
Trevor watched the photograph. "Which means Special Relativity."
"Just so."
Penny allowed herself a smile. "So, perhaps General Relativity isn't involved after all."
Derek surveyed her. "Perhaps."
The computer chimed again. A final line appeared.
GRAVITY: REQUIRED
Derek went very still. Penny's breath caught. Trevor read it twice, just to be sure.
Then Trevor answered: "That's rather rude."
Derek laughed. For the first time since receiving the murder notice, he felt genuinely cheerful. Someone — or something — was giving them instructions.
The instructions were cryptic. The physics was inconsistent. The future appeared to have leaked into the present.
And Alfred Weinstein was apparently going to witness Derek's death tomorrow while Derek remained alive today. In other words, it was exactly the sort of case for which the Bureau existed. Derek put on his coat. "Platform seven."
Penny picked up her handbag. "At eleven seventeen."
Trevor grabbed the photograph. "Tomorrow."
Derek nodded. "Tomorrow."
Trevor studied the ancient brass clock. "What about this?"
Derek picked it up. "We're taking it."
Penny regarded him. "Why?"
"Because it clearly wants to go."
Trevor grimaced. "Clocks don't want things."
Derek opened the old door. "Normally, no."
He examined the clock. "This one appears to be an exception."
They stepped into the narrow corridor. Derek locked the familiar office. Then stopped.
He looked back through the glass. For just a moment, he thought he saw somebody sitting at his desk. A man.
Old. Grey-haired. Wearing a dark suit.
Derek blinked. The chair was empty. He unlocked the door again.
Trevor watched him. "What?"
"Nothing. Trick of the light."
Trevor gestured at the brass clock. "And that?"
Derek grimaced. "Comes with us."
From somewhere that was not the corridor, a train whistle answered — once.
Derek froze. "Did you hear — "
"Platform language," Penny said. "I heard it too."
"Eleven seventeen," Trevor muttered, against his will.
Derek exhaled. "Right. The ship has left the barn."
The brass clock was in his hand. He considered the chair again. Nothing.
Penny mysteriously appeared in the doorway. "Coming?"
"Yes."
Derek switched off the light. As the familiar door closed, the computer screen came on by itself. One sentence appeared.
THE LORENTZ TRANSFORMATION IS CORRECT
Then another.
YOUR INTERPRETATION IS NOT
And beneath it:
NEXT: FIND THE STRANGE TRAIN
The screen went dark. In the narrow corridor, Derek stopped. Penny glanced at him. "What now?"
Derek smiled. "Now we catch a train."
Trevor's brow furrowed. "We're not going anywhere."
"Not yet."
Derek studied the ancient brass clock. "That's the point."
And somewhere, very far away — or possibly very close — the sound of a distant train began to move through the walls.

Rule: If the algebra demands v > c, the premise is the suspect — not the universe.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 3 - Time May Be Less Fundamental
Chapter 4
The Weight of Time
By Wednesday morning, Alfred Weinstein was sitting in a patent attorney's office in Schwabing. He was bored. This was not unusual.
He was in his early fifties, compact and faintly stooped from decades spent leaning over desks that were never quite the right height. His hair had gone entirely grey-white, and he had long since stopped attempting to discipline it. He wore the same jacket regardless of the Munich weather, on the stated grounds that changing it more than once a season introduced an unnecessary variable.
His voice was unhurried and faintly accented, with a habit of dropping towards a whisper exactly when a problem genuinely interested him — the reverse of everyone else in the room, who got louder under pressure.
He had once observed, forcefully and at some length, that a certain other physicist had also spent his most productive years examining patents in an office very much like this one, and that history had found this charming rather than suspicious. Nobody at the Bureau had found a tactful way to change the subject fast enough.
The patent attorney was explaining a device intended to improve the efficiency of a mechanical regulator. Weinstein had been listening for approximately six minutes. He had understood the first two.
The third had been optimistic. The fourth had involved a spring. The fifth had involved a spring behaving in a way Weinstein considered personally insulting.
By the sixth minute, his mind had left the room, drifting towards the cold window. Munich was busy.
A tram rattled past. A cyclist narrowly avoided a delivery cart.
Someone shouted at someone else.
A dog had apparently decided that the entire afternoon belonged to it. Weinstein watched the movement for a while. Then his attention drifted to the silent clock.
He had been in the quiet office for forty-three minutes. It felt like three hours. He allowed himself a small smile.
That was an interesting thought. The feeling of time passing was obviously psychological. Physical time was another matter.
He checked the clock again. Then his watch. They disagreed.
Only slightly. Weinstein frowned. He checked them again.
The difference was still there. He looked around the office. One mysterious clock was on the wall.
His watch was on his wrist. Nothing unusual about that. He returned to the patent.
The attorney was still speaking. Weinstein lasted another twelve seconds. Then his mind wandered back to the clocks.
Weinstein set one watch on the windowsill and held the other at chest height. He waited without theorising. When he compared them, the higher watch had won by a nothing — a nothing too small for Case 1047, and yet the room felt briefly larger, as if a polite universe had admitted it was not entirely flat.
General Relativity predicted that a strange clock deeper in a gravitational field would run more slowly than one higher up. The difference under ordinary circumstances was extremely small. But it was real.
And, more importantly, it was measurable. Weinstein picked up his pencil. On the margin of the patent application he wrote:
GRAVITY CHANGES THE RATE OF TIME
He reread the sentence.
Then added:
THE DIFFERENCE SHOULD BE MEASURABLE
He read it back twice, already elsewhere. The patent attorney stopped speaking. "Herr Weinstein?"
"Yes?"
"Are you following me?"
"Absolutely."
"What did I just say?"
Weinstein's attention drifted from the patent to the attorney to his own pencil.
"You were explaining the regulator."
"Yes."
"And the spring."
"Yes."
"The spring is wrong."
"I beg your pardon?"
Weinstein watched the diagram. "It won't behave as you've described."
"Why not?"
Weinstein surveyed the drawing for a moment. "Because you've assumed the temperature remains constant."
The attorney blinked. "Doesn't it?"
"No."
"Oh."
Weinstein turned the paper around and indicated a line. "This coefficient changes with temperature."
The attorney leaned over the desk. "Good heavens."
Weinstein shrugged. "Well, there we are."
He returned to his mysterious clock. The patent attorney studied the amended diagram, then Weinstein himself.
"You've been listening after all."
"Occasionally."
The attorney permitted himself a small, professional smile. "I suppose I should be grateful."
"You should."
The spring would continue being wrong while he was gone. Gravity would continue being geometry. Only one of these facts was going to London.
Weinstein regarded his watch again. The difference between it and the wall clock had increased. His brow furrowed.
That was no longer ordinary. He stood. "Do you have another clock?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
"I'd like to compare them."
The attorney gestured towards the secretary's desk. "There is one there."
Weinstein walked over. He compared it with his watch. Then with the wall clock.
Then he examined the positions of all three. His expression changed. The patent attorney noticed.
"Something wrong?"
"Yes."
"What?"
"The clocks are disagreeing."
"They do that."
"Not like this."
The attorney appraised him. Weinstein took out his notebook. He calculated.
Then calculated again. The difference was far too large. He threw up his hands at the ceiling.
Then at the floor. The secretary came in.
"Herr Weinstein?"
"Yes?"
"Is everything all right?"
"No."
"What happened?"
Weinstein held up the notebook. "The clocks are behaving badly."
The secretary glanced at the clocks. "They seem fine."
"That's what makes it interesting."
He put the notebook away. "How high is this building?"
"Four floors."
"Exactly?"
"I believe so."
Weinstein inclined his head. He looked up. "Which floor are we on?"
"Second."
He thought. Then stared at his watch again. The difference had increased.
He grimaced. "That's impossible."
The secretary grinned. "I thought you didn't believe in impossible things."
"I don't."
"Then what is it?"
Weinstein considered the question. "A very large error."
The secretary dipped her head. "Ah."
She returned to her desk. Weinstein picked up his coat. The patent attorney straightened. "Are you leaving?"
"Yes."
"What about the rest of the patent?"
"It can wait."
"The spring?"
"It will continue being wrong while I'm gone."
He left the quiet office. Herbert Matkowski was standing in the empty corridor. Weinstein stopped. "You knew."
Matkowski took him in.
"Knew what?"
"The clocks."
"What about them?"
"They're disagreeing."
Matkowski confirmed. "Yes."
"By a ridiculous amount."
"Yes."
"And you knew."
"Yes."
"Well?"
"Well what?"
"What is happening?"
Matkowski's expression warmed slightly. "That is what Derek Gent is trying to discover."
Weinstein's forehead creased. "Who?"
"Derek Gent."
"Never heard of him."
"You will."
Matkowski handed him a vivid photograph. 
Weinstein studied it. The picture showed an office.
A man was lying face-down on the floor. 
"Who is that?"
"Derek Gent."
"Dead?"
"Apparently."
Weinstein peered at the vivid photograph again.
"When?"
"Tomorrow."
Weinstein faced Matkowski. "That's inconvenient."
"I thought you'd say that."
Weinstein turned the photograph towards the light. Then he noticed the clock. 11:03:17.
His expression changed. "Where did you get this?"
"From the Bureau."
"What Bureau?"
"Derek Gent's Relativistic Investigation Bureau."
Weinstein watched the photograph. 
Then at Matkowski. "And you expect me to believe this?"
"No."
"Good."
"I expect you to analyse it."
Weinstein granted. "That is more reasonable."
"What frame?"
Matkowski smirked. "Exactly."
Weinstein faced him. "Don't be pleased with yourself."
"I wasn't."
"You were."
"Perhaps."
Weinstein returned to the vivid photograph. "The timestamp alone tells us almost nothing."
"Correct."
"We need the event coordinates."
Matkowski checked his own notes. "Correct."
"And the observer's frame."
"Correct."
"And the light-travel time between the event and the camera."
"Correct."
Weinstein allowed. "Well, that's straightforward."
Matkowski smirked. "Is it?"
"No."
"Fine."
Weinstein looked again. There was something else. An old brass clock.
The same old clock he'd seen in his office. He indicated it. "That."
"Yes."
"I've seen that clock."
"Where?"
"In London."
Matkowski was silent. 
Weinstein regarded him. "Yesterday."
Matkowski agreed. "Yes."
Weinstein fell silent for a moment. "Why didn't you tell me?"
"I wanted you to notice."
Weinstein sighed. "You have an unfortunate tendency to turn information into puzzles."
"It keeps the mind active."
"It also wastes time."
"Not according to relativity."
Weinstein examined him. "That was terrible."
"I know."
Weinstein handed the photograph back.
"Let's go to London."
Matkowski raised an eyebrow. "Now?"
"Yes."
"You're not curious about the patent?"
Weinstein looked towards the office. "No."
"The spring?"
"No."
"The regulator?"
"No."
Matkowski conceded. "Good."
They walked towards the stairs.
After a moment, Weinstein added: "There's something else."
"What?"
"The difference between the clocks."
"Yes?"
"It's much too large to be explained by the gravitational field of the Earth at that height."
Matkowski nodded. "Exactly."
"So, either the clocks are wrong..."
"Yes."
"... or we're not dealing with the Earth's ordinary gravitational field."
"Yes."
Weinstein stopped. "Then what are we dealing with?"
Matkowski considered him. "That is the interesting question."
Weinstein allowed. "Then it is decided."
"What is?"
"That we don't know."
Matkowski permitted himself the faintest smile. "Quite."
They continued down the stairs. In London, Derek Gent was standing in the Bureau, staring at the same photograph. Penny stood beside him.
Trevor was at the computer. The Lorentz transform against the timestamps had refused to close. "Mathematics says something here exceeds c, or our assumption of a shared event is dead," he maintained. "I vote we kill the assumption before we kill the light speed — and before Sophie's doorway becomes a second assumption we also refuse to kill."
The mathematics worked. The interpretation didn't. Derek considered the latest calculation. "We've got a problem."
"What sort?"
"The relative velocity required to transform the two timestamps is greater than the speed of light."
Penny agreed. "So, the two timestamps cannot simply be the same event described in two inertial frames."
"Correct."
Trevor glanced at the equation. "Then one of our assumptions is wrong."
"Yes."
"Which one?"
"We don't know."
"Could the photograph be genuine but the event misidentified?"
"Yes."
"Could the clock be genuine but the frame wrong?"
"Yes."
"Could both be genuine and there be another effect?"
Derek looked at her.
"Yes."
Trevor thought for a moment. "Gravity."
Derek allowed. "Possibly."
Surprise briefly overtook Trevor's face.
"You think General Relativity?"
"I think we shouldn't ignore it."
Penny agreed. "That would be sensible."
Derek studied the silent clock for a long moment, turning it over once in his hands, as though weight alone might explain what angle couldn't.
"The effect is too large."
Trevor walked over. "By how much?"
Derek showed him. 
Trevor whistled softly. "That isn't a small correction."
"No."
"Could a strong gravitational field do it?"
"Certainly."
"Do we have one?"
Derek surveyed the empty office. "Not unless the landlord has installed a neutron star."
Penny eyed the floor. "Perhaps he has."
Derek smiled. "Then I want my rent reduced."
The telephone rang.
Derek answered, "Derek Gent."
A familiar voice explained: "Good morning."
Derek allowed himself a smile. "Herbert."
"Yes."
"Let me guess," Derek said. "Munich."
"Obviously."
"Still?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
"Alfred is here."
Derek watched Penny. "That's convenient."
"No."
"What?"
"You don't understand."
"Explain."
"He has found the same anomaly."
Derek straightened.
"The clocks?"
"Yes."
"Same magnitude?"
"Essentially."
Penny's expression changed. She had gone very still, the way she did whenever the ordinary rules of a Tuesday afternoon stopped applying. Trevor surveyed her. "What?"
She whispered: "Same anomaly in Munich."
Trevor understood.
Derek declared: "Put Alfred on."
There was a click. Then a voice. "Mr Gent."
"Alfred."
"You have the clock."
"Yes."
"I have seen it."
"I know."
"No. I mean I have seen that exact clock."
"Where?"
"In your Bureau."
Derek glanced at the ancient brass clock. "When?"
"Tomorrow."
Derek sat down. 
Penny took the phone. "Alfred — we already have your tomorrow. Alive and dead. Platform seven. Don't retell it. Tell us what changed since."
"I saw the clock."
