"And?"
"I saw you."
"Alive?"
"Yes."
Penny studied Derek. "And?"
"The clocks," Weinstein reported. "Here and there. The disagreement is growing — and ordinary height cannot buy it. Principle, not magnitude."
Trevor leaned in. 
Derek took the phone. "So we meet where the file points. Not to re-argue whether I can be two things. To measure what kind of geometry makes the file look finished."
"I always am."
"Yes," Weinstein added. "And bring the child out of the noun Murder before the drawer closes."
"Yes."
"At the same location?"
"Apparently."
"At the same time?"
"That depends on which time you mean."
Derek's mouth curved. "Good answer."
"Thank you."
Penny went quiet, looking at the ancient brass clock instead of at either of them. "It isn't settled at all. He's coming here to look at a clock that's already seen tomorrow. That's not an arrangement. That's a rehearsal."
Outside, the rain had not let up. Derek glanced once at the window before returning to the phone. "Alfred, do you know what causes the effect?"
"No."
"Could it be gravitational?"
"Yes."
"Ordinary Earth gravity?"
"No."
"Could it be acceleration?"
"Possibly."
Derek asked, "Could it be spacetime curvature?"
"Perhaps."
"Could it be a combination?"
"Could be."
Derek smirked. "Very helpful."
"I am not there to solve your case."
"Then why are you coming to London?"
There was a pause. "Because I am curious."
Derek confirmed. "You're coming, then."
"Yes."
"Good," Derek allowed, and did not say the rest of it — that if Weinstein had already watched him die once, watching him alive again might not stay true for long.
"When?"
"Tomorrow."
Derek eyed Penny. Penny grinned. "Of course."
Weinstein continued: "I'll meet you at the station."
"Which station?"
"London Terminus."
Derek returned to the photograph. "Platform seven?"
"Yes."
Derek went silent. Penny understood immediately.
"That was in the photograph."
"Yes," Derek said.
Alfred remarked: "I know."
Derek glanced at the brass clock. "Alfred."
"Yes?"
"Why are you so certain?"
A pause. "Because I've already been there."
Derek frowned. "Tomorrow?"
"Yes."
"And you've already seen me?"
"Yes."
"And the clock?"
"Yes."
Derek thought. "Then we have a causal loop."
"Perhaps."
Penny studied him. "Don't get excited."
"I wasn't."
"You were."
Derek let a trace of amusement through. "I was mildly interested."
"That's your version of excitement."
Trevor conceded. "It is."
Derek ignored both of them.
"Alfred, one more thing."
"Yes?"
"Does General Relativity actually explain what we're seeing, or just remind us we're asking the wrong question?"
There was a pause.
Then Weinstein answered: "It explains the principle."
"But not the effect."
"No."
Derek waited. Weinstein continued:
"The gravitational time-dilation effect is real. We understand it extremely well. What we don't understand is why your clocks are showing a difference so large."
Derek allowed. "So, we're not discovering new relativity."
"No."
"We're applying known relativity to an unknown situation."
"Exactly."
"The physics isn't broken," Derek said.
"No."
"The situation is."
"Yes."
There was a silence on the line that lasted a beat too long for a shrug.
The call ended. Derek put down the phone. "We're going to London Terminus tomorrow." Trevor stared at him.
"Yes."
"Yes."
"After your supposed murder."
"Yes."
Penny picked up her coat. "And Weinstein will be there."
"Yes."
Derek picked up the ancient brass clock. The second hand moved. Tick.
Tick. It reached seventeen.
Stopped. Then started again. Derek peered at it.
"There's still one thing."
Penny paused at the door. "What?"
Derek turned the silent clock over. The initials LGV were still stamped into the back.
"I want to know what this means."
Trevor eyed it.
"Perhaps Weinstein knows."
Derek granted. "Perhaps."
Penny opened the door. "Ask him tomorrow."
Derek put the silent clock in his coat pocket. "Good idea."
They left the Bureau. Behind them, the computer switched itself on. A new case note mysteriously appeared.
CASE 1047
REFERENCE FRAME: INERTIAL
GRAVITATIONAL CORRECTION: REQUIRED
Derek's name suddenly appeared beneath it. 
Then Trevor's. 
Then Penny's.
Then Alfred Weinstein's. 
Finally, one more line appeared.
HERBERT MATKOWSKI: CONSULTING
The screen went dark. In Munich, at almost exactly the same moment, the wall clock in the patent attorney's familiar office stopped. Weinstein noticed.
He watched his watch. Then at the clock. Then at the second hand.
It had stopped at seventeen. Something eased in his face. Matkowski noticed.
"What?"
Weinstein held up his watch. "Same thing."
"Indeed."
Weinstein put the watch away. "Good. Progress."
"What is?"
"We're going to London."
Matkowski considered. "And the patent?"
Weinstein was already reaching for his coat. "The spring can wait."
They walked towards the strange station. Behind them, in the patent attorney's office, the silent clock started again. Only this time it was running backwards.

Rule: Height is cheap. Disagreement between honest clocks is not.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 4 - Enter General Relativity
Chapter 5
The Man Who Had Mislaid Time
London Terminus would have to wait a few hours longer. The moment Weinstein heard the name Barbarian over the phone, he had declared — with the particular finality of a man settling an argument that predated the conversation by several years — that they would all be meeting in Munich first, since Barbarian never travelled and the physics would keep no better in London than it had anywhere else. Derek, Penny and Trevor found themselves rerouted onto a connecting train within the hour, with the vague understanding that Weinstein and Matkowski would make their own way there faster, by means neither of them had bothered to explain and Derek had decided not to ask about.
Julius Barbarian lived in a house which appeared to have been designed by someone who had once been told what a house was and had then decided to improvise. It stood on a quiet street in Munich, behind a low wall and a hedge which had clearly given up several years earlier. There was no sign on the gate.
There was, however, a small brass plaque beside the front door.
JULIUS BARBARIAN
PHYSICIST
Underneath, in smaller letters:
APPOINTMENTS ARE A HUMAN CONVENTION
Underneath that, in smaller letters still, clearly added by hand at a later date:
(Tuesdays remain broadly real. Mostly.)
Derek Gent read it twice. "He's going to be one of those people."
Penny surveyed the plaque. "Almost certainly."
Trevor still stood behind them, holding the ancient brass clock. "What sort of people?"
"The sort who put philosophical statements on doorways."
Trevor considered this. "Could be worse."
"How?"
"They could put them on mugs."
Penny conceded. "Fair point."
Derek pressed the bell. Nothing happened. He pressed it again.
Still nothing. He pressed it a third time. The door quietly opened.
A man stood there. Julius Barbarian was in his late sixties, perhaps early seventies. Derek would later discover that this was not his real name — his real name was Julian Barbour, a physicist who had written a very respectable book arguing that time itself might not exist — and would reflect, briefly and with some irritation, that this made another Julian in his life, which was pushing coincidence well past its structural limits.
He was slim, silver-haired, and dressed in a dark jacket, open-necked shirt and rather expensive-looking trousers that had nevertheless acquired a small stain of something orange. He looked at Derek.
Then Penny. Then Trevor. Then the silent clock.
"You're late."
Derek glanced at his watch. "We're three minutes early."
"Precisely."
Barbarian opened the door wider. "Come in."
Derek hesitated. "How did you know who we were?"
"I knew you were coming."
"How?"
"Your car."
"We came by train."
"Exactly."
Derek stared at him. Barbarian looked amused. "Come in."
They entered. The hallway was surprisingly ordinary. Bookshelves occupied both walls.
There were papers everywhere. Not piles of papers, as in an ordinary untidy quiet office. Rather, papers appeared to have been distributed according to some private mathematical principle which Barbarian alone understood.
There were books on physics, philosophy, mathematics and cosmology. There were also several books about gardening. One shelf contained nothing but clocks.
Derek stopped. There were perhaps twenty of them. Pocket watches.
Marine chronometers. Railway clocks. A grandfather clock.
A small digital clock. A sundial. And, inexplicably, an egg timer.
Trevor noticed it too.
"Do you collect clocks?"
"No."
Barbarian took off his jacket. "I collect things people use to pretend time exists. Watches. Calendars. Murder files. Charming superstitions."
Derek and Penny traded a look. 
Trevor inspected the clocks. "That's a rather strong statement."
Barbarian smirked gently. "I've been told."
He walked into the sitting room. They followed. A large wooden table occupied the centre of the room.
There were five chairs, which struck Derek as an oddly specific number for a man who lived alone, until he noticed Weinstein already standing at the far bookshelf, coat still on, apparently having let himself in some minutes earlier and gone straight for the books without troubling anyone with a greeting.
Barbarian sat down. The others did likewise.
For several seconds nobody spoke. 
Then Barbarian looked directly at Weinstein. "Alfred."
Weinstein, who had been examining a book on the shelf, spun to face them.
"Yes?"
"You've spent most of your life making time behave."
"I've spent most of my life making physicists stop treating it as obvious."
"Better."
Barbarian leaned back. "Now you're having trouble with it."
Weinstein sat opposite him. "We have clocks that appear to disagree by an amount that cannot be explained by ordinary gravitational time dilation."
Julius allowed. "Yes."
"And the same anomaly appears in London and Munich."
"Yes."
"And the clocks appear to be connected to an event which hasn't happened yet."
"Yes."
"And you invited us here because you think the problem is that we're assuming time exists."
Julius Barbarian smirked. "Perhaps."
Derek nodded. "Close enough for government paperwork."
Barbarian smirked. "No."
Weinstein waited. Barbarian smirked over his tea. "You still think time is a river you can fall into, Alfred. Events do not happen. Only relationships exist. Derek wants a culprit. Trevor wants a closed algebra. Penny wants meaning for a child in a doorway. I want you to stop voting for a substance that isn't on the menu."
Weinstein conceded slowly. "That's different."
"Very."
Derek leaned forward. "How?"
Barbarian considered him. "Tell me what time is."
Derek's mouth twitched. "That's a dangerous question."
"It's a simple one."
"No. It's a question designed to make the answer complicated."
"Excellent."
Derek glanced at the silent clock on the wall. "Time is what clocks measure."
Barbarian's eyes glinted. "No."
"That's at least a useful operational definition."
"Certainly."
"Then what's wrong with it?"
"Nothing."
Derek paused. "That's a trap dressed as a definition."
Barbarian laughed. "I like you."
Penny smiled. "He's getting that a lot lately."
Barbarian turned the old clock in his hands. "You want a killer. He wants a clean equation. She wants the child out of the noun. I want the relationship that made the Bureau shout Murder at a geometry problem."
"Yes."
"So, a clock doesn't measure some invisible substance called time."
Derek confirmed. "It measures change against change."
"Exactly."
Interest sharpened Trevor's expression.
"That doesn't mean time doesn't exist."
Barbarian faced him. "No. It means we have to be careful about what we mean by 'exists.'"
Trevor allowed. "I'll grant you that."
Barbarian smirked. "Excellent."
He gestured at the table. "Suppose I place an apple here." He put it down, then moved it. "The apple has changed position. Do we need time to describe that, or only two configurations and a relationship between them?"
Trevor's brow creased. "So where does time go?"
Barbarian allowed himself a small smile. "That's the question."
Derek leaned back. "It doesn't necessarily go anywhere."
"Precisely."
Penny gazed at the clocks. "So, you're saying that reality consists of configurations, and what we call time is the ordering or relationship between them."
"Something like that."
Weinstein folded his hands. "That is philosophically attractive."
Barbarian raised an eyebrow. "But?"
"But General Relativity doesn't give us such an easy picture."
Julius granted. "Quite."
"In General Relativity, time is part of spacetime."
"Yes," Julius granted.
"And the geometry of spacetime is dynamical."
"Yes."
"Proper time along a worldline is measurable."
"Yes."
"An observer carries a clock, and the clock measures proper time along that observer's worldline."
"Yes."
Weinstein's mouth twitched with something like appreciation. "So, we cannot simply declare time nonexistent and walk away."
Barbarian smirked back. "I wasn't planning to."
"Good."
"I have a much more irritating plan."
Derek faced Penny. "He's definitely one of those people."
Penny confirmed. "Yes."
Barbarian continued, "General Relativity already told you: there is no single universal time. Your alibi problem is that everyone still behaves as if there were."
Weinstein agreed. "Correct."
"In Special Relativity, simultaneity is relative."
"Yes."
"In General Relativity, the situation becomes even richer because spacetime itself is dynamical."
"Yes."
"So, the universe doesn't come with a giant clock mounted above it."
Derek turned that over privately. If simultaneity was relative — if there was no "now" that everyone shared — then dread had no more claim to being universal than a wristwatch did. His fear of tomorrow was one frame's reading. It wasn't the only one available, and it certainly wasn't the invariant one.
Derek mentioned: "That would be inconveniently large."
Barbarian looked pleased with himself. "Quite."
Trevor eyed the silent clock shelf.
"Then what are clocks actually telling us?"
"Relationships."
"Between what?"
"Between physical processes."
Trevor inclined his head. "Right."
Barbarian's smile sharpened.
"As settled as anything ever is."
Derek laughed. "We've been here ten minutes and you're already using our phrase."
"I adapt quickly."
Penny gestured towards the shelf. "Then why all the clocks?"
"Because they're useful."
"But you responded that they don't measure time."
"I opined that they don't measure a substance called time."
"What's the difference?"
Barbarian smirked. "Quite a large one."
He picked up an old railway watch. "This tells you how one physical process relates to another. If you synchronise two clocks under specified conditions, move them apart, accelerate one, place them at different gravitational potentials and compare them again, the difference tells you something about spacetime."
Weinstein confirmed. "Exactly."
"So, clocks are not the problem."
"No."
"They are evidence."
"Yes."
"Excellent."
Barbarian put the watch down. "The problem is the interpretation." Penny agreed. "Exactly. We're skating on thin gravy."
Derek surveyed the brass clock Trevor was holding. "Which brings us to this."
Barbarian's expression changed. Only slightly. But everyone noticed.
"Yes."
He stood. "May I?"
Trevor handed him the clock. Barbarian turned it over. He examined the initials.
Then the mechanism. Then the face. He remained silent.
Weinstein noted the flicker in his expression. "You've seen it before."
Barbarian looked up. "Yes."
"When?"
Barbarian smirked. "That's the wrong question."
Weinstein grimaced. "Don't start."
Barbarian laughed. "Very well."
He placed the clock on the table. "I saw it yesterday." Derek didn't move.
Derek frowned. "Where?"
"Here. On this table." The silence that followed had weight. Everyone understood simultaneously: if the clock was here yesterday, and is here now, someone—or something—is describing the same location in two different moments.
Derek cast a quick look around.
"You had this clock yesterday?"
"No."
"Then what do you mean?"
"I saw it."
"When?"
"Yesterday."
Trevor's forehead creased. "That's perfectly ordinary."
Barbarian studied him. "Is it?"
Trevor thought. "Yes."
"Why?"
"Because yesterday precedes today."
Barbarian considered. "According to what?"
Trevor hesitated. "According to... Time."
Barbarian's eyes glinted. "Exactly."
Trevor, Derek, and Penny traded looks, ending on Barbarian. "That's rather clever," she said — then, quieter: "Clever does not get Sophie out of a photograph."
"Thank you."
Derek picked up the silent clock. "Let's be precise."
"Please."
"You saw this clock yesterday."
"Yes."
"The physical clock."
"Yes."
Derek clarified, "Not a photograph."
"No."
"Not an identical clock."
"No."
"This exact clock."
"Yes."
Derek frowned. "And it is currently here."
"Yes."
"Which means either you saw it before it arrived here..."
Barbarian nodded. "... or the clock has somehow moved backwards through whatever we mean by time."
"Possibly."
"Or your memory is wrong."
"Also possible."
Derek conceded. "Good."
Barbarian smirked. "You see the difficulty."
"Yes."
Weinstein checked the silent clock. "Where did you see it?"
Barbarian indicated the silent clock. "Here."
Derek looked up sharply. "Here?"
"On this table."
"But it wasn't here yesterday."
Barbarian allowed. "Not according to your description."
Weinstein smirked. "Now we're getting somewhere."
Barbarian sat down again. "This is precisely why I wanted to speak to you."
"Why?"
"Because you understand the mathematics well enough not to be frightened by the philosophical consequences."
Derek smirked. "That's a generous assessment."
"Is it wrong?"
"No."
"Right."
Weinstein picked up a notebook. "Suppose we forget the word 'time' for a moment."
Derek confirmed. "We have events."
"Yes."
"Each event has relationships to other events."
"Yes."
"Those relationships can be described geometrically."
"Yes."
"Some observers call one relationship earlier and another later."
"Yes."
"But those labels are not fundamental."
Barbarian smirked. "Now you're talking."
Weinstein continued, "And the Lorentz transformation tells us how different inertial observers assign coordinates to events — labels, not sacred hours."
"Yes."
"While General Relativity tells us how the geometry of spacetime behaves in the presence of matter and energy."
"Yes."
"And proper time is what a physical clock measures along a worldline."
"Yes."
"So, perhaps what we're calling time is the structure of these relationships."
Barbarian granted. "That is a respectable way of putting it."
Derek leaned forward. "And if the clocks aren't showing different amounts of some universal substance called time..."
Barbarian waited. 
"They're showing different relationships between events."
"Exactly."
Derek glanced at Penny. "Good. Moving on."
Penny confirmed. "Good."
Relief crossed Trevor's face.
"Does it?"
Everyone stared at him. Trevor gestured at the silent clock. "We still don't know why the relationships are wrong."
There was silence. 
Barbarian's mouth curved. "Excellent."
Derek huffed, not quite a laugh. "Yes."
Trevor surveyed the room. "What?"
"Nothing," Derek said.
"You all look pleased."
"We are."
"Why?"
"Because you noticed the actual problem."
Trevor conceded. "Oh."
Penny smiled. "Well, there we are."
Barbarian still stood. "Come."
"Where?"
"The garden."
Derek looked sharply towards the window. "Why?"
"Because I want to show you something."
They followed him through the back door. The quiet garden was larger than Derek had expected. There was a lawn, a small pond, several trees and a stone path.
At the far end quietly stood a tall wooden post. Barbarian walked towards it. At the top was a strange clock.
Derek's brow furrowed. "Another one."
"Yes."
Barbarian gazed at the silent clock.
"This one is synchronised with the one inside."
Derek checked. They were.
"How far apart?"
"About forty metres."
"Same gravitational potential to a very good approximation."
"Yes."
"Then they should remain almost synchronised."
"They do."
Trevor visibly relaxed.
"Good."
Barbarian smirked. "Now wait."
They waited. Nothing happened. A bird landed on the fence.
A car passed somewhere beyond the garden. The wind slowly moved the leaves. The clocks continued ticking.
Derek peered at his watch. Five minutes passed. Then ten.
Then fifteen. Still nothing. "What are we waiting for?"
"Change."
Derek smirked. "That's a very philosophical answer."
"It's also accurate."
Another five minutes. Then the silent clock on the post stopped. The indoor clock continued.
Derek watched Barbarian. "Well, that's odd."
"Yes."
Trevor examined the clock. "Mechanical failure?"
"Possibly," Barbarian affirmed.
Derek shook his head.
"No."
"Why?"
"The second hand isn't jammed."
He indicated it. The hand was moving. But it was moving in tiny jumps.
One second. Then a pause. Then another.
Penny leaned in. "The clock is still running."
"Yes."
"Then why isn't the display advancing?"
Puzzlement crossed Derek's face. He surveyed the silent clock inside the house. That one had also stopped.
His phone had stopped too. 
Stopped. 
Stopped. Everyone looked at one another. Barbarian's eyes lit up. "Interesting."
Derek stared at him. "Did you arrange this?"
"No."
"Did you know it would happen?"
"No."
"Then why are you smiling?"
"Because now we have something useful."
The seconds did not move. The world did not stop. A bird flew across the quiet garden.
A leaf fell. The wind continued. Derek studied the clocks. "Everything is moving."
"Yes."
"Yet all the clocks have stopped."
"Yes."
Trevor's brow creased. "So, the clocks have failed."
Derek shook his head.
"No."
"How do you know?"
"Because they're all failing identically."
Penny confirmed. "And at exactly the same reading."
Derek thought. "The train."
Everyone went quiet. The photograph.
11:03 - DEATH
12:04 - ARRIVAL
Derek took out the photograph. 
The clock in the photograph showed 11:03:17. 
Then he examined Barbarian. "Can you make them start again?"
Barbarian shook his head.
"I don't control them."
"Who does?"
"I don't know."
Derek watched Weinstein. 
Weinstein was staring at the sky. "What?"
Weinstein gestured sharply. "Look."
They looked upward. Nothing unusual was visible. Blue sky.
Clouds. A plane. Then Derek noticed something.
The plane wasn't moving. Neither was the cloud. The leaves had stopped.
The bird was suspended in the air. 
"That's impossible."
Derek grinned. "Yes."
Penny considered him. "Don't say it."
"Why?"
"Because I know what you're going to say."
Derek's grin died halfway. "Nothing about this is settled. Everything just stopped, and we're still moving."
Barbarian looked remarkably calm. Weinstein looked delighted, patting his pockets for a pencil he didn't have. "The physical world has stopped," he announced, in the tone of a man who has just been handed a research grant.
Barbarian shook his head. "No."
"What do you mean, no?"
"Look at the pond."
They did. The surface of the water was perfectly still. But a small insect was sitting on it.
Not moving. Derek took in the room. Nothing moved.
Not the leaves. Not the clouds. Not the distant plane.
Not the bird. Not the water. 
Then Penny gestured. "Look at Trevor."
Everyone glanced at Trevor who was standing perfectly still. He blinked.
Everyone leaned in. 
Trevor blinked again. "What?"
Derek allowed himself a smile. "You're moving."
Trevor flexed his fingers, testing whether they'd argue back. "So, are you."
Penny slowly moved her head. "So, am I."
Weinstein turned to Barbarian. "You can move."
"Yes."
"So, can we."
"Yes."
"But everything else is frozen."
"Apparently."
He eyed the sky. Still frozen. 
He watched Barbarian. "How?"
Barbarian smirked. "Perhaps time has stopped."
Derek's expression tightened. "You've been waiting your entire career to say that, haven't you?"
"Possibly."
Trevor tapped his wrist, as if checking it still worked. "If time has stopped, why are we moving?"
Barbarian inclined his head.
Derek thought. "Because our local physical processes haven't stopped."
"Perhaps."
"Then time hasn't stopped."
Something amused crossed Barbarian's face. "Perhaps."
Weinstein looked annoyed. "You're enjoying this."
"Immensely."
"Why?"
"Because you're trying to make the word 'time' do too much work."
Weinstein folded his arms. "Explain."
Barbarian gestured around the quiet garden. "When every clock freezes at 11:17 while people still change, either the clocks failed — Trevor's nightmare — or they identified an event. Decide which, or stop calling this a murder investigation."
"All right."
"Suppose instead there are physical configurations."
He indicated the frozen plane. "One configuration contains that plane in one position."
He gestured at the garden. "Another contains it somewhere else."
He nodded at the clocks. "The clocks are physical systems within those configurations."
He gestured at Trevor. "And so are we."
Trevor agreed. "Yes."
"So if we move from one configuration to another while the clocks don't advance, the clocks don't provide the ordering."
Derek understood.
"The relationships do."
"Just so."
Weinstein frowned. "But then what has happened to the time coordinate in the equations?"
Barbarian smirked. "Nothing."
"Nothing?"
"The equations remain useful."
"Then you aren't denying time in physics."
"I'm denying that the symbol t must correspond to a fundamental substance flowing through the universe."
Weinstein conceded slowly. "That's more defensible."
"Much."
Penny surveyed the strangely frozen plane. "So what we're seeing is a physical configuration in which the clocks aren't advancing."
"Yes."
"But we're still changing."
"Yes."
"Then the clocks aren't fundamental."
"No."
"They're just one type of physical process."
"Exactly."
Derek studied the silent brass clock. "And this one is somehow associated with a different sequence of configurations."
Barbarian allowed. "Perhaps."
Derek smiled. "Fine. Next problem."
Trevor regarded him. "What?"
"We've learned something."
"What?"
"That the clock isn't measuring some universal time."
"Right."
"It is measuring something physical."
"Yes."
"And that physical process can disagree with other physical processes."
"Yes."
Penny allowed herself a smile. "Which is exactly what relativity already told us."
Derek confirmed. "Yes."
Weinstein fell silent in thought. "And yet this is different."
"Why?"
"Because in ordinary relativity, the clocks disagree according to the geometry of spacetime."
Barbarian considered. "And here?"
Weinstein swept a hand towards the frozen garden. "We have no idea what geometry we're dealing with."
Barbarian smirked. "Now you've reached the interesting part."
The bird suddenly moved. It flew away. The leaves rustled.
The distant plane continued across the sky. The cloud moved. Water rippled in the pond.
Everyone stood silently. 
Trevor took in the room. "So what actually happened?"
"Time exists," Derek declared.
Barbarian shook his head. "No. Something used it as a hinge. There's a difference, and Sophie is standing on the wrong side of it."
Nobody laughed. The clocks continued ticking. 
Derek studied the photograph again.
11:03 - DEATH
12:04 - ARRIVAL
Then he noticed something he hadn't noticed before. There was a fourth line. It had been hidden beneath the fold — not philosophy. A case answer waiting for a frame.
He unfolded the paper. It read:
12:04 - ALL CLOCKS START
A new line appeared in Case 1047 under REMOTE CONSULTANT NOTE: SHERLOCK 
 If every clock agrees too perfectly, ask whether it is telling time or identifying something.
Derek froze. Penny leaned over. Trevor read it.
Weinstein faced Barbarian. 
Barbarian's smile disappeared. "That's new."
Derek raised his eyes. "Very."
Trevor confirmed: "But the clocks just stopped."
"Yes."
"And started again."
"Yes."
"Exactly as described."
"Yes."
Penny glanced at the paper. "So, this wasn't a malfunction."
Derek shook his head.
"No."
"It was an event."
"Yes."
Weinstein allowed. "And the clocks recorded it."
Barbarian tilted his head. "Or participated in it."
Derek considered that. "What's the difference?"
Barbarian permitted himself the faintest flicker of amusement. "That is the question."
The ancient brass clock in Derek's pocket began ticking. Once. Twice.
Then it stopped. Derek took it out. 
The face now read: 12:04. 
Derek met his eyes.
"Apparently."
Penny stared at the clock's new face. "Twelve-oh-four. That's not inconvenient. That's a deadline with a different name."
Derek peered at the silent clock. "Then we'd better find out whose."
Weinstein took it from him. He examined the mechanism. Nothing.
No obvious fault. No hidden mechanism. No battery.
No radio receiver. No trick. He eyed Barbarian.
"Do you still think time doesn't exist?"
Barbarian considered the question. "I think reality is perfectly capable of existing without asking my permission."
Weinstein smirked. "That's not an answer."
"No."
"Then?"
Barbarian handed the silent clock back.
"Time may be a very useful description of relationships between events."
He paused. "But I have never been convinced that the universe itself carries a watch."
Derek watched the silent clock. "Perhaps it doesn't."
"Perhaps."
"Perhaps we're the ones carrying it."
"Exactly."
Derek put the silent clock back into his pocket. "We've got forty-seven minutes."
Trevor checked his phone, and his voice lost its usual lift. "Forty-seven minutes to what?"
Derek didn't smile this time. "To find out before it finds Sophie."
Barbarian surveyed him. "Excellent?"
"We have forty-seven minutes to find out where the train is going."
Barbarian shook his head.
"No."
Derek frowned. "What?"
"You have forty-seven minutes to find out where the train has already been."
Derek stared at him. Then grinned. "Right."
Penny picked up her coat. "London?"
"London."
Trevor stood. "Platform seven?"
"Platform seven."
Weinstein studied the clock. "And the train?"
Derek carefully opened the door. "We'll find it."
Barbarian called after him, "Mr Gent."
Derek pivoted. "Yes?"
Barbarian indicated the silent clock. "Don't trust it."
A flicker of amusement crossed Derek's face. "We weren't planning to."
Barbarian conceded. "Good."
Derek opened the door. Then stopped. "One thing."
"What?"
"If time doesn't exist..."
Barbarian's eyes glinted with something like mischief. "Yes?"
"What day is it?"
Barbarian regarded him. For the first time since they'd arrived, he seemed genuinely uncertain.
"Ah."
Penny laughed. Trevor laughed. Even Weinstein smiled.
Barbarian examined the clocks around the empty room. Then at the sky, then at the brass clock in Derek's hand. He did not answer the question about what day it was. That, more than anything else that afternoon, was the detail that stayed with Derek on the train back.
"You're taking this well," Barbarian observed, on the platform.
"There's very little else to do with it," Derek said. "I can't negotiate with a frozen bird. I can investigate one. That's the whole of the choice, so I might as well make it."
Barbarian looked at him with something that was not quite approval and not quite pity. "Most people need rather longer to arrive at that."
Penny's smile faded on the platform. "He can mislay time," she said. "He cannot mislay a twelve-year-old. That one still has a school bag."
"Most people," Derek added, "haven't had a Tuesday like mine."
And somewhere in London, a distant train began moving towards empty Platform Seven. It had left yesterday. It would arrive tomorrow.
And, according to the timetable, it had never existed at all.

Rule: Mislaid time is still someone's proper time. Find whose.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 5 - The Apple
Chapter 6
The Apple Problem
Alfred Weinstein had never entirely trusted apples. This was not because of any defect in the fruit — apples were perfectly respectable, if badly overcredited: one particular red apple had been blamed for an astonishing amount of physics, and Weinstein was thinking about it on the mysterious train. He was on his way back to Munich after the meeting with Julius Barbarian, although "on his way back" was perhaps an unnecessarily confident description of the situation. The train had stopped between two stations, for "circumstances" — a word Weinstein had learned, from long experience, meant absolutely anything. Two rows ahead, a child dropped an apple. It rolled beneath the seat. Weinstein watched the space where it had disappeared, and thought of Newton's tidy, over-managed story about a tree — charming, and almost certainly misleading.
The apple had not explained gravity. It had offered Weinstein a decision: treat falling bodies as geometry probes, or keep inventing forces until the Bureau's clocks looked polite.
It had merely demonstrated that something fell. The really interesting question was why. Weinstein leaned back.
Why did the apple fall? Everyone knew the answer. Gravity.
But that was not an answer. It was a name. He had spent most of his life telling physicists not to confuse naming a phenomenon with explaining it.
Newton had described gravity extraordinarily well. The inverse-square law worked. The equations worked.
The predictions worked. The planets obeyed them. The Moon obeyed them.
The apple obeyed them. Everything seemed to obey them. Then General Relativity had come along and changed the question.
Weinstein dropped a pencil. It fell. He dropped it again from the other side of the desk. Same fall — until the brass clock on the blotter skipped seventeen seconds without moving. The pencil had taken an ordinary path. The clock had not. He did not explain. He circled both.
The poor apple had been promoted from fruit to geometry. The distant train moved. Then stopped again.
Weinstein wearily sighed.
Then, very suddenly, he had an idea.
It was not a complete idea. It was not even a particularly respectable idea. It was the sort of idea that arrived without knocking, sat down in the middle of one's mind and refused to explain why it was there.
Weinstein sat upright. The ordinary apple. Not the falling. The falling together. He took out his notebook and drew the Earth, then the apple, then two arrows — one pointing from the apple towards the Earth, one pointing from the Earth towards the apple. He stared at them. "Of course." Newton's description had made it natural to think of the Earth as pulling the apple.
He tried a third drop. The pencil hit. The office mug slid three centimetres left by itself. Weinstein wrote: PROBES DISAGREE. Then, smaller: Sophie's doorway is also a probe. Do not forget.
The Earth moved too. Tiny amount. Almost impossibly tiny.
But not zero. Weinstein wrote:
THE SIMPLE APPLE DOES NOT FALL TOWARD THE EARTH.
He stopped. That was not quite right. He crossed it out.
THE EARTH AND THE APPLE FALL TOWARD EACH OTHER.
He inspected it. Better. Then another thought struck him.
If the apple and the Earth were both following the geometry, what exactly determined the geometry? 
Mass and energy. Fine.
He dropped the pencil again. Same fall. The brass clock skipped. He wrote: LOCAL FALL ≠ GLOBAL CLOCK. No further essay.
That was the heart of the matter. An ordinary apple falling from a tree was not being dragged through space by an invisible rope. It was freely falling.
And the tree was preventing it from doing so. 
Weinstein stopped. He considered the words.
The tree was preventing the simple apple from following its natural path. 
He grinned. "That's rather good."
Weinstein turned the page and drew a tree, the apple, the ground — then the same apple in a lift.
He imagined the lift accelerating upward in empty space. The apple would appear to fall.
Yet there would be no gravitational field. Then he imagined the lift sitting on Earth. 
The apple would fall again.
He closed the notebook before it could become a lecture.
Everyone in the Bureau knew this. So why had the thought bothered him? He glanced at his drawing again.
Because the apple was falling towards something. And in the accelerating lift there was no Earth. 
The apparent downward direction was created by the acceleration.
Gravity could be imitated by acceleration. But perhaps the deeper question was the reverse. 
Could acceleration be understood as geometry in the same way gravity was?
The idea felt new because the room was misbehaving, not because a textbook had cleared its throat.
Then he saw it. Not the answer. The question.
The question was not: Why does the apple fall? The question was:
Why does the apple have the trajectory it does? He wearily sat back. 
That was better.
Much better. 
The trajectory of a freely falling object was determined by spacetime geometry. But the geometry was determined by the distribution of matter and energy.
So, the simple apple was both responding to the geometry and, in principle, contributing to it. The apple affected the spacetime through which it moved. Tiny amount.
But real. The Earth affected the apple. 
The apple affected the Earth.
The Earth affected spacetime. 
The apple affected spacetime. Everything was interacting with everything else.
Weinstein tapped the page. "That's going to annoy someone."
The man sleeping beside the woman opened one eye. "What?"
"Nothing."
The man closed his eye again. Weinstein wrote:
ASK DEREK.
Then underneath:
INVESTIGATE THE ORDINARY APPLE.
He stopped. That sounded ridiculous. He crossed out  
THE ORDINARY APPLE 
Then wrote:
INVESTIGATE THE FALLING BODY.
He stared at it. Too clinical. He crossed out the whole line.
He wrote:
WHY DOES THE APPLE FALL?
Something in him eased. That was better. He took out his phone.
He called Derek.
Derek answered on the fourth ring, "Yes?"
"Alfred."
"Where are you?"
"On a train."
"Which one?"
"The Munich one."
Derek paused. "That's not terribly specific."
"I know," agreed Weinstein.
"Are you going somewhere?"
"Apparently."
"Do you know where?" asked Derek.
"Munich."
"You're already in Munich."
"I know."
There was silence.
Then Derek asked: "Has Julius Barbarian finally broken you?"
"No."
"Fine."
"I have a question."
"Go on."
"Why does an apple fall?"
Derek was silent. Then: "Are you serious?"
"Perfectly."
"Gravity."
"No."
Derek sighed, out of patience. "Don't start."
"I'm not starting anything."
"You've just asked me why an apple falls."
"Yes."
Derek continued, "And you're Alfred Weinstein, inventor of General Relativity - the theory that explains why it falls."
"Precisely."
"Then why are you asking me?" wondered Derek.
"Because I think the question is wrong."
Derek sat down. "All right."
Penny was sitting nearby. She sat up a little straighter.
Derek put the phone on speaker. "Go on."
Weinstein queried: "Suppose an apple is freely falling."
"Yes."
"Is the apple accelerating?"
"In the Newtonian sense, yes," Derek answered.
Weinstein dropped a coin. It curved wrong. "Geometry is the suspect. Don't name the path until it embarrasses you."
"Exactly," Weinstein confirmed.
"So, locally, the apple is not experiencing a force."
"Correct."
"Then why does it appear to accelerate towards the Earth?"
Derek thought. "Because we are observing it from a non-free-falling frame."
"Yes."
"And the Earth's surface is preventing us from following our own geodesic."
"Yes."
Penny confirmed. "That settles the basic point." Weinstein looked out of the window for a while, watching a bus fail to arrive on schedule. "But that's not the question. The apple and the Earth are both moving towards each other. Why?"
"Their mutual gravitational interaction," Derek said. "That's the Newtonian answer. You want the geometry — stress-energy determines it, the geometry determines the geodesics, the apple follows the geodesic."
"Yes."
Derek paused. "There we are, then."
Derek smiled. "You've just agreed to everything."
"I know."
"Then what isn't settled?"
Weinstein consulted his notebook. "The apple."
Penny leaned towards the phone. "What about it?"
"Why that apple?" Derek asked with a frown.
"Why that particular apple?"
"Yes."
"Why does it matter which apple?" Weinstein emphasised.
"That's what I don't know."
Penny smirked. "Now that's interesting."
Derek agreed. "Yes."
Weinstein continued: "The Bureau has been investigating events that appear to depend on which frame we use. Julius Barbarian has been arguing that time may not be fundamental. We have clocks behaving differently. And now I am thinking about an apple."
Derek commented: "That sounds like a normal Thursday."
"It's Wednesday."
"Right."
Weinstein continued, "Suppose the apple falls."
"Yes."
"Suppose we reverse the process."
Derek paused. "Reverse it?"
"Imagine reconstructing the trajectory."
"From the ground upward?"
"Yes."
"We'd have to know its position and velocity."
"Yes," Weinstein said, without hesitating.
"And the gravitational field."
"Yes."
Derek continued, "And the geometry."
"Yes."
"And the matter distribution."
"Yes. Exactly," Weinstein noted.
Derek waited. Then he understood.
"We'd need the entire local configuration."
"Yes."
Penny eyed Derek.
"So, the apple's trajectory contains information about the geometry."
"Yes."
"And the geometry contains information about the matter."
"Yes," Weinstein agreed.
"And therefore the falling apple is effectively telling us something about its environment."
"Exactly."
Derek grinned. "There, that's—"
"No," Weinstein cut in. "Don't say it's settled. I've just had another thought."
"Because I've just had another thought."
"Of course you have."
"The Bureau's clocks."
Derek straightened. "What about them?"
"They are following worldlines."
"Yes."
"They're measuring proper time."
"Yes."
"They are effectively sampling spacetime along those worldlines."
"Yes."
"And if their readings are anomalous..."
"Then the geometry is anomalous — perhaps."
Derek grimaced. "Or the clocks are."
"Yes."
"But we've already ruled out ordinary clock failure," said Derek.
"Yes."
"So..." Weinstein paused. Then he suggested "Investigate the trajectories."
Derek stared at the phone. "The trajectories of what?"
"Everything."
"Everything?"
"Start with the clocks."
Derek watched Penny.
Penny allowed. "That actually makes sense."
Derek observed: "We've been treating the clocks as stationary measuring devices."
"Yes."
"Perhaps we should treat them as physical objects following worldlines."
"Exactly."
Trevor, who clearly had been listening, suddenly appeared in the doorway.
"That means the clocks themselves are part of the experiment," he concluded.
Derek surveyed him. "Yes."
Trevor nodded. "Splendid. Moving on."
Derek's mouth twitched. "See? He's learning."
Trevor's expression brightened. 
Then asked: "What's a worldline?"
Penny laughed.
Derek replied: "Come in."
Trevor did.
Derek said: "In spacetime an object's history is a worldline. I'm not giving a lecture. I'm asking whether the brass clock's path is strange enough to sample a geometry that makes Case 1047 look finished."
Trevor considered. "And a clock follows a worldline, then?"
"Yes."
"So, the clock doesn't merely tell us time."
"Correct," said Derek.
"It gives us a physical record along its worldline."
"Quite."
Trevor studied the ancient brass clock. It skipped. He skipped the explanation. "Strange path. Strange filing. Same symptom."
Derek smiled. "Now you're ahead of me."
Trevor conceded. "That happens occasionally."
Penny regarded him. "Don't overdo it."
Trevor grinned. "I won't."
Derek returned to the phone. "Alfred, what exactly do you want us to investigate?"
Weinstein lowered his gaze at his notebook. "The apple."
Derek laughed. "You've come all this way in physics to send me after an apple."
"No."
"Then what?"
"The apple is the model."
"For what?"
"Everything."
Derek became serious.
Weinstein continued: "Enough trajectories and you reconstruct geometry. Your brass clock is a probe whether you like it or not. Reconstruct first. Name the tensors later — preferably never in front of Trevor."
Derek nodded. "Yes. So, if the Bureau is experiencing an unusual spacetime effect, we should stop staring at the clocks. We should watch what moves around them."
"Exactly."
Penny allowed herself a smile. "That's actually rather elegant."
Weinstein answered: "Thank you."
Derek asked: "Do you have a particular trajectory in mind?"
"Yes."
"Whose?"
Weinstein looked out the train's narrow window. A landscape passed. Trees.
Fields. Buildings. Everything apparently moving.
Or perhaps the train was moving. Or perhaps both. He smiled. "Mine."
Derek's brow creased. "What?"
"I want you to investigate my trajectory."
"Your worldline?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
"Because something happened in Munich."
"What?"
"My clock stopped." Derek heard him swallow, an odd sound down a phone line. "Before yours did. A full minute before."
Derek froze. Penny examined him. Trevor stopped moving.
Derek added: "When?"
"Eleven sixteen."
Derek stared at the ancient brass clock. "But ours stopped at eleven seventeen."
"Yes."
"So, there's a one-minute difference."
"Exactly."
Derek thought. "That could be ordinary synchronisation."
"No."
"How do you know?"
"Because I wasn't where you think I was."
Derek grimaced. "What do you mean?"
"I was on the train."
Derek went very still. "What train?"
"The train to Munich."
"You were going to Munich."
"Yes."
"You were already in Munich."
"Yes."
Derek closed his eyes for a moment. "Alfred."
"Yes?"
"You were on a train going to Munich while you were in Munich."
"Yes."
"How?"
"I don't know."
Penny smirked. "That's rather good."
Derek appraised her. "Is it?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
"Because now we have a genuine problem."
Derek returned to the phone. "Alfred, where are you now?"
There was a pause. "On the train."
"Which train?"
"The train to Munich."
Derek's brow furrowed. "But you're in Munich."
"It would seem so."
Trevor looked at Penny. "Could he be in two reference frames?"
Penny shook her head. "Not in that sense."
Trevor agreed, "Right."
Derek asked, "Alfred, can you see the station?"
"Yes."
"Which one?"
"Munich."
Derek pressed the phone harder to his ear. "Are you moving?"
"Yes."
"Relative to the station?"
A pause, then: "Yes."
Derek rubbed his forehead. "Then what is happening?"
Weinstein declared: "I don't know."
Derek allowed. "Excellent. Case closed."
Weinstein laughed. "Precisely."
Derek glanced at Penny. She was smiling. "What?"
"You're enjoying this."
"Not the impossible train," she replied.
"The investigation."
"Yes."
Derek turned back to the phone. "Alfred, what does the train clock say?"
All four devices were synchronised.
Derek asked: "And your watch?"
"All right." Derek took a breath. "Now look outside. What do you see?"
"Munich."
Derek asked, "Moving?"
"Yes."
"Backwards?"
"No."
"Forward?"
"Yes."
Derek confirmed. "Good."
"Why?" asked Weinstein.
"Because I was beginning to worry."
"About what?"
"That we might have to investigate Munich."
Weinstein laughed. Then his quiet voice changed. "Wait."
"What?"
"I see something."
"What?"
"An apple."
Derek eyed Penny. "What sort of apple?"
"Red."
"That's encouraging."
"It is falling."
"Where?"
"From a tree."
Derek grinned. "Alfred, are you sure you're not just watching an ordinary apple?"
"I am."
"Then what is it doing?"
Weinstein was silent long enough that Derek checked whether the call had dropped.
Finally Weinstein asserted: "It's falling upward."
Nobody spoke. Trevor's mouth fell open. Penny regarded Derek.
Derek remarked:
"Well."
Nobody smiled. Trevor said, quietly, "Gravity doesn't behave. Not like that. Not ever."
Weinstein's strange voice came through the phone. "No."
Derek fixed him with a look. "What?"
"The apple isn't falling upward."
"Then what is happening?"
Weinstein watched through the train window. "The train is."
Derek sat up straighter. "The train is falling?"
"No."
"Moving?"
"No."
Weinstein paused. "The train is changing."
Derek frowned. "Changing how?"
"The geometry."
There was silence.
"Alfred."
"Yes?"
Then Derek inquired: "Investigate the apple?"
Weinstein allowed himself a smile. "I was hoping you'd say that."
The line went dead. Derek put the phone down. Trevor regarded him. "So, we're investigating an apple."
Derek granted. "Apparently."
Penny picked up her coat. "When?"
"Immediately."
Trevor's brow furrowed. "Where?"
Derek's eyes moved from the ancient brass clock to the photograph, then towards the door.
"Munich."
Penny grinned. "Good."
Trevor still stood. "How are we getting there?"
Derek put on his coat. "Train."
Trevor considered him. "Which train?"
Derek paused. "The one that's already arrived."
Trevor considered this. "That sounds difficult."
Derek carefully opened the familiar door. "Yes."
Penny followed him. "That's why we have a Bureau."
They left. The quiet office remained empty. On the old desk lay Weinstein's forgotten notebook.
The last thing he had written was:
WHY DOES THE APPLE FALL?
Underneath, in smaller letters:
AND WHY DID IT CHOOSE TODAY?
The mysterious brass clock ticked once. Then twice. Then, very quietly, it began to run backwards. 
Weinstein did not immediately go to Munich. He went to Paris. This was not because Paris was on the way. It wasn't. It was also not because he had suddenly developed an interest in French cuisine, although he had no objection to French cuisine and considerable objections to German railway sandwiches. He went to Paris because Derek Gent had gone to the Louvre.
This was, in itself, suspicious.
Derek had once described museums as "warehouses for dead people who had better PR than most living people."
He had never shown the slightest interest in art. Once, in the National Gallery, he spent twenty minutes looking at an abstract painting before concluding that they must have hung it upside down.
He had also claimed that the Mona Lisa was "smiling because she knows something."
When Penny had asked him what, Derek had replied:
"That's what I'm trying to find out."
So when Weinstein learned that Derek had abruptly travelled to Paris and gone to the Louvre, he did not waste much time wondering whether Derek had developed an appreciation for Leonardo. He assumed Derek had found something. And knowing Derek, it would be something weird.
Very weird. 
Weinstein was standing beside the Seine when the thought about the red apple returned. Water moved continuously beneath the bridges, yet there was no obvious thing called "the flow of time" visible anywhere — only change. A boat here, then there. Barbarian's argument was irritating precisely because it was difficult to dismiss: time might not be a thing at all, merely a way of describing relationships between things, no stranger a claim than saying position or velocity isn't a substance either.
Weinstein stopped walking. He had been thinking about the apple as though it were following a line. But what if an event wasn't a line — what if it was something with structure? An ordinary apple falling from a tree wasn't merely a point moving through time. It had a history, a path, a geometry, all at once.
He took out his notebook, drew three axes, then crossed out the ordinary t for time and wrote τ instead. Proper time. But the apple did not experience the whole spacetime diagram — only its own worldline. A sequence of events, except Barbarian would object even to the word sequence, since sequence implied time. The apple's path was already finished on the page. Asking where it was "going" suddenly sounded like asking a railway timetable which passenger was guilty.
He closed the notebook. If Case 1047 was a finished curve wearing a murder label, then chasing a killer was walking the curve. He thought of Sophie in the doorway and stopped walking for a full minute — which, on his watch, lasted fifty-nine seconds, and on the brass clock lasted none. The red apple did not need to know about the future. The geometry did.
He wrote:
THE EVENT DOES NOT HAPPEN IN TIME.
THE EVENT IS IN SPACETIME.
He stared at the sentence. That was better. Much better. And suddenly he understood why the clocks in London and Munich were so troubling. They might not be disagreeing about time. They might be sampling different geometrical relationships. The clocks were innocent. The geometry was not.
Then his phone rang. It was Derek.
Weinstein answered, "Where are you?"
"Paris."
"I know."
A pause. "You know?"
"Yes."
"How?"
"You're at the Louvre."
Another pause.
"How do you know that?"
"Penny."
"Of course."
"Why are you there?"
"Because I found something."
"What?"
Derek replied, "I'll tell you when you get here."
"I'm already in Paris."
"Good."
"Where are you?"
"Inside."
"The Louvre?"
"Yes."
Weinstein glanced at the enormous building. "Why?"
"Because I think Leonardo knew something."
Weinstein closed his eyes briefly. "About what?"
"Time."
Weinstein opened his eyes. "Leonardo da Vinci?"
"Yes."
"The painter?"
"Among other things."
Weinstein exhaled slowly. "All right."
"Come inside."
"I am."
"Excellent."
The line went dead. Weinstein entered the Louvre. Marble underfoot, cool enough to feel through his shoes; the particular museum hush that wasn't silence so much as several hundred people agreeing, independently, to lower their voices; the smell of old varnish, polished stone, and someone's over-applied perfume drifting past a gilt frame. Tour groups clustered and dispersed like weather systems. It took him less than ten minutes to find Derek.
He was standing in front of a painting. Not the Mona Lisa. A much less famous work, in a side gallery the crowds hadn't discovered yet, where the quiet had a different, older quality to it.
Derek was staring at it with the concentration of a man trying to solve a murder. Weinstein approached.
"What are you looking for?"
Derek didn't turn.
"Geometry."
"Of the painting?"
"Of the event."
Weinstein stared at the painting. It was beautiful. It was also completely irrelevant.
Probably.
"What did you find?"
Derek gestured. "Look at the perspective."
"Yes."
"Notice anything?"
"No."
"Good."
Weinstein gazed at him.
"Good?"
"Yes. I wanted to know whether you'd see it immediately."
"See what?"
"The problem."
Weinstein examined the painting again. Then he understood.
"The vanishing point."
Something close to a smile crossed Derek's face. "Exactly."
Weinstein leaned in. "That's not unusual."
"No."
"But the way the scene is constructed..."
"Yes."
"It isn't simply perspective."
"No."
"It's a coordinate system."
Derek agreed. "Just so."
Weinstein stared at the painting. The objects appeared to converge towards a point. A mathematical point.
Not a physical object. A representation. A transformation.
A way of describing a three-dimensional scene on a two-dimensional surface. 
He smiled. "Interesting."
Derek finally faced him. "Now look at the other one."
They suddenly moved to another painting. Then another. Derek had marked nothing.
He didn't need to. He simply indicated it. Weinstein looked.
The same conceptual structure suddenly appeared. Not identical. But related.
Different projections. Different perspectives. Different ways of representing the same kind of spatial relationships.
Weinstein suddenly understood why Derek had brought him there.
"A coordinate system depends on the observer. So which event are we even arguing about in the Louvre — the painting, the clock, or the moment we mistook a label for a corpse?"
"Yes."
"And the underlying scene doesn't."
"Usually."
Weinstein gave him a measured look.
"Usually?"
Derek smirked. "We're dealing with my murder."
"Fair enough."
They moved to a quieter gallery. Derek stopped. "Now imagine that the paintings aren't two-dimensional."
"Go on."
"Imagine each painting is a slice through a three-dimensional object."
Weinstein confirmed. "Yes."
"Different observers see different slices."
"Yes."
"But the object itself is larger than any one slice."
"Yes."
Derek eyed him.
"And now add time."
Weinstein became still.
"Three dimensions of space."
"Yes."
"And one temporal dimension."
"Yes."
"Then an event is not merely a point moving through time."
"No."
"It's a point in four-dimensional spacetime."
"Yes."
"There," Derek concluded. "We've found the shape of the problem."
Weinstein shook his head. "Not quite."
"Why?"
"Because you're still thinking of time as the fourth dimension in the same way as the three spatial dimensions."
Derek's brow furrowed sharply. "What's wrong with that?"
"Nothing mathematically."
"Then?"
"The metric."
Derek waited, then grimaced in an entirely different direction. "Is that the same metric as in metric system? Miles, kilometres, that business?"
"No."
"Because Britain never actually finished converting. We measure distance in miles, beer in pints, and people's weight in stone, and then get personally offended when a French train timetable uses anything sensible."
"Derek."
"I'm simply saying, if the universe wanted us to trust a metric, it should have seen how we handled the last one." Derek considered this further. "Even after leaving the EU. Especially after leaving the EU, if anything — we complained about being told what a kilogram was, and then kept the kilogram."
Weinstein exhaled with the particular patience of a man who had, at this point, budgeted for detours. "That is a different metric."
"Is it a metric tensor or a system of measurement, though? Genuinely. Because both of you keep saying 'the metric' as though the other one doesn't exist, and one of you invented inches."
"They are unrelated by every definition except the word."
"That seems like exactly the kind of coincidence this case has taught me not to trust." Derek was not finished. "And another thing. Three-eighths of an inch. Everybody knows what that is. You can see it. You can practically feel it. Say 'nought point three-seven-five' to a carpenter and watch him age badly in real time."
"Three-eighths is not more precise. It is merely more familiar to you."
"It's easier, Alfred. There's a difference. The metric system is correct. Fractions are comfortable. This case has spent a week teaching me that being correct and being comfortable are not the same axis, and frankly I resent how well that generalises."
Weinstein said nothing for a moment, which from Weinstein counted as a concession.
Derek waited. 
Weinstein continued, "The temporal coordinate doesn't behave like an ordinary spatial coordinate. The sign is different."
Derek allowed. "Lorentzian geometry."
"Exactly."
"So, not an ordinary four-dimensional Euclidean space."
"No."
"Spacetime has a different geometry."
"Yes."
Derek returned his attention to the painting. "Could we represent it visually?"
"Only imperfectly."
"Why?"
"Because we're trying to draw four dimensions on three-dimensional paper."
Derek grinned. "That sounds like the sort of thing the Bureau was designed for."
Weinstein laughed. Then Derek's expression changed.
"There's something else."
"What?"
"Look at the people."
Weinstein did.
"Yes?"
"They aren't simply objects in space."
"No."
"They are events."
Weinstein surveyed him. "Careful."
"Why?"
"Because a person is a worldline, not a single event."
Derek let himself look pleased, briefly. "Precisely."
"And you're about to tell me you've found something."
"I have."
"What?"
Derek gestured towards the gallery. "That man."
A security guard stood near the doorway. Weinstein looked. "Yes?"
"He's walked past us three times without ever appearing in the same place twice."
Weinstein frowned. "That's normal."
"Not in a straight corridor, it isn't."
"Yes."
"And each time he was carrying a different newspaper."
Weinstein stared. "That's odd."
"Very."
"Could be different guards."
"No."
"How do you know?"
"Same face."
"Same clothes?"
"Exactly."
Weinstein studied the guard again. The man pivoted. For a fraction of a second, his eyes met Weinstein's.
Then he walked away.
Derek mentioned quietly:
"That's our man."
Weinstein's brow creased. "Who?"
"I don't know."
"That's rather inconvenient."
"Yes."
Derek smiled. "But I know where he goes."
"Where?"
Derek indicated a staircase. "Down."
They followed. The staircase led beneath the museum. The further they went, the older the building became.
Eventually they reached a corridor with no tourists. No paintings. No signs.
Only stone walls. At the end still stood a door. Derek stopped. "That's odd."
"What?"
"There wasn't supposed to be a door here."
Weinstein regarded it. "Are you sure?"
"I've been here before."
"Why?"
"Long story."
"Fair enough."
Derek opened the heavy door. Inside was a small room. And in the middle of it stood a table.
On the table was a strange clock. Brass. Railway style.
11:03:17
Weinstein went quiet. "The same clock."
"Yes."
"How did it get here?"
Derek shook his head.
"I don't know."
Weinstein approached. There was something beside the silent clock. A sheet of paper.
He picked it up. Three coordinates were written on it.
x = 3,  y = 4,  z = 12
Then:
τ =? 
Weinstein stared. Derek came closer.
"Well?"
Weinstein looked, unmistakably, pleased. "Now we have a problem."
Derek conceded. "Good."
"Why?"
"Because problems are what we investigate."
Weinstein examined the silent clock. Then the coordinates.
Then the empty space where the fourth coordinate should have been.
He responded:
"The three spatial coordinates are specified."
"Yes."
"But proper time isn't."
"No."
"Someone has given us a three-dimensional event."
Derek grimaced. "An event needs four coordinates."
"Exactly."
"So, whoever left this knows that."
"Yes."
Derek inspected the silent clock. "And they're asking us to find the fourth."
Weinstein agreed. "Yes."
Penny's calm voice suddenly came from the doorway.
"Then I suppose we ought to."
They both spun around. Penny stood there. Trevor was behind her, looking rather pleased with himself.
"How did you get here?"
Penny grinned. "Train."
Derek looked at Trevor. "And you?"
"Also train."
Derek allowed. "Right. Next."
Penny glanced at the clock. "No."
"What?"
"That's not the weird part."
She tapped the paper. "The weird part is the date."
Weinstein dropped his eyes. He hadn't noticed it. At the bottom of the page was a date.
Tomorrow. Derek stared at Penny. Then at Trevor.
Then at Weinstein. 
Weinstein's mouth curved slowly. "Now," he opined, "we're getting somewhere."
And above them, in the Louvre, a clock struck twelve. Below them, the brass railway clock struck eleven seventeen. For the first time, Weinstein wondered whether the problem was not that the clocks disagreed about time. It was that reality had decided to play the pot calling the kettle fabulous.
Perhaps they disagreed about which event they belonged to.

Rule: Drop something. Watch the path. Name the geometry only after it embarrasses you.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 6 - Singularities
Chapter 7
The Fourth Coordinate
For several seconds nobody offered anything. The room beneath the Louvre was small, stone-walled and entirely too quiet. There was a table.
There was a clock. There were four people. And there was a piece of paper asking them for a value of τ.
Derek allowed himself a brief, private observation: this was becoming a pattern. A city, a clock, a number that refused to add up. He kept the observation to himself, mostly because Trevor would have agreed, and agreement on that particular point was unlikely to help anyone. What was new, this time, was the rate. In Munich the discrepancy had grown by roughly a seventeenth of a second per hour. Here, beneath the Louvre, it was growing by nearly a full second. Whatever was causing this, it was not slowing down.
Trevor was the first to speak.
"That's proper time."
Weinstein confirmed. "Yes."
Derek gazed at the paper.
"Which means?"
Trevor indicated the three numbers. "Three spatial coordinates have been supplied. The fourth coordinate is missing."
"Correct."
"And they're asking us to calculate it."
"Apparently."
Trevor considered the problem. "Do we know the metric?"
"No."
"The worldline?"
"No."
"The gravitational field?"
"No."
"The initial conditions?"
"No."
Trevor peered at the paper again.
"Then we can't calculate it."
He paused. "Unless that's the point."
"What do you mean?"
Trevor tapped the board. "τ is not a missing when. Or it is. If τ is a timestamp, we're still worshipping a universal now. If τ is an interval along a path, the murder becomes a missing worldline segment — and that I can calculate.
"They were showing us that τ is missing. 
"That's not a question. That's a diagnosis."
Derek studied him for a moment. "That's rather good, Trevor."
Trevor looked pleased, and then faintly suspicious of looking pleased. "Thank you."
Penny, however, had not moved. She was looking at the silent clock. "There's something else."
Derek followed her gaze. "What?"
"Look at the time."
"Precisely."
"Yes."
"That's when the clocks stopped."
Derek confirmed. "And restarted."
"At 12:04."
"Yes."
Penny tapped the paper. "Then perhaps τ isn't asking us for a number."
Trevor faced her. "What else could it ask for?"
"An interval."
Trevor's brow furrowed. "Between what?"
Penny gestured at the mysterious clock. "Two events."
Weinstein permitted himself a small, satisfied sound. "Exactly."
Derek rubbed the back of his neck. "So, the missing coordinate isn't necessarily a timestamp."
"No."
"It's proper time between events."
"Perhaps."
Trevor studied the paper again. "Then we'd still need the worldline."
"Yes."
"We don't have one."
"We have several."
Trevor raised his eyes. "Whose?"
Weinstein indicated the silent clock. "The clock."
Trevor inclined his head. "Right."
Derek picked up the clock. "Then let's start with the obvious."
He turned it over. "How does this thing know when to stop?"
Nobody answered.
He examined the mechanism. There was nothing unusual. No obvious electronics.
No radio receiver. No battery. No hidden switch.
The brass casing was old and scratched. The movement was mechanical. Penny took it from him. "May I?"
"Of course."
She opened the casing. Trevor leaned over. 
The mechanism was intricate. Beautiful, even. 
Tiny gears. Springs. Jewelled bearings. A balance wheel.
An escapement. Penny examined it for several seconds. "There's nothing here."
Derek smiled. "That's becoming a recurring feature."
"I mean literally."
She held the movement towards the light. "There's no mechanism capable of producing the behaviour we've seen."
Trevor considered. "Then the clock isn't doing it."
Penny surveyed him. "What?"
"The clock isn't deciding when to stop."
"Go on."
"It's responding to something."
Derek confirmed. "Exactly."
Weinstein took the clock. "The clock is a physical system."
"Yes."
"It has a worldline."
Weinstein turned the movement over in his palm. "Yes."
"It measures proper time along that worldline."
"Yes."
"So if it stops while everything else continues..."
"Then something about the physical conditions along its worldline has changed."
Trevor turned his attention to him. "Or the clock itself has changed."
"Yes."
"Could it have been accelerated?"
Weinstein allowed. "In principle."
"Enough to create a significant time dilation?"
"Not without enormous acceleration."
"How enormous?"
"Enough that you'd notice."
Derek's expression eased. "That seems useful."
"Yes."
Penny regarded the clock. "But the clock didn't move."
"Not in any ordinary sense."
"So, acceleration isn't the explanation."
"Not ordinary acceleration."
Trevor thought. "Gravity?"
Weinstein granted. "Possibly."
Derek gestured towards the ceiling. "We're beneath the Louvre."
"Yes."
"Could there be something beneath us?"
Penny examined him. "Such as?"
"A very large mass."
Trevor grinned. "How large?"
"Large enough to stop clocks."
"That would be rather noticeable."
"Probably."
Weinstein shook his head.
"Not necessarily."
Everyone quietly eyed him. He continued, "A gravitational field doesn't have to announce itself by dropping buildings on people."
"Fair."
"And gravitational time dilation depends on the metric, not merely on some crude idea of gravitational force."
"Meaning?"
"Two clocks at different gravitational potentials can accumulate different proper times."
Trevor allowed. "But not by forty-seven minutes in an ordinary room."
"Correct."
Derek considered the clock. "Then we're back to the same problem."
"Yes."
He put the old clock down. "Unless..."
Nobody spoke. Derek glanced at the paper. Three spatial coordinates.
A missing proper time. He walked to the wall and put his palm on the plaster. The plaster was warm where no radiator reached. "If the room is the coordinate system," Derek murmured, "then Sophie is already written on a wall we have not opened yet."
His phone buzzed. A voicemail from an hour ago, somehow: Sophie, bored, "Dad, the maths homework says time is a line. It isn't, is it?" He played it twice. He did not reply immediately. Replying felt like choosing a Tuesday.
That evening he called her back. She answered on the fourth ring, unimpressed, as if she had somewhere better to be that involved considerably less physics.
"You're not going to explain the homework thing, are you."
"Not tonight."
"Typical." A pause, muffled, a door closing somewhere on her end. "Also, Ben says hi. Not that I care."
"Who's Ben?"
"Nobody. Forget I said it."
"Consider it forgotten."
"You're so annoying when you do that."
"Do what?"
"Agree with me. It's very suspicious." A pause, quieter. "Are you okay, though? You sound weird."
"I'm fine."
"You're always fine. Also very suspicious."
"I learned it from someone."
A short laugh, despite herself. "Whatever. Don't die or anything."
"I'll do my best."
She hung up before he could decide whether that had been a joke.
Back beneath the Louvre the next morning, Trevor was still waiting for an answer to a question he'd asked the day before.
Trevor grimaced. "Explain."
Derek indicated the floor. "We're somewhere in three-dimensional space."
"Yes."
"Suppose the numbers describe a point."
"Yes."
"Then the missing τ describes which event occurs at that point."
Trevor confirmed. "Yes."
"So, perhaps the paper isn't telling us where to look."
Penny finished: "It's telling us when."
Derek smiled. "Exactly."
Weinstein shook his head. "Not quite."
Derek pivoted. "What?"
"The word 'when' is precisely what we should avoid."
"Why?"
"Because we're trying to determine whether the events are ordered by a fundamental time or merely related geometrically."
Derek conceded. "Fair."
Penny smirked. "So what should we call it?"
Weinstein studied the equation. "Which event."
There was silence. 
Trevor nodded slowly. "That's better."
Weinstein continued, "Suppose the point 
x = 3,  y = 4,  z = 12 
corresponds to a particular location."
"Yes."
"Then τ identifies a particular event along a worldline."
"Yes."
"But if the geometry is unusual, there may be more than one relevant event associated with that spatial location."
Derek stared at him. "More than one?"
"Yes."
"At the same place?"
"Perhaps."
"How?"
"Different times."
Derek smirked. "That's a rather circular answer."
"Only if you assume time is fundamental."
Derek laughed. "Can't argue with that."
Trevor walked over to the wall. He took the piece of paper and copied the numbers. Then he drew three axes.
x,y, z
He marked the point.
"Here."
Derek considered. "Now what?"
Trevor added a fourth axis.
τ
"Here."
He drew a curve.
"The clock's worldline."
Weinstein agreed. "Yes."
Trevor continued, "If the clock stops at one point and restarts at another..."
"Then its worldline has a strange section."
"Quite."
Penny leaned over. "Or we've misunderstood what stopping means."
Trevor stared at her. "What do you mean?"
"If the hands stop but the clock's physical state continues changing..."
Trevor's eyes widened.
"Then the clock's reading has stopped, but the clock hasn't."
"Yes."
Derek studied the movement. "That's interesting."
Trevor allowed. "The clock could be experiencing proper time normally while its display remains fixed."
Weinstein smirked. "That is much more interesting."
Derek turned to Penny.
"Can we test it?"
Penny picked up the silent clock. "Probably."
"How?"
"Compare the mechanism's state before and after."
Trevor confirmed. "Balance wheel frequency."
"Yes."
"Escapement."
"Yes."
"Gravitational potential."
"Yes."
Derek raised an eyebrow. "How long?"
Penny shrugged. "Ten minutes."
Trevor grinned. "We'll need a laboratory."
Penny surveyed the room. "We have one."
Derek took in the room. "This is the Louvre."
"Yes."
"Not CERN."
"No."
"Then where exactly is the laboratory?"
Penny tapped the old clock. "Here."
Derek huffed, not quite a laugh. "Of course."
They spent the next half hour examining the silent clock. There was nothing supernatural about it. That was, in some ways, the most unsettling thing.
Somewhere at the edge of his attention, faint enough that he'd have missed it in a noisier room, Derek had the fleeting, ridiculous impression of being watched by someone very patient and very far away — the way you sometimes sense a person in a doorway before you've turned around. He put it down to the gallery. Old buildings did that. He didn't mention it to anyone, and by the time they left, he'd almost managed to talk himself out of it.
The balance wheel oscillated normally. The spring behaved normally. The escapement behaved normally.
The gears moved. Everything mechanical suddenly appeared perfectly ordinary. And yet the silent clock had stopped.
Weinstein watched the movement. "There's no anomaly."
Trevor conceded. "None."
Derek tapped the clock's face. "Then why did it stop?"
Penny turned the clock over. "It didn't."
Derek frowned. "The hands stopped."
"Yes."
"But the movement didn't."
"Correct."
Trevor snapped his fingers. "That means the hands aren't coupled to the movement in the ordinary way."
"How?"
Trevor examined the mechanism. "There must be an intermediate mechanism."
"Which we can't find."
"Correct."
Weinstein allowed. "Then perhaps it isn't mechanical."
Derek surveyed the clock. "It certainly looks mechanical."
"Appearance isn't evidence."
Penny smirked. "That's something Derek usually maintained."
Derek pressed a hand to his chest. "I say it more elegantly."
"Naturally."
Weinstein turned the silent clock over again. The initials were still there.
He stared at them. Then he took out his notebook. "LGV."
Derek confirmed. "We've seen it before."
"Yes."
"What does it mean?"
"We don't know."
Weinstein wrote the letters. Then, beneath them, he wrote:
LOCAL?
GRAVITATIONAL?
VECTOR?
Penny tapped the page. "That's what Trevor suggested."
Weinstein regarded Trevor. "Did you?"
Trevor conceded. "Yesterday."
"Interesting."
Trevor smiled. "Thank you."
"Local Gravitational Vector."
Derek shook his head.
"No."
"Why?"
"Because a vector requires a direction."
"Yes."
"And we've got no indication that the initials represent one."
"Fair enough."
Penny affirmed: "Could be initials."
"Whose?"
Nobody knew. Weinstein examined the clock. Then at the paper.
Then at the three coordinates. 
He suddenly stood. "Let's go upstairs."
Derek studied him. "Why?"
"Because I want a map."
"Of the Louvre?"
"Yes."
They returned to the main museum. A staff member directed them towards a map of the building. Weinstein examined it.
Then the underground room. Then the coordinates. 
His brow furrowed. "These aren't Louvre coordinates."
Derek leaned closer. "How do you know?"
"Because the scale is wrong."
Trevor studied the map for a long moment, tracing one finger slowly along the river. "Then what are they?"
Weinstein took a pencil. 
He marked three points. "Not ordinary Cartesian coordinates."
"Then?"
"Relative coordinates."
Derek glanced at him. "Relative to what?"
Weinstein drummed his fingers against the map.
Then something in his expression sharpened into interest. "That's the question."
Penny gestured at the silent clock. "Could the clock be the origin?"
Weinstein granted. "Possibly."
Trevor stared at the numbers. "Three, four, twelve."
He paused. "That's a Pythagorean triple."
Derek looked at him.
"What?"
Trevor indicated the numbers. "Three squared plus four squared equals five squared."
Then he added: "Five squared plus twelve squared equals thirteen squared."
Weinstein peered at the numbers. 
Derek grinned. "So?"
Trevor shrugged. "Nothing necessarily."
Penny agreed. "Don't force patterns."
Trevor insisted. "I wasn't."
Derek met his gaze. "You absolutely were."
"Perhaps slightly."
Weinstein watched the coordinates again, tapping the pencil twice against the desk before setting it down. "Three, four, twelve."
He wrote: 13. 
Then: 5. 
Then: 12.
He paused. "Thirteen."
Derek waited.
"What about it?"
"The spatial distance from the origin is thirteen."
"Assuming Euclidean space."
"Yes."
"And?"
Weinstein surveyed the silent clock. "The missing coordinate may be related to the invariant interval."
Derek's expression changed.
"Go on."
Derek drew three marks and refused to give them Greek names. "Timelike, spacelike, or null. Pick the fork that keeps Sophie's doorway causally connected to my corpse — or admit they're different events entirely."
He wrote: 
ds² = dx² + dy² + dz² - c²dt²
Trevor allowed. "Depending on sign convention."
"Yes."
Weinstein continued, "If we know the spatial separation..."
He indicated the coordinates. "... and the interval..."
He looked at the missing τ. "... we can infer the temporal relationship."
Derek conceded. "But we don't know the interval."
"No."
"Then?"
Weinstein allowed himself a smile. "Perhaps we do."
He gestured towards the Louvre. "The clock."
Derek's expression tightened. "The clock gives us proper time. So τ is the proper-time coordinate, and the spatial distance is thirteen. So..."
Weinstein glanced towards the silent clock. "Yes. All of it."
Weinstein stopped. "Nothing."
Derek laughed. "That's a very dramatic pause for nothing."
"We're missing one more piece."
"What?"
"The nature of the interval."
Penny confirmed. "Timelike, spacelike or null."
Weinstein regarded her. "Exactly."
Trevor smiled. "If the interval is timelike, the events can be causally connected."
"Just so."
"If spacelike, they can't."
"Correct."
"If null, they're connected by light."
"Precisely."
Derek examined the silent clock. "Then we need to determine which one this is."
Weinstein allowed. "And that may tell us what the clock is doing."
Derek grinned. "Right, that's that, then."
Trevor watched him. "What?"
"We have a method."
Penny confirmed. "And a destination."
Derek's brow creased. "Destination?"
She indicated the map. "Those coordinates."
Weinstein checked the map again. They corresponded to a location in the museum. A gallery on the upper floor.
Derek's mouth curved. "Right."
They climbed the stairs. The gallery was crowded. Visitors moved between paintings.
Tour guides spoke quietly. Cameras suddenly appeared everywhere.
Derek checked the coordinates. "We're here."
"Where?"
"Somewhere in this room."
Trevor checked the numbers again. "Three metres east."
"From what?"
Trevor's ears reddened. "Right."
Derek rubbed his eyes. "Origin."
"Yes."
"Which is?"
They considered the clock. Then at one another. 
Penny gestured. "The Louvre entrance."
They walked. The three coordinates took them towards a large painting. 
Derek stopped. "This is it."
Weinstein studied the painting. It showed a landscape. A road.
Trees. Mountains. A distant building.
Nothing unusual. Then he noticed the perspective. The vanishing point.
The same feature Derek had noticed earlier. Weinstein stepped closer.
"The coordinate system."
Derek allowed. "Yes."
Trevor glanced at the painting. "The spatial geometry is encoded in the perspective."
"Exactly."
Penny indicated the horizon. "And there."
A tiny ancient clock was visible in the distant building. Weinstein leaned towards the window. 
It showed 12:04.
Derek took the ancient brass clock from his pocket.
Its hands had suddenly moved. 12:04.
Trevor checked his phone. 12:04.
Penny checked hers. 12:04. 
The gallery strange clock showed 12:04. 
For several seconds, nobody spoke.
Then Derek said, low: "We've found it."
Weinstein was already shaking his head before Derek finished. "No. You've found where it's projected. Not where it is."
They turned to him. He gestured at the painting. "We've found the projection of the event."
Derek tapped the frame of the painting. "And the event itself?"
Weinstein returned his attention to the silent clock. "That may be somewhere else."
The painting suddenly changed. Not dramatically. Not with a flash.
Nothing so vulgar. 
The tiny clock in the distant building simply moved. 12:04.
12:05. 
Then the entire scene shifted. The road was different.
