The trees were different. The building was gone. The people in the gallery continued talking.
Nobody else seemed to notice. Derek stared. "Did you see that?"
Penny confirmed. "Yes."
Trevor agreed. "Yes."
Weinstein smirked. "Good."
Derek faced him. "Why are you pleased?"
"Because now we know."
"Know what?"
Weinstein indicated the painting. "The event isn't moving through time."
Derek waited.
"It's changing the geometry."
The brass clock in his hand began ticking backwards. Somewhere beneath Paris, another clock began ticking forwards. Derek did not speak. Somewhere beneath the gallery, he could feel the two clocks disagreeing, the way you feel weather changing before you can name it.
Neither was wrong. 
And, for the first time, Weinstein understood why the question on the paper had not been:
WHEN?
It had been: 
τ =? 
The answer was not a date.
It was a path. A worldline. And somebody had just drawn them one.
Weinstein did not leave the gallery. Everyone else seemed to have moved on. 
Visitors wandered past the painting. A guide explained something about perspective to a group of tourists. Someone took a vivid photograph. A child complained that he was hungry. The universe continued behaving as though nothing particularly remarkable had happened.
Weinstein remained in front of the painting. He was thinking. Derek knew better than to interrupt him.
Penny knew better still. 
Trevor, after several years of experience, had learned that there were two kinds of silence from Weinstein. There was the ordinary silence.
And there was the dangerous silence. This was the second kind. Weinstein didn't move.
Then at the ancient brass clock. 
Then at the three coordinates. Three spatial dimensions.
One temporal coordinate. A geometry. A worldline.
An invariant interval. And curvature. 
He suddenly took out his notebook.
"What have you got?"
Weinstein didn't answer. He drew three axes. Then curved them.
Derek leaned closer. "What is that?"
"Space."
"That's not very flat."
"No."
"Should it be?"
Weinstein smirked. "That's the question."
He drew another curve. Then another. He began to imagine spacetime itself.
Not a rubber sheet. 
Not a picture of a planet sitting in a depression. 
Something more fundamental.
A four-dimensional geometry. The curvature was not necessarily in some larger space. The geometry itself was curved.
An observer moving through it would experience that geometry as reality. 
Weinstein wrote:
CURVATURE
Then underneath:
MORE CURVATURE
Then:
HOW MUCH IS TOO MUCH?
He stopped. 
Derek slowly read it. "That's rather philosophical."
"No."
Weinstein lifted his gaze. "It's physics."
"Fair enough."
Weinstein continued, "If spacetime can be curved..."
"Yes."
"And matter and energy determine that curvature..."
"Yes."
"Then there must be some limit."
Trevor's brow furrowed. "Why?"
Weinstein surveyed him. Trevor waited. Weinstein considered the question. "Perhaps there isn't."
Trevor inclined his head. "Right."
Weinstein continued, "But suppose there is."
The corner of Derek's mouth lifted. "That's rather different."
"Yes."
Weinstein drew a curve that became increasingly steep.
"Imagine curvature increasing."
"Yes."
"More and more."
"Yes."
"At some point our ordinary description becomes inadequate."
Penny confirmed. "Because the equations stop giving sensible physical answers."
"Perhaps."
"Or because an invariant diverges."
"Yes."
"Or because a geodesic can't be continued."
"Yes."
Weinstein studied the drawing. "And that..."
He paused. "... is where we call it a singularity."
Trevor regarded him. "You're defining a singularity as 'too much curvature'?"
Weinstein shook his head.
"No."
He crossed out the phrase.
"A singularity isn't simply a region where spacetime is very curved. It is where the classical description breaks between two events — which is also where a computer might file Murder instead of Pending Geometry."
He wrote:
BREAKDOWN OF THE CLASSICAL DESCRIPTION.
"That's better."
Derek nodded. "Much."
Weinstein looked again at his drawing. "But the intuitive idea remains."
"Which is?"
"If curvature increases without bound, the classical theory can cease to provide a meaningful description."
"Yes."
"And that's precisely the sort of thing we're looking for."
Derek's expression changed.
"Why?"
Weinstein gestured at the ancient brass clock. "Because we have a clock behaving as though it has crossed from one geometry into another — or as though it has stopped measuring proper time and started displaying an event coordinate."
Penny frowned. "Or stopped measuring the same proper time."
"Exactly."
Trevor examined the painting. "So, you're thinking there's an extreme gravitational region somewhere."
"Perhaps."
"Near Paris?"
"No."
"Then where?"
Weinstein smirked. "That's what Derek is going to investigate."
Derek wearily sighed. "Of course I am."
Weinstein appraised him. "You went to the Louvre."
"Yes."
"Why?"
"Because I followed a clue."
"Right."
"You knew that."
"I suspected."
"And now?"
"Now I suspect you found something else."
Derek grinned. "I did."
"What?"
Derek indicated the painting. "Look at the landscape."
Weinstein did.
"The geometry changes."
"Yes."
"Not the painting?"
"No."
"The projection."
"Just so."
Derek took out a photograph. It showed the same painting. But the perspective was subtly different.
The vanishing point had shifted. 
Weinstein studied both images. "The projection has changed."
"Yes."
"Without the underlying objects changing."
"Yes."
Weinstein grimaced. "That's impossible."
Penny immediately insisted: "Don't."
Weinstein laughed. "Fine."
"If the projection changes while the objects remain..."
He stopped. "The coordinate system has changed."
"Yes."
"And if the coordinate system changes..."
"The metric might."
Weinstein confirmed. "Exactly."
Trevor's finger hovered between them. "So, we're not looking at a strange painting."
"No."
"We're looking at a change in the geometry used to describe it."
"Yes."
Trevor thought. "Then perhaps the painting isn't showing us a place."
Weinstein spun to face them. "What is it showing us?"
Trevor indicated it. "An event."
There was silence. Derek didn't quite smile, but it was close. "Good. Moving on."
Penny conceded. "Good."
Weinstein faced Trevor. "You may keep him."
Derek laughed. 
Trevor smiled. "I'll take that as a compliment."
"You should."
Weinstein suddenly turned back to the painting. The idea of a curved event returned to him. An event wasn't merely a point.
The Louvre corridor lengthened while they stood still. A door that had been twelve paces away became fifteen. Trevor counted. Counted again. Refused to announce a theory. The count was the evidence; the theory could wait until it stopped being optional.
The interval mattered. The worldlines mattered. And if the curvature became extreme enough, the familiar description could break down.
Weinstein wrote one word.
SINGULARITY
Derek read it. "That's a rather dramatic word."
"It's precise."
"Is it?"
"In the appropriate mathematical sense."
Derek didn't smile this time. "That's not the word I'd use."
Weinstein continued writing, "Black holes."
"Yes."
"Cosmological singularities."
"Yes."
"Geodesic incompleteness."
"Yes."
Derek leaned back. "You're thinking about the clock."
"Yes."
"Could a clock pass near a singularity?"
Weinstein quietly stared at the brass clock. "Not in any ordinary sense."
"Why?"
"Because a singularity isn't simply a very strong gravitational field that you can safely approach."
"Fair."
"The classical spacetime description itself becomes pathological."
"Meaning?"
"You can't simply ask what the clock does at the singularity. And you especially can't ask what it does if the singularity is only one end of a wormhole."
Derek allowed. "Because the theory has stopped telling us."
"Exactly."
Penny gazed at the clock.
"Then perhaps the clock isn't near one."
Weinstein granted. "Perhaps."
"Perhaps it's telling us that the geometry between two events has become extreme."
"Yes."
Trevor studied the three coordinates. "If τ is the gap, then the gap is what we measure — not what we assume." He glanced at Derek, and the anxious edge came back into his voice. "I don't want Sophie's name anywhere near a number we haven't actually measured yet."
Weinstein spun sharply. "Yes."
Trevor continued, "And if the proper time remains finite while something else becomes infinite..."
"Then we could have a singular behaviour."
"Yes."
Something in Weinstein's expression eased. "Very good."
Trevor brightened. Derek peered at Penny.
"He's becoming dangerous."
"He was always dangerous."
Trevor ignored them.
"So what do we do?"
Weinstein quietly closed his notebook. "We find out where the curvature becomes extreme."
Derek nodded. "How?"
"The same way we found the event."
"The coordinates?"
"Yes."
"And the clock?"
"Yes."
"And the Louvre?"
"Yes."
Derek cast a quick look around.
"Then we've got a problem."
"What?"
"We don't have enough coordinates."
Weinstein smirked. "No."
He tapped the painting. "We have four."
Three numbers had been written on the paper.
He had assumed the fourth coordinate was missing.
It wasn't. A tiny number was visible beneath the ancient brass clock in the painting. It had been almost completely hidden by the frame.
Derek leaned closer. "What's that?"
The number was—
Trevor went very still. "Thirteen."
Penny nodded. "That's the distance."
Weinstein shook his head.
"No."
"Why not?"
"Because we're assuming Euclidean space."
Trevor conceded. "Right."
Weinstein took out his pencil. "If this is spacetime, the invariant interval isn't simply the Euclidean distance."
He wrote:
ds² = dx² + dy² + dz² — c²dt² 
Then stopped. "Unless we're using the opposite sign convention."
Derek grinned. "Does it matter?"
"Not for the physical content."
Weinstein didn't answer right away — he was looking past Derek at the tiny symbol beneath the coordinates, the one neither of them had pointed out yet.
He looked again at the painting. Three.
Four. Twelve. Thirteen.
And beneath them, almost invisible: ∞ Derek stopped smiling.
Penny saw it. Trevor saw it. Weinstein narrowed his eyes. "That's not a coordinate."
"No," Derek said.
"What is it?"
Weinstein watched the symbol. "Infinity."
Trevor's forehead creased. "That would suggest a divergent quantity."
"Yes."
"Curvature?" Trevor tried.
"Possibly."
"Proper time, then."
"Perhaps."
Trevor threw up his hands. "You're just saying words that end in the right sound."
"Also possible."
Derek surveyed him. "Which is it?"
Weinstein didn't answer. He was staring at the painting, and for several seconds nobody dared prompt him. Then satisfaction settled slowly over his face. "Now we have our singularity."
Derek gave Penny a measured look. Penny regarded Trevor. Trevor examined Weinstein.
And somewhere, far beneath the Louvre, an ancient brass clock began ticking. Not forward. Not backwards.
But sideways. At least, that was what the silent clock appeared to think it was doing.
Derek did the arithmetic without quite meaning to. If the discrepancy kept growing at this rate, by the end of the week the clocks would not merely disagree about the time. They would start disagreeing about the day.

Rule: A fourth coordinate is not a decoration. It is where labels pretend to be events.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 7 - Why SR and GR Don't Fight
Chapter 8
The Cold Rain
Wednesday had come and gone. Eleven-oh-three had arrived, been logged, and passed without incident, which Derek found almost more unsettling than if something had actually happened - a threat that simply declines to go off doesn't stop being a threat. It just becomes a longer one.
Derek Gent had been given a difficult assignment. He was to investigate the London Underground on rainy days.
This was not, in itself, unusual. The London Underground was already difficult on most days. On rainy days it became something else entirely.
People arrived wet, irritated and vaguely convinced that the entire transport system had been personally designed to inconvenience them. Trains became late. Platforms became slippery. Umbrellas became weapons. Shoes acquired the consistency of soup. Derek had been instructed to find out whether anything in the Underground, specifically on rainy days, could prevent Special Relativity and General Relativity from both being true at the same time.
He had read the instruction three times. Then he had called Weinstein. "Alfred. You've sent me to investigate the London Underground on rainy days, to determine whether it contains something that makes Special Relativity and General Relativity mutually incompatible. Why?"
Weinstein had been silent for several seconds. "Because time doesn't exist. Or rather — I'm beginning to suspect it's a bookkeeping device. Like accounting."
"That sounds dangerous."
"It is."
"And the Underground?"
"Also dangerous."
Derek had considered this. "Anything else?"
"Rain."
"Right."
Derek did not own a car and had never held a driving licence, a fact he attributed to a childhood theory instructor who had failed him twice on grounds of "excessive philosophical hesitation at junctions," and which he now regarded, on balance, as a mercy — a man who spent his working life arguing about whose version of "now" was correct had no business also being asked, at speed, to agree with other drivers about it. The Underground, whatever its opinion of causality, at least ran on rails that had already made up their mind.
And that was how, at 4:18 on a wet Thursday afternoon, Derek Gent found himself standing on a London Underground platform with a notebook in one pocket, a train ticket in the other, and an increasingly damp left shoe. He looked up at the distant station sign. 
He inspected the rainwater dripping from the entrance. He considered the arriving empty train. Then he flipped open his notebook.
At the top of the page he had written:
INVESTIGATION 1047-B
QUESTION:
Can London Underground infrastructure, under rainy conditions, prevent SR and GR from simultaneously describing reality?
Under that he had written:
SECONDARY QUESTION:
Why?
And underneath that:
THIRD QUESTION:
What the hell does poetry have to do with it?
Derek closed the notebook. A woman standing beside him glanced at the page.
She stared at him. Then at the page. Then back at him.
"Work?"
Derek granted. "Unfortunately."
She smiled sympathetically. "Important?"
"Apparently."
She stepped onto the empty train. Derek followed. The train doors quietly closed.
The carriage moved. Derek sat down. Across from him, a man was reading a newspaper.
The headline concerned the weather. Derek glanced at it, then at the cold rain running down the window.
Then at his notebook. He quietly opened it again. 
Derek's notebook held one line only: 
LOCAL RULES FAIL AT ARM'S LENGTH
He closed it before it could become a textbook.
Nothing about that contradicted ordinary experience. It merely contradicted the assumption that everyone was entitled to the same old clock. 
Rain struck the platform — then paused mid-air, reconsidered, and chose upward. Derek did not say gravity was geometry. He held out a cup. The cup filled from the bottom.
"If the rain can disagree with itself in public," Derek said, "we do not arrest the weather. We ask which description we have been reading as a crime."
Freely falling objects followed geodesics. Clocks followed worldlines and measured proper time along them. Again, nothing inherently contradictory.
Trevor timed the upward drops against his watch and the station clock. The watch advanced. The station clock did not. "Local rules still work in my fist," he explained. "They fail at arm's length. That is the whole argument. Write nothing else down."
Derek stopped writing.
Derek wrote nothing about enemies or limits. The cup had filled from the bottom. That was enough.
So why had Weinstein sent him into the Underground? Derek looked at the wet floor. A drop of water rolled along the rubber mat.
He watched it. It moved. Stopped.
Moved again. Something eased in his face. Perhaps the question wasn't whether SR and GR could both be true.
Perhaps the question was whether the way they described time could be reconciled with the strange behaviour they were observing. The train entered a narrow tunnel. The lights flickered.
Derek eyed his watch. Normal. He checked the empty station strange clock.
Normal. He checked the silent clock on the train. Normal.
Three clocks. Three agreements. He wrote:
Everything disappointingly normal. The train stopped.
A recorded voice announced the empty station.
Derek lifted his gaze. He had been expecting something. He wasn't sure what.
Perhaps the carriage would suddenly become six-dimensional. Perhaps the passengers would all age at different rates. Perhaps a small black hole would appear near the emergency familiar door.
Nothing happened. A man sneezed.
Derek softly sighed.
He went back underground. This time he chose an empty station with a long escalator. He quietly stood still while it carried him downward.
He quietly studied the steps. They moved beneath him. He tracked the handrail.
It moved at a different speed relative to the walls. He surveyed the people. They were moving relative to him.
The entire station was a small laboratory of relative motion. He grinned. "Special Relativity."
A man beside him edged half a step away.
Derek pretended he had said something else.
At the bottom, he walked towards the platform. Then he stopped. There was a puddle.
A large one. It had formed where rainwater had been carried down by passengers. Derek studied it, then the moving escalator, the rails, the tunnel.
"Case question only," Derek said. "Does the rainy Underground make SR and GR incompatible, or only our stitching of frames? Answer that and we know whether to board."
Trains. Platforms. Escalators.
Ventilation systems. Water droplets. Everything moved relative to everything else.
And yet the laws of physics were supposed to work for all of them. SR did not say that only one frame was correct. GR did not say that either.
So what could possibly go wrong? Derek took out his notebook. He wrote:
SR: local inertial frames. Then: GR: curved spacetime.
Then: Question: what happens where the Underground is not locally inertial? He stopped.
That was better. A distant train accelerating away from a station was not an inertial frame. A train braking into one wasn't either.
A passenger standing on the platform was supported by the Earth. The platform was therefore not a freely falling frame. The Earth itself was rotating.
The entire planet was orbiting the Sun. The Sun was moving through the galaxy. The galaxy was moving relative to other galaxies.
There was no shortage of motion. Derek smiled. The universe had an extraordinary sense of humour.
He put the notebook away. Then he heard a quiet voice behind him.
"You're thinking too hard."
He pivoted. A woman was standing there. She was perhaps forty, dressed in a dark coat, holding an umbrella.
Derek slowly regarded her. "Excuse me?"
"You've been staring at that puddle for five minutes."
"Have I?"
"Yes."
"Did you time me?"
"No."
"Then how do you know?"
She smirked. "Because I was staring at you."
Derek considered this. "That's fair."
She indicated his notebook. "Physics?"
"Yes."
"What sort?"
"Relativity."
She considered. "Special or General?"
Derek stared at her. "Both."
"Good."
She grinned. "Then you have a problem."
Derek laughed. "So it appears."
She walked away. Derek watched her disappear into the crowd. He stood still.
Then he opened his notebook. He wrote: Find woman.
Then crossed it out. He wrote: Find out why she knew.
Then crossed that out too. Finally: Probably irrelevant.
He put the notebook away. He was learning. Not everything was a clue.
Sometimes a woman on the Underground simply knew what relativity was. It was London. Anything was possible.
He did not write her down as irrelevant a second time.
The empty train arrived. Derek boarded. He sat down.
And then, for no particular reason, he began thinking about poetry. The thought had been there since Weinstein's phone call. Time didn't exist.
It was merely a way of preventing everything from happening at once. The phrase had been absurd. But the more Derek thought about it, the less absurd it became.
Consider a poem. The words had an order. A line followed another line.
A word followed another word. Change the order and the poem changed. Not merely its meaning.
Its rhythm. Its structure. Its sound.
Derek imagined reading a poem in which every word was spoken simultaneously. He immediately understood the problem. It would sound like a fart.
A very literary fart, perhaps. But a fart nonetheless. He smiled.
He wrote:
TIME PREVENTS POETRY FROM SOUNDING LIKE A FART.
Then added:
Possibly its greatest achievement. 
A man sitting beside him looked over. 
Derek closed the notebook.
The train entered another deep tunnel. The lights flickered again. And this time something happened.
Derek's watch stopped dead on his wrist — no warning tick, just gone. Around him, phones blinked out one by one, screens going dark like held breath. Derek's pulse was still going; he could feel it in his throat. The train did not slow down.
A woman coughed. Someone turned a page. 
The mysterious train rocked.
Everything was happening. But the clocks had stopped. Derek allowed himself a smile. "Right."
He looked around. 
Nobody else seemed particularly alarmed. 
A man checked his phone again.
Derek looked through the window. The tunnel walls moved past.
He stood. 
He walked towards the end of the carriage. 
The train continued.
Then he noticed something else. The cold rain. He could see it through a small window near the door.
Rainwater was running down the outside of the mysterious train. But the drops suddenly appeared to be moving differently. Not backwards.
Not forwards. Simply differently. Derek stared. One of them, for a fraction of a second, seemed to be going both ways at once.
The distant train emerged from the deep tunnel. 
Daylight flooded the carriage. 
The clocks jumped. 11:18. 
Everyone continued reading. Nobody noticed.
Derek sat down. 
He opened his notebook and wrote:
Ancient clock stopped for approximately?
He looked at the second hand. It had not advanced. But the train had travelled several kilometres.
Derek thought. The clock had measured proper time. The mysterious train had followed its worldline.
The passengers had followed theirs. 
The Earth had followed its own. 
All of them had continued through physical processes.
Yet the displayed time had stopped. That was not necessarily a failure of time. It was a failure — or transformation — of the relationship between a physical clock and the events around it.
Derek grinned. "Weinstein."
He called. No answer. He called again.
Still nothing. Then a message appeared.
WEINSTEIN: WHAT?
The reply came immediately.
WEINSTEIN: WHERE?
LONDON UNDERGROUND.
A pause.
WEINSTEIN: RAIN?
Rain still hammered the window.
YES.
The reply:
WEINSTEIN: GOOD.
Derek stared. He typed:
GOOD?
The response:
YES
NOW FIND OUT WHAT THE WATER IS DOING
A small official notice was taped to a pillar:
RAIN IS NOT TO BE CONSIDERED EVIDENCE WITHOUT FORM 17B-W
Beneath it, in different handwriting:
Especially if it falls sideways.
Derek studied the notice for a moment. Someone in the Bureau had clearly been here before him, or after him, or in a reference frame that made the distinction unhelpful. He photographed the notice, filed it under Pending Geometry, and only then looked back at the rain.
Derek glanced at the cold rain. Then at the window. Then at the track.
Then at the puddles on the lonely platform as the train approached the next station. He understood. Water was not merely water.
It was matter moving through spacetime. 
Each droplet followed a trajectory. 
Each trajectory depended on its initial conditions and the geometry.
If there was something strange about the geometry, the water would reveal it. 
The Underground had thousands of natural test particles. Rain provided millions.
Derek got off the train. 
He stood beneath the gloomy station canopy. 
Rain poured beyond it.
He quietly watched. 
Droplets fell. Most followed ordinary paths.
But some struck the pavement. Some hit umbrellas. Some bounced.
Some ran along walls. 
Some disappeared into drains. 
Every one of them had a history.
A worldline. 
Derek suddenly understood what Weinstein had been getting at. 
The apple. The clock. The train. The cold rain.
They were all the same problem. 
Different physical systems. Different worldlines.
Different measurements. One geometry. 
He opened his notebook.
He underlined one line in the notebook and stopped: if local rules fail at arm's length, stop stitching frames with hope.
Then:
GR determines how those local frames fit together globally. 
Then: 
Therefore, if the Underground appears to produce a contradiction, the contradiction must be in our description, not in the theories.
He stopped. 
That was it. 
That was what Weinstein wanted him to find.
Unless... Derek stared at the cold rain.
Unless there was a region where the assumptions needed to connect the local frames failed.
A singularity. A boundary. Something pathological.
Something where the geometry itself ceased to behave normally. 
He thought of the Louvre. 
The painting.
The changing perspective. 
The missing coordinate. The infinity.
Then 12:04.
Then the train. Then the cold rain. 
Something close to a smile crossed his face.
Perhaps the Underground wasn't causing the contradiction. 
Perhaps it was revealing it. 
He called Weinstein.
This time Weinstein answered, "Yes?"
"I think I understand."
"Fine."
"SR and GR don't conflict."
"No."
"SR works locally."
"Yes."
Derek continued, "GR tells us how those local inertial frames fit into curved spacetime."
"Yes."
"And if we find something that appears to make them incompatible..."
"We've misunderstood something."
"Precisely."
There was a pause. 
Derek let a breath out slowly. "Good. So SR and GR aren't fighting."
"No."
"There's just one problem."
"What?"
"I think the Underground has a singularity. Or something that wants to be a wormhole and hasn't decided yet — and Sophie takes this line to school on Thursdays."
Weinstein looked pained. "Please do not invent new topology without filing a form."
Derek considered this. "Pending Geometry. It's a guess, not a diagnosis — but it's the first one that doesn't require a killer."
Weinstein did not look reassured. Pending Geometry was already three drawers deep.
Mrs Marsh appeared at the end of the platform as if she had always been part of the architecture. "If that is a hole, it is not one of mine. Pending Geometry is not a joke drawer either. Stamp it properly, or stop inventing Murder."
She indicated a small official sticker on a nearby pillar. "No unauthorised singularities." 
Derek found himself almost respecting the sticker. It had the finality of a stamped refusal.
A tabloid, later that week, ran a headline asking whether a footbridge near Vauxhall might be an actual Einstein-Rosen bridge, on the grounds that a local resident had crossed it and sworn he'd arrived twenty minutes earlier than physically possible, which turned out on investigation to mean his watch had stopped. Weinstein, faxed a copy by someone at the Bureau who thought he'd enjoy it, did not enjoy it. He observed that holding a traversable wormhole open long enough for anyone to cross it — alien, human, or otherwise — required exotic matter with negative energy density, a substance London Transport had not yet applied to a footbridge over the Thames, chiefly because nobody had worked out how to fund it and it would still need a health and safety inspection. "If aliens are coming," he pronounced, "they are not coming via Vauxhall. Standards would not permit it."
On the windowsill sat a white mug. It was the same mug that had been in the office that morning, except that it was now empty when it should have been half full, and the tea stain inside had moved three centimetres to the left. 
No one commented. 
In the Bureau, objects that quietly disagreed with themselves were considered ordinary until they filed a complaint.
Mr Pendleton appeared as if summoned by the word "unauthorised." 
He made a note on his clipboard. "Future minutes, open doors, and children in photographs are still not my form. When they become evidence, find Marsh. When they become Murder, find someone else. Localhost is definitely not my form."
He looked faintly satisfied, as though he had just closed a stable door on a horse that had never existed.
Weinstein was silent. 
Then: "Where?"
Derek lowered his gaze to the platform. At the far end, beyond the lights, a distant train was approaching. He gazed at the rain.
Then he peered at the dark tunnel. "That's what I'm trying to find out."
Weinstein remained silent. Derek waited.
Finally Weinstein stated: "Don't go into the tunnel."
Derek smiled. "Why?"
"Because I know you."
"That's not a scientific reason."
"No."
"Then?"
"Because the last time you claimed 'I'm trying to find out', you ended up in the basement of the Louvre."
Derek considered this. "Fair."
He hung up. The train arrived. The doors opened.
Derek stepped inside. 
The doors quietly closed. The mysterious train suddenly moved into the tunnel. And the cold rain continued falling outside.
Derek watched the droplets through the window. One of them ran upward. He stared.
Then another. Derek grinned. "Well."
He took out his notebook. 
And wrote:
THE COLD RAIN IS FALLING UP
He thought about it.
Then added:
OR THE TRAIN IS FALLING DOWN
He paused. Then, beneath that:
OR NEITHER
The mysterious train entered darkness. 
And somewhere ahead, something in the geometry of London appeared to be waiting for him.

Rule: If the rain fills a cup from the bottom, stop stitching frames with hope.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 8 - The Door
Chapter 9
The Open-Door Principle
There was a rule at the Bureau. It was not written down.
It did not appear in the employee handbook.
There was, in fact, no employee handbook, largely because Derek considered employee handbooks "a form of administrative poetry."
Nevertheless, everyone knew the rule. Open door: virtuality. It had been comedy until Sophie appeared in a doorway that was not theirs. Now an open door was a missing-person report that had not admitted it yet. Derek stopped treating empty offices as jokes.
Penny considered this. "That's the sort of rule that sounds sensible until it starts wearing a hat."
There was no point knocking, calling, or standing in the doorway shouting someone's name. An open old office door meant the person was missing in a way paperwork refused to admit — in another room, in London, in Munich, nobody knew. The Bureau had learned not to worry.
If the door was quietly closed, however, the situation was entirely different.
Then the person was in there.
Obviously. They were either working hard... or shagging, equally hard.
There was no third possibility worth discussing. 
Derek had first formulated the principle after Penny had disappeared for three hours. 
He had found her office door open.
He had checked the kitchen. Nothing. 
He had checked the laboratory. Nothing. 
He had checked the basement. Nothing.
He had checked the train timetable. Nothing. 
Eventually he had returned to her office.
The door was still open.
Trevor had suddenly appeared beside him.
"Where's Penny?"
"Virtuality."
Trevor dipped his head. "Right."
Trevor's phone buzzed. He glanced at the screen, exhaled slowly, and put it face-down on the desk without answering. Only one contact got that reaction.
Derek raised an eyebrow. "Tabitha?"
"Tabitha."
"You never pick up."
"I always pick up. Eventually. On my terms, at a time of my choosing, several hours after hers." The phone buzzed again. Trevor did not look at it a second time. "She has an unerring instinct for calling exactly when I've said something clever, purely so she can ask me to repeat it more slowly."
"She sounds delightful."
"She is extremely delightful. That is precisely the problem." Trevor pocketed the phone, unanswered, with the air of a man postponing a reckoning he fully expected to lose. "If she ever actually turns up somewhere, Derek, brace yourself. She has never once entered a room the way anyone else does."
Trevor drove an ancient Mini Cooper the colour of a bruise, which he maintained personally, argued with regularly, and refused every offer to replace. He was fond of pointing out, entirely unprompted, that it had covered more miles than the Bureau had solved cases, and was measurably more reliable at both. Derek, who could not drive at all, considered this less an argument for the car than an argument against Trevor's other hobbies. Weinstein once calculated, purely for his own amusement, the speed at which the Mini would need to travel for its famously narrow chassis to contract a further inch in the direction of motion — a speed the Mini had never approached, would never approach, and which Trevor nonetheless referred to afterwards, with some pride, as "well within range."
They went back to work. 
Three hours later Penny had emerged from Derek's office carrying a notebook. 
Derek had watched her. "Where were you?"
"Working."
"With the door closed?"
"Yes."
Derek allowed. "There we are, then."
Penny's mouth had curved. Trevor had surveyed the two of them. 
Then at the quietly closed old door.
Then at Derek. 
Then at Penny. He had wisely remained silent.
The rule had subsequently become part of Bureau culture. 
And on Friday morning, Derek's office door was open. Penny's was quietly closed.
Trevor's was open. Weinstein's was quietly closed. Nobody found this unusual.
Trevor walked down the narrow corridor carrying three cups of tea. 
He stopped outside Derek's office. The door was open.
He looked inside. Empty. 
He confirmed. "Virtuality."
He continued. 
Penny's heavy door was closed. 
Trevor knocked.
"Come in."
He opened it. 
Penny was sitting at her desk. She was reading a report.
Trevor put down her tea. "Working hard?"
"Yes."
"Good."
He left. 
Penny waited until the door closed. Then smiled.
The Bureau was a peculiar place. 
Derek returned twenty minutes later. He walked through the front door carrying a wet umbrella.
Penny glanced up. "Where have you been?"
"Underground. Second look at Thursday's platform."
"Did you find anything?"
"Yes."
"What?"
"Rain."
Penny waited.
"Anything else?"
"Yes."
"What?"
"It was falling upward. Same as Thursday."
Penny conceded. "There. Sorted."
Derek took off his coat. "Exactly."
He put the umbrella in the stand. "Except I don't know whether the rain was falling upward."
"Or?"
"The train was falling downward."
Penny considered. "Or?"
"Neither."
"Good."
Derek fixed her with a look. "You're getting rather good at this."
"I've had excellent training."
The corner of his mouth lifted. 
Then he noticed her familiar door. It was open.
Derek frowned. "Where's Trevor?"
"Virtuality."
"Of course."
He looked towards Trevor's office. His familiar door was open too. 
Derek considered. "Both at once?"
"Apparently."
"That's unusual."
"Not really."
Derek considered this. "Point taken."
He walked to the whiteboard. 
There were equations everywhere.
Lorentz transformations.
Metric tensors.
A sketch of a geodesic.
A badly drawn simple apple.
An empty train.
A cloud.
And, in large letters:
THE RAIN IS NOT OBLIGED TO FALL DOWN.
Derek stared at it. "Did you write this?"
"No."
"Trevor?"
"Probably."
Derek regarded the open office door. "Virtuality."
Derek picked up a marker. 
Underneath the sentence he wrote:
NOR IS THE TRAIN OBLIGED TO KNOW WHERE IT IS GOING
He put down the marker. Then the telephone rang.
Penny answered, "Relativistic Investigation Bureau."
A pause. 
She examined Derek. "It's for you."
"Who?"
"Herbert."
Derek took the phone. "Matkowski."
"Derek."
Derek straightened. "Yes?"
"We've found something."
Derek grinned. "Where?"
"London."
"Good. "
"What do you mean, good?"
"That's where we are."
"Yes."
"Then you've saved us a journey."
There was silence.
"We're in Munich."
Derek paused. "Ah."
Penny considered him. "Virtuality?"
Derek allowed. "Apparently."
Matkowski continued, "The problem concerns your clocks."
Derek became serious.
"What about them?"
"We've compared the London readings with the Munich readings."
"And?"
"They don't disagree."
Derek grimaced. "That's good."
"No."
"Why not?"
"Because they should."
Derek sat down slowly, the chair creaking in a way that felt, this week, unnecessarily editorial. "Explain."
"The clocks are following different worldlines."
"Yes."
"Different gravitational potentials."
"Yes."
"Different motions."
"Yes."
"So, their proper times should differ."
"Yes."
"But they don't."
Derek frowned. "Then they're synchronised."
"Exactly."
"That's not a problem."
"It is if they have never been synchronised."
Derek stopped. Penny considered him. "Go on."
Matkowski commented:
"Someone has synchronised the clocks."
"When?"
"We don't know."
"How?"
"We don't know."
"Why?"
"We don't know."
Derek didn't smile. "We know almost nothing. Someone synchronised clocks across two cities without touching either of them, and hid a file with all our names in it under tomorrow's date."
Matkowski was quiet for a second too long. "Yes. That's the part I called about."
Derek glanced at Penny. 
She wore the particular smile she reserved for moments when she already knew how the conversation would end. "I'll take it."
Matkowski continued, "There is something else."
"Of course."
"We found a reference to the Louvre."
Derek's expression changed.
"Where?"
"In a file that should not exist."
"What's in it?"
"Your name."
Derek asked, "Anything else?"
"Alfred's."
"Penny?"
"Yes."
"Trevor?"
"Yes."
Derek paused. "And Julius Barbarian?"
There was silence.
"Yes."
Derek stared at Penny. "That's interesting."
"Very."
Matkowski noted: "The file is dated tomorrow."
Derek leaned back. "Excellent. Case closed."
Matkowski sounded irritated.
"Would you stop saying that?"
"Sorry."
"No, you're not."
"Not particularly."
There was another pause.
Then Matkowski observed:
"You should examine your own office."
Derek looked towards the narrow corridor, where the light seemed, if anything, slightly reluctant to reach the far end. "My office?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
"Because the file indicates something is there."
"What?"
"We don't know."
Derek stood. He walked towards his familiar office. The door was open.
He stopped. Penny followed him. "What?"
"Nothing."
The office was empty. The old battered desk was empty.
The chair was empty. The computer was off. Derek smiled. "Virtuality."
Penny confirmed. "Obviously." 
Then she fixed him with a look. "You're not the brightest bulb in the toolbox today."
Derek stepped inside. On the desk was a single sheet of paper. He picked it up.
It contained one sentence.
IF THE FAMILIAR DOOR IS OPEN, DON'T GO IN
Trevor suddenly appeared behind them. "What's that?"
Derek took in the room. "Where did you come from?"
Trevor gestured at his own office. "The door was closed."
Derek conceded. "Ah."
Trevor gazed at the paper. "What does it say?"
Derek handed it to him. 
Trevor read it. 
"That's confusing."
Penny agreed. "Yes."
Trevor thought. "Perhaps we're not supposed to be here."
Derek's mouth twitched. "Excellent."
A pleased smile appeared on Trevor's face. "I've solved it."
"Not quite."
"Oh."
Derek eyed the paper again. 
Then at the door. 
The ancient clock stopped. All three watched.
The second hand froze. 
The computer switched on. A message quietly appeared:
DOOR STATUS: OPEN
Then:
OCCUPANT STATUS: VIRTUAL
Derek frowned. "Who's the occupant?"
The computer answered.
DEREK GENT
Derek went rigid. So did Trevor. So did Penny.
Derek dropped his eyes at himself.
"I'm here."
Penny confirmed. "Yes."
Trevor considered this. "Apparently you're both here."
"Both?"
Trevor indicated the screen. "Virtual Derek."
Then at Derek. "Actual Derek."
Derek didn't answer at first. Two of him. One typing at a keyboard that wasn't there.
The screen changed.
DOOR CLOSING IN 10 SECONDS
They stepped backwards. The heavy door slammed shut. 
The clocks restarted. 11:18.
The computer went dark. 
Derek opened the door again. Empty. He closed it. "Me, apparently."
He knocked. From inside came Derek's own quiet voice. "Come in."
Trevor's mouth fell open. Penny looked delighted — then not delighted at all.
Derek carefully opened the door. His office was still empty. Except for himself.
His own voice came from somewhere inside. "You're late."
"Late for what?"
The distant voice said: "For the event you're already in."
Trevor whispered, "There are two of you. That's impossible."
"No," Penny corrected slowly. "There are two descriptions. That is not the same crime — unless we've been reading three crimes that are one."
Derek did not look away from the empty air. "Say it properly."
Penny's voice had gone flat. "Weinstein saw you dead tomorrow."
Trevor shook his head, then forced himself to nod. "Blood is observational. Mathematics says the observations can disagree without any observer being wrong — if we assumed a universal 'now' that doesn't exist. The absurdity was the evidence."
"Blood in an image generated by a machine that answers to LOCALHOST," Derek said. "I am beginning to dislike that word."
The voice from the empty office — his voice — continued, almost kindly. "If you keep investigating it as an ordinary killing, you will complete the death coordinate. Act in the wrong frame and eleven-oh-three stops being paperwork."
Derek felt Penny's hand find his sleeve. Not dramatic. Necessary. He was thinking of Sophie in the doorway — and of the ugly possibility that preventing his death might delete whatever future had placed her there, or worse, leave her alone in a room that should not exist.
"Then the question isn't who murdered me," he reflected. "It's what I'm willing to sacrifice to change a future that already has Sophie's face in it — including, if the geometry is spiteful, the version of her that remembers I tried."
Penny finished it for him, quieter. "Whether eleven-oh-three becomes proper time — or stays a label. Death, Departure, Arrival. One structure."
Trevor stared at the closed door as if causality itself had personally insulted him. "I preferred the version with a perpetrator."
Derek said nothing to the empty room.
The distant voice answered anyway: "Not remotely."
Outside, somewhere that was not quite the corridor, a train whistle sounded once — the sound of an arrival insisting it was also a departure.

Rule: An open door is a missing-person report that has not admitted it yet.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 9 - Relativistic Travel
Chapter 10
The Relativistic Ride
The first important observation was that Derek Gent had apparently not quite murdered himself yet. Reassuring. Less so: passengers stepping off a train whose departure board still read NOT YET. The conductor apologised for the delay as if tomorrow were a timetable error. For a moment the platform felt bigger than London.
A woman with a tomorrow-dated newspaper asked him the time. His watch said 11:18. Hers said 12:04. She thanked him as if both were correct, and walked into a Tuesday that had not finished arriving. Derek did not explain. He followed wet footprints that aimed the wrong way — and did not look at the school-bag strap visible at the edge of the photograph in his pocket.
It was also becoming slightly embarrassing. 
The Bureau had announced his death with considerable confidence. 
There had been a photograph, a timestamp, a location, and enough circumstantial evidence to make most people reasonably uncomfortable about their own mortality — and about their children's.
Yet Derek remained alive.
He found this increasingly difficult to reconcile with the paperwork. He was standing in his office, staring at the quietly closed door. 
From inside came his own voice. "You're late."
Penny stood behind him. 
Trevor stood beside her.
Nobody spoke.
Finally Derek added: "I don't think I've murdered myself yet."
Penny allowed. "Apparently not."
Trevor inspected the photograph. "That's encouraging."
"Is it?"
"Well, if you had already done it, we'd have a rather awkward problem."
"We already have an awkward problem."
"True."
Derek returned to the photograph. The dead man was unmistakably him. Same face.
Same clothes. Same regrettable tendency to look as though he had just been interrupted while thinking about something complicated. Yet he was still standing.
Alive. Annoyed. And, at least for the moment, in possession of all the usual body parts.
Derek put the photograph down. "Perhaps the photograph is from the future."
Penny conceded. "Obviously."
"Or another event."
"Yes."
"Or another worldline."
"Possibly."
Trevor's brow furrowed. "Or another Derek."
"There's only one of me."
Trevor granted. "That's what makes it difficult."
Derek let out a long breath. "Thank you."
"You're welcome."
The office door remained quietly closed. Which meant Derek was in there. Obviously. "Or we're being mildly inconvenient again."
Penny quietly stared at the door. "Although technically..."
Derek raised a hand. "No."
"What?"
"Let's not complicate the door."
"Fair."
Rain was falling. It had been raining for most of the day.
This was not particularly surprising. It was London. Rain in London was not weather so much as a local administrative policy.
Derek watched the drops run down the glass. "Rain."
Trevor looked at him.
"Yes."
"We keep coming back to rain."
"Yes."
"Why?"
"Because it keeps falling."
Derek smirked. "Not always."
Trevor peered at him. Derek indicated the window. Trevor followed his finger.
The drops were falling normally. Downward. He grimaced. "That's disappointing."
"Give it time."
Penny picked up the silent brass clock. It was reading 11:18. She turned it over. "Still no explanation."
Derek allowed. "Then perhaps we're asking the wrong question."
"Which one?"
"Why the clock stops."
Trevor gave him a measured look.
"What should we ask?"
"Why does it start again?"
Penny grinned. "That's better."
"Yes."
"And what is that?"
Derek watched the photograph. "The train."
Penny confirmed. "Platform seven."
"Exactly."
"Then why are we still here?"
Derek smiled. "We're not."
Penny understood immediately.
"We're taking the Underground."
Trevor surveyed her. "In the rain?"
"Yes."
"Why?"
Derek reached for his coat without quite deciding to. Some choices simply finish making themselves.
"Because Weinstein wants a relativistic ride."
Trevor considered. "That sounds expensive."
"It's the Underground."
"Ah."
They left the Bureau. 
Derek locked the front door. Then stopped.
He looked at the office doors. 
Penny's was closed. 
Trevor's was open.
Derek's was quietly closed. He allowed himself a satisfied breath. "Good."
Penny regarded him. "What?"
"Everyone's where they ought to be."
Trevor glanced over his shoulder. "My office is empty."
Derek shrugged. "Virtuality."
"Obviously."
They walked into the cold rain. 
The Underground was busy. It was always busy.
But on a rainy afternoon it was something else. 
Wet coats. Wet shoes. Wet umbrellas. Wet people. 
The smell of rainwater, electricity and human resignation.
Derek descended the stairs. 
He examined the crowds. "Excellent."
Trevor eyed him. "What?"
"Test particles."
Penny grinned. "Thousands of them."
"Quite."
Displays froze at 11:17. The train kept moving. Derek checked his pulse against the frozen board. Pulse continued. Board did not. He put every useful word away unused.
Trevor confirmed. "Every train as well."
"Yes."
"The station."
"Yes."
"The Earth."
"Yes."
"The rain."
"Yes."
Penny declared: "And you."
Derek considered her. "Yes."
"Don't forget yourself."
"I wasn't planning to."
They reached the platform. The train arrived. 
Derek glanced at his watch. 11:12. 
He stared at the station clock. 11:12.
He gazed at the train display. 11:12. "Five minutes."
"Yes."
Penny smirked. "What are we expecting?"
Derek considered this for a moment longer than the question deserved. "Nothing."
"Good."
"That's usually when something happens."
They boarded. The doors quietly closed. The train moved.
Derek sat beside the window. 
Trevor sat opposite. 
Penny stood holding the rail.
The train accelerated. 
"Now."
Trevor found his eyes. "Now what?"
"Acceleration."
"Ah."
At 11:17 the displays froze. The train did not. Derek chose, silently: believe the freeze, or believe the motion — or admit both were true and stop talking.
Trevor slowly eyed the tunnel.
"So, there's no contradiction."
"Not ordinarily."
"And this train isn't going to disprove either theory."
"Not ordinarily."
Trevor didn't finish the sentence. He was watching the tunnel wall, counting something under his breath.
The mysterious train entered a station. 
Brakes applied. Derek felt the deceleration.
He watched the passengers. Everyone leaned slightly forward. 
The mysterious train stopped.
Then started again. 
Derek checked his watch. 11:14.
Normal.
"Again."
Trevor surveyed him. "Again what?"
"Acceleration."
Trevor sighed. "Yes."
They continued. 11:15. 11:16.
Derek counted the seconds under his breath.
The second hand moved smoothly.
The second hand stopped. The train continued moving.
Derek allowed himself a smile. "Right."
Derek examined Penny. "All stopped."
She confirmed. "Yes."
Trevor looked around the room. Nobody else seemed concerned. 
The train continued through the tunnel.
Derek stood. He walked towards the window. 
Outside, the tunnel wall slid past.
Then he noticed something. 
The mysterious train was moving.
The silent clock was not. He smiled. "That's interesting."
Trevor joined him. "What's interesting?"
"The train is still changing position."
"Obviously."
"Yet its clock isn't advancing."
"Yes."
"So, the train's spatial coordinates are changing while its displayed temporal coordinate isn't."
Trevor allowed. "That doesn't mean its proper time has stopped."
"Correct."
Penny came closer. "The clock could simply be failing."
"Except all of them stopped."
"Yes."
"At exactly the same reading."
Rain kept hammering the window.
"Yes."
"And we've already seen this elsewhere."
"Munich."
"The Louvre."
"Yes."
Trevor frowned. "So, the anomaly isn't in the clocks."
Derek grinned. "That's what I'm thinking."
"What is it in?"
"The relationship between the clocks and the events."
Penny conceded. "Good."
Derek inspected the rain through the window. 
The tunnel ended. Rain streaked across the glass.
He watched one droplet. It slid downward. Then sideways. Then upward. 
Derek blinked. "Did you see that?"
"Yes."
"Yes."
Nobody said Next. They watched the droplet finish being wrong.
Trevor's brow creased. "What?"
The rain did not apologise.
The mysterious train entered another tunnel. The droplet disappeared. 
The strange train continued.
He felt the acceleration. 
Outside the glass, stopped clocks kept time with a moving tunnel. That was argument enough.
The idea was ridiculous. 
Derek wrote in his notebook:
TIME MAY BE THE THING THAT STOPS POETRY FROM BEING A FART
Trevor leaned towards the notebook. "What are you writing?"
"Physics."
Trevor read it. "That's not physics."
"It will be."
She smiled. "Actually, he's got a point."
Trevor stared at her. "How?"
"A poem requires ordering."
"Yes."
"Ordering requires distinguishing one event from another."
"Yes."
"And our experience of that ordering is what we call temporal succession."
Trevor conceded slowly. "That's fair."
Derek looked pleased for a moment, then didn't. "That's a theory about poetry. It isn't a theory about why the clocks keep doing this."
The train slowed. Derek glanced at the station sign. They had arrived at the next station.
Derek stepped onto the platform. 
Water was running along the edge.
He crouched. 
A stream of rainwater moved towards the drain. 
Derek watched.
The water reached the drain. Then stopped. 
The mysterious train doors closed. The train departed. The water remained. 
Derek stood.
"What?"
"The water."
"What about it?"
"It stopped."
"Because the train left?"
"No."
"Then why?"
Derek shook his head.
"I don't know."
Trevor studied the water. "It could be blocked. Debris, maybe, or the gradient."
"Maybe."
Trevor grinned. "Blocked drain, then."
Derek didn't laugh. "The water stopped at the exact second every clock in the station did. Go find me a drain that does that."
He took in the tunnel, slowly. Then the station clock. Then the water.
"Let's see what happens at 12:04."
Penny's expression changed.
"The restart."
"Exactly."
They waited. The cold rain continued. 
The empty station remained busy.
Trains arrived. Trains departed. People came and went.
Nobody paid any attention to the three people standing beside a puddle. 
At 12:03:30 nothing happened. 
At 12:03:50 nothing. 
At 12:03:59: The water moved. 
The strange station clock did the same. Derek's watch did the same. 
Penny's phone jumped.
Trevor's phone jumped. 
The train arriving at the cold platform seemed to hesitate for the briefest fraction of a second. 
Then everything continued.
Derek stared at the silent clock. 12:04. He peered at the puddle.
The water was flowing normally. He eyed Penny.
"Well."
Penny watched the water resume its ordinary crawl towards the drain. "They weren't broken."
"No," Derek said. "They were waiting."
"For what?"
Derek lowered his gaze to the tunnel. "For the next event."
A distant train approached. Its headlights suddenly appeared in the darkness. 
Then he noticed the destination display.
The gloomy London Terminus. Cold Platform 7.
Derek's face changed. "That's our train."
"We're underground."
"Yes."
"The train is coming from the wrong direction."
"Yes."
Penny studied the silent clock. 12:04. 
Something eased in Derek's face. "Of course."
The train stopped. The doors opened. Inside, the carriage was empty.
Completely empty. No passengers. No driver.
Then Derek stepped aboard. Penny grabbed his arm. "Derek."
He surveyed her. "Yes?"
"The door is open."
Derek smiled. "Yes."
"So, whoever's in there..."
"Has vanished into virtuality."
"Obviously."
"Then we'd better find out where they've gone."
He stepped inside. The doors slowly closed behind him. The strange train began to move.
And somewhere ahead, beneath London, something was waiting at Platform Seven. 
It would remain there until 12:04.
And, according to Derek's watch, it was already tomorrow. 
The Flight Derek fell asleep on the empty train.
This was unfortunate. Not because sleeping on the Underground was particularly dangerous, although it was generally considered unwise to trust one's neck with the suspension system of a London carriage. 
It was unfortunate because Derek had a dream.
In the dream, he was on an aeroplane. 
He was already late. This was especially irritating because Derek had been absolutely certain he had left with plenty of time.
His flight had already departed.
"But I was here."
The airport appeared to have no sympathy. He ran. He reached the gate.
The gate was quietly closed.
The attendant regarded him. "Sorry."
"How long ago?"
"About forty minutes."
"Impossible."
She shrugged. "It happens."
He inspected the airport clock. 12:04.
His brow furrowed. "Ah."
"Different airline," the attendant said, before he could ask. "Ryanair uses whatever time gets you to the gate."
She didn't smile when she said it. Derek noticed that, even asleep.
"And please put your seat in the upright position."
Derek dropped his eyes. He was suddenly sitting in the aircraft. A very attractive flight attendant was leaning over him.
Her mouth curved. "Your seat, sir."
Derek glanced down. His seat was reclining. "Yes."
"Upright, please."
Derek adjusted it. She nodded approvingly. "Much better."
Then she walked away. Derek watched her go. 
He looked at the aircraft strange clock. 12:04.
He paused. "Well, that's not good."
The captain's voice came over the speaker: cruising altitude, nothing else. Derek looked out at the Earth below and, for once, didn't think about proper time at all. He closed his eyes.
When he opened them, the aircraft was empty.
The flight attendant was gone. The pilot was gone. The passengers were gone.
The plane was still flying. 
The Earth was still below. Then he noticed something.
The clouds were moving backwards. Derek stood. He walked towards the cockpit.
The door was open. Obviously, the pilot had vanished into virtuality.
Nobody. The instrument panel was running. The plane continued flying.
Derek's eyes moved from the controls to the clock, then to the empty cockpit.
"Right, that's that, then."
The aircraft suddenly descended. Derek grabbed the nearest seat. The flight attendant appeared beside him.
"Sir?"
"Yes?"
"Your seat."
"What about it?"
"Upright."
Derek lowered his gaze. His seat was reclining again. He adjusted it.
She grinned. "Thank you."
Then she disappeared. Derek took in the empty aircraft — no crew, no passengers, engines still running themselves — and understood, with the flat certainty of dreams, that nobody was flying the plane and it did not matter, because it had already landed. It just hadn't told anyone yet.
Derek woke with his seat upright and two clocks disagreeing on his tray table.
The ticket said arrived. His watch disagreed. Both were stamped.
He smiled without naming why.
Then he examined the airline ticket.
It announced:
ARRIVAL: YESTERDAY
"Right."
The distant plane landed. The doors opened. Derek stepped out.
He was back in the London Underground. He woke with a start. The mysterious train was still moving.
Penny was sitting opposite him. Trevor was asleep beside the window. 
Penny studied Derek. "You were talking."
Derek rubbed his eyes.
"What did I say?"
She grinned. "You made a remark about an airline."
He looked out the window. Cold rain streaked across the glass. Then he noticed that his seat was reclined.
Penny glanced at him. "What?"
Derek slowly pushed the seat upright.
"Nothing."
Penny smiled. "Lovely. Next?"
"Not quite."
He took out his notebook. Under the previous entry he wrote:
NOTE: Never book a relativistic flight with Ryanair.
"If this carriage is a twin paradox," Penny said, "I want the receipt for the missing years. I will not accept a delay certificate."
He closed the notebook without adding the joke he'd have made yesterday. Somewhere under London, a clock was still waiting for 12:04, and this time he didn't feel like laughing about it.
The distant train continued into the darkness. And somewhere ahead, the clock was still waiting for 12:04.

Rule: Believe the freeze and the motion if both are stamped. Explain later.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 10 - The Cucumber
Chapter 11
The Ordinary Cucumber Problem
Penny had a theory. It was not a particularly complicated theory.
Nor, for once, did it involve a lecture. She needed an ordinary cucumber — a control that could become Exhibit B if the Bureau kept filing children under Murder. She was standing in an organic fruit market in London while a man in a green apron praised soil. Penny bought the cucumber because it would not argue back. Sophie would. Sophie already had, in a photograph.
Penny picked one up. "Naturally?"
"Without synthetic pesticides."
"Right."
She examined it. "Are cucumbers fruits?"
The man hesitated. "Botanically, yes."
Penny confirmed. "Excellent."
"But culinarily — "
"Don't."
He stopped. Penny put the cucumber in her basket. She had no particular interest in the botanical classification of cucumbers.
She had an interest in what they could be used for. And at the moment, she needed one for an experiment. Nothing sexual.
Nothing particularly mysterious. Just a cucumber. "Anything else?"
"No."
"Tomatoes?"
"No."
"Courgettes?"
"No."
"Organic aubergines?"
"No."
He sighed. "Just the cucumber."
"Very well."
Penny paid. She left. It was raining.
Of course it was. London had apparently decided that weather was unnecessary and rain was sufficient. She walked back towards the Bureau with the cucumber under her coat.
She crossed the street. A bus went past. Water sprayed from its wheels.
She watched the droplets scatter. She grinned. Everything had a frame.
She tucked the cucumber under her coat like contraband — evidence first, and if the Bureau asked awkward questions later, personal use was a perfectly respectable second description. If two descriptions could share one object, Case 1047 could share one event. If not, they were raising Sophie on a superstition.
Perhaps especially cucumbers. She reached the Investigation Bureau. Trevor was sitting at the familiar front desk.
He looked at the cucumber. Then at Penny. Then at the cucumber again.
"What is that?"
"A cucumber."
"I can see that."
"Then why ask?"
Trevor granted. "True."
He peered at it again.
"What are you doing with it?"
"Experiment."
"What sort?"
"Relativistic."
Trevor conceded. "Of course."
Penny walked past him. He followed. "Is it dangerous?"
"No."
"Explosive?"
"No."
"Quantum?"
"No."
"Gravitational?"
"Possibly."
Trevor stopped. "With a cucumber? Instruments admit they are instruments. Vegetables become dangerous when they start proving things. Especially when Sophie is in the file."
Penny faced him again. "You'd be surprised what can be gravitational."
Trevor considered that. "Can't fault the logic."
They entered the laboratory. Derek was there. His familiar office door was quietly closed.
Obviously. Trevor gestured towards it. "He's in there."
"Working?"
"Probably."
Penny smirked. "Or shagging."
Trevor pinched the bridge of his nose. "Please don't."
"Why?"
"Because I know what you're going to say."
Penny smiled. "Then you have excellent predictive powers."
She placed the ordinary cucumber on the laboratory table. Trevor eyed it. 
Derek's voice came through the quietly closed door. "What are you two doing?"
Penny called back, "Science."
"Good."
A pause. "Carry on."
The door remained closed. Trevor considered this. "He's working hard."
"Obviously."
Penny picked up the cucumber. "Right."
"What exactly are you testing?"
Penny watched the cucumber. Then at the silent clock. Then at the equipment.
"Whether an ordinary object can help us detect the strange change in the local frame that Derek encountered on the Underground."
Trevor's expression changed.
"Ah."
"Exactly."
She placed the ordinary cucumber on a small platform. The platform was mounted on a sensitive motion sensor. Trevor leaned over. "You're using it as a test mass."
"Yes."
"Why a cucumber?"
Penny surveyed him. "Because it's convenient."
"Why not a metal sphere?"
"Because we've already tested those."
"And?"
"Nothing."
"And cucumbers?"
"We haven't tested one."
Trevor allowed. "That's one less variable, then."
Penny grinned. "Precisely."
She switched on the apparatus. The cold platform began to move. The cucumber rolled slightly.
Then stopped. Trevor watched the graph. A smooth curve suddenly appeared on the monitor. Trevor watched it a beat too long, waiting for it to behave exactly as it should, which was somehow worse than if it hadn't.
Then — A tiny discontinuity. Trevor's forehead creased. "That's odd."
Penny confirmed. "Very."
The platform returned to its starting position. She reset the experiment. Again.
The same thing happened. Smooth acceleration. Tiny discontinuity.
Smooth acceleration. Trevor stared at the screen. "Same position."
"Yes."
"Same acceleration?"
"Almost exactly."
"Same discontinuity?"
"Yes."
He studied the cucumber. "Perhaps cucumbers are strange."
Penny smiled. "That's what I'm hoping."
They replaced the cucumber with a wooden block. They ran the experiment. The discontinuity remained.
They replaced the block with a brass sphere. Same result. Then a glass sphere.
Same result. Then a bag of sugar. Same result.
Trevor regarded the collection of objects. "The cucumber is innocent."
Penny confirmed. "Completely."
"Poor thing."
Penny picked it up. "You can go back to being a fruit."
She put it beside the apparatus. Trevor examined the graph. "It's not the test object."
"No."
"It's not the apparatus."
"Apparently not."
"It's not the acceleration."
"Not ordinary acceleration."
Trevor watched her. "What does that mean?"
Penny sat down. "Imagine you're in a train."
"All right."
"The train begins accelerating."
"Yes."
"You feel yourself pushed backwards."
"Yes."
"But if you're inside a sufficiently small room, you could describe the effect as though there were a gravitational field pointing backwards."
Trevor nodded at the graph and did not name the principle. "Change observer. See what survives."
"Just so."
"Locally, acceleration and gravity can be indistinguishable."
"Yes."
"But only locally."
"Right."
"Because a real gravitational field can produce tidal effects."
Penny grinned. "Exactly."
Trevor considered the graph again. "So, we're looking for something that isn't simply acceleration."
"Yes."
"Something that changes from one location to another."
"Yes."
"Tidal effects."
"Possibly." Penny stood. She moved the sensor platform a metre to the left. They ran the test.
The discontinuity occurred. She moved it a metre to the right. Again.
She moved it upward. Again.
Trevor stared. "Everywhere."
"Yes."
"Then it's not a local effect."
Penny shook her head. "Not necessarily."
"Why?"
"Because our laboratory is tiny compared with the scale of the geometry we're investigating."
Trevor allowed. "So, the curvature could be approximately uniform across the room."
"Exactly."
"Then we'd need a larger baseline."
Penny smirked. "Now you're thinking."
Trevor glanced at the ceiling. "The Underground."
"Yes."
"The whole train."
Penny's smile widened. "Yes."
"Perhaps the whole station."
"Yes."
"The whole city?"
"Could be."
Trevor whistled low. "That would make the experiment rather inconvenient."
Penny confirmed. "That's why we have Derek."
Trevor nodded at Derek's closed office door. "He's in there."
"Yes."
"Working hard."
"Obviously."
"Or — "
Penny aimed a finger at him. "Don't."
Penny walked to the whiteboard. She drew one jagged discontinuity and nothing else. "Invariant or label? Measure first. If it survives a change of observer, keep it. If not, bin it. And if Sophie is standing inside the jump, we do not get to call the jump philosophical."
Then a series of small local frames around it.
"Imagine the train moving through space and time."
Trevor continued. "At any particular point along its worldline, we can construct a local inertial frame."
"Yes." Penny didn't look up from the whiteboard.
"Inside that sufficiently small region, Special Relativity works." Trevor was pacing now, warming to it.
"Yes."
"But as we move farther along the train — or farther through space — the local frames don't necessarily line up globally."
Penny capped her marker. "Because of curvature."
"Exactly."
She drew a second frame. Then a third. The axes were slightly rotated.
Trevor stared at the axes. "So the fight was never between two theories. It was between our stitching and the rain."
Penny went still for a second, the way she did when something actually landed. "Yes."
"SR describes each local frame."
"Yes."
"GR describes the geometry relating those frames."
Penny finally looked up. "Precisely. Write that down before you lose it."
Trevor considered. "Then what are we actually looking for?"
Penny tapped the tiny discontinuity in the graph. "That."
"Why?"
"Because something has happened to the relationship between our local frame and the physical system we're measuring."
Trevor frowned. "Could it be a coordinate effect?"
"Yes."
"Meaning nothing physical has happened?"
"Possibly, and don't sound so hopeful about it."
"Then why does the apparatus detect it?"
"Because coordinate effects can change what different observers assign to an event."
Trevor agreed, slower this time. "But measurable quantities must remain invariant."
"Correct."
Penny indicated the graph. "So, the next question is whether that discontinuity survives a change of coordinates."
Trevor's mouth curved. "That's clever."
"Thank you."
"How do we test it?"
"We change observers."
Trevor stared at her. "How?"
Penny gestured at the door. "Derek."
Trevor allowed. "Of course."
He went towards the door. Then stopped. It was still closed.
He knocked. Nothing. He knocked again.
Still nothing. "Working hard."
"Evidently."
Trevor opened the door. Derek was sitting at his desk. "What?"
"We need you."
"For what?"
"An experiment."
Derek looked at the ordinary cucumber, then at Penny, harder than either of them deserved.
Then back at the cucumber.
"No."
Penny laughed. "It's not about the cucumber."
"Good."
"It's about the reference frame."
"Better."
She showed him the graph. Derek studied it. "When does the discontinuity occur?"
"At the same position every time."
"Relative to the apparatus?"
"Yes."
"Not the room?"
"No."
"The room itself?"
"We haven't tested that."
Derek confirmed. "Then that's what we need."
He stood. Penny grinned. "You're coming?"
"Yes."
Trevor's eyebrows rose.
"Just like that?"
Derek shrugged. "It's interesting."
They went outside. The cold rain had become heavier. Derek looked at the street, the laboratory, the ordinary cucumber.
"Bring it."
Penny held it up. "Seriously?"
"Absolutely."
"Why?"
Derek smiled. "If we're going to do relativistic physics in London, we may as well have a proper control."
Trevor inspected the cucumber. "What's the control?"
"The cucumber."
"Why?"
"Because everyone expects us to lose our minds."
Trevor didn't argue with that.
They placed the apparatus on a trolley. Then pushed it into the street. The cold rain struck the sensor.
Water ran down the equipment. Penny covered it. Derek watched the traffic.
Buses. Cars. Bicycles.
Pedestrians. Umbrellas. Everything moving.
Everything with its own worldline. "Perfect."
Penny surveyed him. "Why?"
"Because now we've got millions of test particles."
Trevor grinned. "Rain."
"Yes."
"Again."
"Again."
They started the apparatus. The trolley moved down the pavement. The graph suddenly appeared.
Smooth. Then — The discontinuity.
Derek stopped. "Where?"
Penny indicated the spot. "Exactly here."
Derek cast a quick look around. They were beside a distant station entrance. He checked the map. "Which line?"
"Central."
Derek studied the entrance. 
He went still. Penny noticed. "What?"
The ordinary cucumber rolled slightly on the trolley. Then stopped.
Derek grinned. "The cucumber has found the reference frame."
Trevor stared. "That's not possible."
"No."
"Then what?"
Derek dropped his eyes into the Underground entrance.
"I think we're looking at the same event."
"Yes."
"The clocks stop."
"Yes."
"The rain behaves strangely."
"Yes."
"And now our test mass detects a discontinuity."
"Yes."
Trevor examined the cucumber. "So, the cucumber isn't the cause."
"No."
"It's a witness."
Derek looked, briefly, rather pleased with himself. "Exactly."
Penny inspected the Underground entrance. "Then perhaps we should go down."
Derek inclined his head.
Trevor exhaled through his nose. "Again?"
Derek turned his attention to him. "Did you have another appointment?"
"No."
"Good."
They descended. The cold rain continued falling above them. The cucumber came too.
Derek quietly glanced at the silent clock. It stopped.
The train arrived. The doors opened. For one second, Derek could see through the carriage.
There was nobody inside — except at the far end, sitting calmly by the window, was Derek Gent. Derek stared at himself.
The other Derek tipped his head back. Their eyes met. The other Derek smiled.
"Right," Derek murmured, mostly to himself. "New case. Working title: me."
Derek did not smile back.
Then he raised an ordinary cucumber. Derek looked at Penny. Penny turned to Trevor.
Trevor peered at the ordinary cucumber in Penny's hand. Then back at the mysterious train.
"Well," he remarked.
"That's rather settled."
Derek shook his head.
"No."
He stepped towards the carriage.
"That isn't settled at all."
Mrs Marsh, later, declined to inventory the cucumber. "It is also lunch if you fail to return it," she said. "I will not have dual-use veg left on the blotter."

Rule: One object. Two descriptions. Neither is a lie until you file it as Murder.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 11 - The Hologram
Chapter 12
The Hologram Problem
Derek was still staring at himself. The other Derek was still sitting on the mysterious train. The train was still sitting in the distant station.
The empty station clock still read 11:17. The cucumber was still in Penny's hand. Trevor was still trying very hard not to say anything stupid.
This was proving difficult. The other Derek lifted the ordinary cucumber again. Derek raised an eyebrow. "Why has he got a cucumber?"
Penny regarded him carefully. "Why have you got a cucumber?"
"I haven't."
She held hers up. "No. You have."
Derek glanced down. He was holding a cucumber. He stared at it, then Penny, then the other Derek. For a moment the joke didn't arrive on schedule.
"Ah."
Trevor sighed. "That clears everything up."
The other Derek grinned. Then the train doors closed. The train departed.
Derek ran after it.
"Wait!"
The empty train disappeared into the deep tunnel. Derek stopped. He watched the empty track.
Then at the cucumber in his hand.
"Right."
Penny walked over. "You've got a cucumber."
"Apparently."
"Where did you get it?"
"I don't know."
"You were holding it."
"I know."
"Then you got it somewhere."
"That's annoyingly logical."
Trevor surveyed the dark tunnel. "Perhaps we should ask whether the other Derek is real."
Derek met his gaze. "Excellent question."
Penny considered. "How would we know?"
Trevor thought. "If he can interact with things..."
"He can."
"If he has a worldline..."
"Apparently."
"If he has proper time..."
"Unknown."
"If he can eat a cucumber..."
Derek regarded him. "Why would that prove anything?"
Trevor shrugged. "It would at least be satisfying."
Penny laughed. Derek examined the cucumber. Then at the dark tunnel.
He suddenly smiled. "Maybe we've been asking the wrong question."
Penny confirmed. "Again?"
"Yes."
"What is it this time?"
"Not whether the other Derek is real."
"Then?"
"Whether reality itself is real in the way we think it is."
Trevor grimaced. "That's rather broad."
"Yes."
"How broad?"
Derek inspected the ceiling. "Potentially four-dimensional."
Penny grinned. "Or more."
Derek allowed. "Or fewer."
Trevor looked sharply concerned. "Fewer?"
"Don't worry."
"That's not reassuring."
Derek began walking.
"Come on."
"Where?"
"Back to the Bureau."
"Why?"
"Because I have an idea."
Penny followed. Trevor followed Penny. The cucumber came too.
The idea was not Derek's.
Back at the Bureau he put the cucumber on the desk beside Case 1047 and said nothing for ten seconds. Other-Derek had held one too. Matching props. Matching timestamps. Trevor opened his mouth to theorise. Penny gestured at the vegetable. "Evidence first."
Trevor reached past a glass Klein bottle — bought at a maths conference, and never quite forgiven for its inability to hold tea — and picked up the cucumber instead. "Isn't this basically my bottle? No inside, no outside?"
"No," Derek said. "Yours is a joke about topology. This is a joke about description. Don't let the glassware confuse you."
Trevor wearily sat down. "That's not your idea."
"I know."
"Who proposed it?"
"Several people contributed to the development."
"Fine."
"People who dislike comfortable volumes," Derek remarked.
He set the cucumber on a sheet of paper and traced only its outline. "Describe the inside using only the edge."
Trevor grimaced. "That's cheating."
"That's the point."
"It's backwards."
"Exactly."
Derek shaded the outline. "Bigger box, more edge — not necessarily more story inside, if the universe is stingy that way."
Penny looked at the outline. "So the edge knows the middle."
"Or pretends to."
"Like a hologram?"
"Don't marry the word."
Penny sighed. "There goes my giant cosmic cinema screen."
"Good."
Trevor waited. "So what survives the cheat?"
Derek tapped the cucumber. "This. Until it doesn't."
"Yes."
Derek stepped back.
The board was now covered.
He considered the ordinary cucumber. A stopped clock and a train that only sometimes existed felt less like jokes and more like cousins.
Trevor softly sighed. "What?"
Penny nodded once. No speech.
Trevor weighed it. Same mass as before. Same length — until he faced the window, where the reflection held a cucumber that did not match. He closed the blinds. "I am not explaining that."
Derek slid the case file next to the cucumber. One object. Two descriptions. 
He wrote 
PENDING GEOMETRY 
on a sticky note and stuck it to both.
"Exactly."
Derek smiled. "Well, that's settled."
Penny regarded him carefully. "Not quite."
Derek paused. "Why?"
"Because we still have the cucumber."
He looked down. The cucumber was sitting on the table. Derek stared at it. "Yes."
Penny picked it up. "If spacetime is emergent..."
"Yes."
"And the cucumber is made of atoms..."
"Yes."
"And the atoms are described quantum mechanically..."
Derek rubbed the back of his neck. "Yes."
"And the geometry in which those atoms exist might itself be emergent..."
"Yes."
"Then the cucumber is an emergent object in an emergent spacetime."
Derek smirked. "That sounds unnecessarily complicated."
Penny shrugged. "It's a cucumber."
Trevor agreed. "That simplifies things."
Derek laughed. "Does it?"
"Yes."
"How?"
"We know it exists."
"Do we?"
Trevor held up the cucumber. "Yes."
"How?"
Trevor considered this. "It's here."
Derek grinned. "Excellent epistemology."
Penny took the ordinary cucumber from him. "Perhaps we should test it."
Derek looked suspicious. "How?"
"Measure it."
"With what?"
"A scale."
"Fine."
She weighed it. Trevor recorded the result. Penny measured its length.
Then circumference. Then temperature. Then mass distribution.
"What are we proving?"
"Nothing."
"Good."
"We're establishing that the cucumber has observable properties — Trevor's sacred facts — while still allowing Derek's second description."
"Yes."
Penny turned the photograph face-down and pressed the sticky note flat. She did not explain.
Derek confirmed. "Yes."
"So, the cucumber can be described in more than one way."
"Like spacetime."
"Just so."
Trevor stared at the paired notes. "Then the cucumber is Exhibit B, Marsh's form is Exhibit A, and LOCALHOST is the clerk who filed a child under the wrong noun. I preferred when vegetables were lunch."
Derek let a trace of amusement through. "Of course."
"Really?"
"In principle."
Trevor surveyed it. "That's extraordinary."
Derek continued. "The information describing the physical system can have different representations."
Penny smiled. "Just don't tell the cucumber."
"Why?"
"It may become arrogant."
Somewhere, he suspected, a different representation of the same eleven minutes was already causing trouble.

Rule: Describe the inside using only the edge — then ask what survived the cheat.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 12 - Not Actually a Multiverse
Chapter 13
Everything Everywhere All at Once
Penny had decided that Derek was becoming unbearable. Not because he was wrong. That would have been easier. It was because he was usually right, and there was something deeply irritating about a man who could spend an afternoon discussing curved spacetime, singularities, and the holographic principle and then, when Penny pointed out an obvious practical difficulty, simply say "Yes," as though the universe had just been waiting for him to catch up. So Penny went to the cinema. Alone.
What was unusual was the film she chose: Everything Everywhere All at Once. She settled into her seat with popcorn and sat upright. "Blimey." Jamie Lee Curtis had survived Halloween after all, and had apparently decided that the next sensible career move was to become an IRS employee tormenting bewildered families across multiple universes. Penny decided the question the film was allowed to answer for the case wasn't "are there many worlds" but "which observables must agree — and which future of Sophie are we accidentally voting for when we pick a description?"
It wasn't trying to answer that. But the underlying idea was strangely familiar — different histories, different outcomes, a universe in which one thing happened and another in which it didn't. She thought about Derek, the clock, the distant train that existed in one description of reality and not in another.
