If spacetime itself could be emergent, what exactly did it mean for there to be one universe?
The film's multiverse wasn't the same thing as the multiverse physicists sometimes discuss, but it was useful as an intuition pump. Suppose reality could be described many different ways; suppose other histories existed in some mathematical sense. Then what was fundamental — the particular history, the collection of histories, or the mathematical structure connecting them? Penny thought about holography: if a higher-dimensional gravitational description could be equivalent to a lower-dimensional quantum description, then two apparently different descriptions could still be one underlying physics. "Now we're getting somewhere," she murmured. On screen, Jamie Lee Curtis did something completely unreasonable. "Twelve franchises," Penny observed, "and she still hasn't died for good." A woman sitting several seats away glanced at her. Penny lowered her voice. "This is actually rather relevant." The woman shuffled discreetly further away.
Something about the film's constant world-hopping bothered her, and it wasn't the physics — it was the logic. If every possible version of a person existed, what distinguished one from another? Their history. Their state. Their relationships. Their information. Investigate descriptions, not clocks — clocks were how the Bureau had got lost. A clock had a state. An ordinary cucumber had a state. A person had a state. A train had a state. Perhaps the geometry of spacetime itself emerged from relationships between quantum states. She texted Alfred: Holography: information on boundary -> bulk geometry? — then read it back and deleted it, since it had sounded profound for exactly four seconds before revealing itself as nonsense.
That was also, she realised, the flaw in the film's own multiverse: it needed every branch to stay crisp and separately visible, universes stacked like drawers you could simply open the wrong one of. Real physics was ruder than that. A superposition — a cucumber described by two states at once, not literally sitting in two places simultaneously — didn't survive contact with its surroundings for longer than an embarrassingly short time; the moment it brushed against air, light, a countertop, or Trevor's opinions, the different possibilities smeared their interference out across the environment and stopped talking to each other, a process physicists called decoherence. It wasn't that the other branches stopped existing mathematically. It was that once a cucumber became entangled with everything around it, asking which branch you were in stopped being a question with an answer worth having. That, Penny suspected, was also why nobody had ever caught the Bureau's brass clock mid-superposition, dead and standing at once, in a photograph that held still long enough to argue with. By the time light left it and reached a camera, the argument was already over.
The characters weren't merely travelling through space; they were switching between descriptions of reality, and the important thing wasn't that they moved between physical universes but that the information defining the character changed — different memories, different relationships, different histories, same person.
Penny thought about worldlines. A worldline was a history through spacetime, but if spacetime itself was emergent, perhaps worldlines were emergent too — which would mean Derek wasn't fundamentally a line through spacetime, but a particular physical state embedded in a network of relationships, with his apparent history just one way of describing it.
"That explains the other Derek." Then, frowning: "No, it doesn't." If Derek was a state rather than a line, nothing in the mathematics said there could only be one of him. Two identical states, two separate histories, no contradiction anywhere in the equations. That was the trouble with elegant explanations — they had an alarming tendency to explain slightly too much.
The multiverse stopped being merely funny and became a question: if there were many mathematically possible descriptions of reality, what had to remain unchanged between them? The answer had to involve observables — physics couldn't depend on which coordinates, or which mathematical language, someone chose. The measurable predictions had to agree.
That was already familiar from relativity: a Lorentz transformation changed the coordinates assigned to events, not the invariant interval. General Relativity wore the same lesson in a heavier coat — the geometry could bend, gravity itself could be curvature rather than a force, and the physics still didn't care which description you found frightening. Fear was a coordinate too. Whether Sophie was safe did not change with the angle you looked at it from.
Perhaps holographic duality was a more radical version of the same lesson — and the murder file was merely the Bureau's least elegant dual description, one description with gravity and curved spacetime, another with quantum fields and none, both encoding the same physics. "Bloody hell," Penny said, to nobody in particular.
There was a harder version of the same thought, and she made herself finish it rather than flinch. If every possible history was, in some sense, equally real mathematically, then perhaps the correct picture wasn't a multiverse of separate branching worlds at all, but a single four-dimensional block in which Tuesday and next Tuesday were simply two addresses in the same building — both permanently there, neither one waiting its turn. Not many futures. One geometry, viewed one slice at a time by someone stuck experiencing it in order. If that was right, the file marked ARRIVAL: 12:04 wasn't a prediction. It was a postcode. She did not enjoy that thought and declined, for now, to text it to Derek.
She typed: "A change of stationery is not a change of daughter." She deleted it.
Then she remembered the cucumber. She'd left it at the office.
Derek texted: WHERE ARE YOU?
Cinema.
WHAT ARE YOU WATCHING?
Everything Everywhere All at Once.
WHY?
Because you keep talking about holography and I wanted to see what the Americans think it means.
AND?
Jamie Lee Curtis survived Halloween.
THAT'S YOUR PHYSICS CONCLUSION?
Penny typed: My physics conclusion is that if different descriptions can encode the same underlying reality, SR and GR don't need to be fundamental to be true.
NOT BAD.
Then, a moment later: ALSO, JAMIE LEE CURTIS DID NOT "SURVIVE HALLOWEEN" IN THE PHYSICS SENSE.
You know what I meant.
I don't.
Americans.
That settles it.
She put the phone away, laughing, and then noticed a character on screen holding a cucumber. Not identical to hers. Of course it wasn't; that would have been ridiculous. Still, she photographed the screen and sent it to Derek.
LOOK.
I'M LOOKING. THAT'S A CUCUMBER.
So?
Nothing.
Yet.
She put the phone away for good this time, and thought about the Bureau, the clocks, the cold rain, the train, the other Derek. Perhaps they had been approaching the problem backwards all along — assuming something strange was happening inside spacetime, when the strange thing might be the relationship between different descriptions of it. What if the clocks weren't moving incorrectly, only belonging to different descriptions? What if the train wasn't travelling through a strange geometry — what if the train was the strange geometry? She wrote one last message: Don't investigate the clocks. Investigate the descriptions. Maybe the universe doesn't have one story. Maybe it has several descriptions that all have to agree on what can actually happen.
Derek replied: Now you're talking.
Penny smiled, put the phone away, and let the rest of the film wash over her with the specific satisfaction of someone who had just done more useful physics than the man who'd driven her out of the office to do it.
Rule: If everything happens everywhere, measure what must still agree.
-> Lesson for this chapter: 13 - Curved Corridors and Tides
Chapter 14
The Station Wasn't Quite There
The air had the particular cold of somewhere that had never been heated because no one had ever officially arrived to complain about it. A strip-light flickered two floors up, keeping to its own private clock — on, off, on — out of time with the others. It smelled, faintly and wrongly, of hot brake dust and cold stone at once — a smell that belonged to two different kinds of weather that had never met anywhere else. The first thing Derek noticed was that the gloomy station had twelve doors. The second thing he noticed was that it had thirteen. The third thing he noticed was that Trevor was counting them.
"How many?"
"Twelve."
Derek indicated it. "Thirteen."
Trevor counted again.
"Twelve."
Penny counted them herself. "Thirteen."
Trevor rubbed his eyes. "Twelve."
Derek allowed. "Good."
Trevor's forehead creased sharply. "Good?"
"At least we're all seeing the same disagreement."
They were standing in a strange corridor beneath London. It looked like an ordinary Underground corridor. This was, naturally, suspicious.
The tiles were ordinary. The fluorescent lights were ordinary. The smell was ordinary.
Even the damp patch on the wall was reassuringly ordinary. But the narrow corridor was wrong. Not dramatically wrong.
That would have been easier. Dramatically wrong things announce themselves. They have the decency to be obvious.
This narrow corridor was merely wrong enough to make a sensible person uncomfortable. Derek walked back to the entrance and eyed the sign. 
There was no station name. Only:
PLATFORM 7
Penny watched it. "We're already on Platform Seven."
Derek conceded. "Apparently."
Trevor looked behind them. There was no Platform Seven. There was only the narrow corridor.
"Perhaps it's a service corridor."
Derek considered this. "Then why does it have a platform number?"
Trevor shrugged. "British Rail?"
"It's the Underground."
"Even worse."
Penny smirked. "Glad that's cleared up."
Derek ignored them. He took a tape measure from his coat. 
Trevor surveyed him. "You carry a tape measure?"
"I investigate things."
"You carry one everywhere?"
"Not everywhere."
"Where don't you carry it?"
Derek thought. "The shower."
Penny studied him. "Why not?"
Derek stared. "Because I have limits."
They measured the strange corridor. Forty-seven metres.
Derek wrote it down. Then he walked from one end to the other. Forty-seven metres.
He walked back. Forty-seven metres. 
Penny did the same. Forty-seven metres. 
Trevor did the same. Forty-seven metres.
Derek confirmed. "Good."
Trevor regarded him. "What's good about forty-seven metres?"
"Nothing."
"Then why — "
"Because it's the same."
Trevor wearily sighed. "Of course it's the same. Everything's the same until it isn't."
Derek examined the doors. "Now let's count them again."
They did. 
Twelve. Then thirteen. Then twelve. Then thirteen. 
Penny folded her arms. "Perhaps we're counting different things."
Derek grinned. "Excellent."
He indicated the first door. "Describe it."
"Brown."
"Number?"
"One."
"Next?"
"Two."
"Next?"
"Three."
They continued. Every door was ordinary. Every number was ordinary.
Every distance between them was ordinary. And yet, somehow, the corridor contained either twelve or thirteen doors depending on who was doing the counting. Derek took out a notebook. "Trevor."
"Yes?"
"Walk to the end."
Trevor did. "How many?"
"Twelve."
"Penny?"
"It's not rocket surgery." 
Trevor fixed him with a look. "That is not reassuring."
"Derek?"
He counted.
"Twelve."
Penny smirked. "So, it's me."
Trevor appraised her. "You're the problem."
"It would seem so."
Derek shook his head.
"No."
They gave him a measured look.
Trevor counted twelve doors. Blinked. Counted thirteen. Penny refused to count. Derek put his palm on a door that had not been there a breath earlier; it was warm, like a cheek. Nobody said curvature. The corridor had already made the argument.
Trevor frowned. "A what?"
"Something every observer must agree on."
Penny agreed. "Like the spacetime interval."
"Exactly."
Trevor glanced at the doors. "So, door-counting isn't invariant."
"Not if the corridor isn't behaving like ordinary Euclidean space."
Trevor looked suspicious. "Are you saying we've found curved space?"
"Perhaps."
"Underground?"
Derek kept walking. "Yes."
"In London?"
"Yes."
Trevor considered this. "That seems unnecessarily ambitious."
Derek smiled. "It's London."
"Fair."
They slowly moved farther down the narrow corridor. Derek stopped beside one of the doors. "Open it."
Trevor did. There was a small room inside. 
Two chairs. A table. Two clocks. Nothing else.
Derek entered. Penny followed. 
Trevor remained in the doorway. "What?"
One: 11:17. The other: 12:04.
Trevor stared silently. "Again?"
Derek confirmed. "Again."
Penny stepped inside.
"Are they broken?"
Derek checked them. "No."
"Different mechanisms?"
"No."
"Different batteries?"
"No."
He compared them carefully. Identical. Same make.
Same model. Same calibration. Same temperature.
Same reference frequency. Same everything. 
Except the time.
Trevor gazed at the two clocks. "They're less than two metres apart."
"Yes."
"And they're disagreeing by forty-seven minutes."
"Yes."
Trevor considered this. "That's not good."
"No."
"Could one be in a different gravitational field?"
Derek allowed himself a smile. "Good question."
He took out an instrument. They measured the local gravitational field. Nothing unusual.
They measured again. Nothing. Derek moved the 12:04 clock three metres down the corridor.
The difference changed. 
Trevor's eyebrows rose.
"Did you see that?"
"Yes."
Derek moved it back. The difference shrank. 
Penny peered at him. "That's gravitational."
"Something gravitational."
"But the field is nowhere near strong enough."
"Correct."
"So?"
Derek eyed the two clocks. "The important thing may not be the strength of the gravitational field."
Penny confirmed. "The variation."
"Exactly."
"Explain."
Derek leaned against the table. "Imagine two people falling towards Earth."
"All right."
"They are both accelerating towards the centre of Earth."
"Yes."
"But the person slightly to the left is falling in a slightly different direction from the person slightly to the right."
"Because they're pointing towards different parts of the Earth."
"Exactly."
Trevor concluded, "So, their paths slowly change relative to each other."
"Yes."
"That's a tidal effect."
"Precisely."
Penny confirmed. "Geodesic deviation."
Derek grinned. "Precisely."
Trevor tapped one mysterious clock, then the other. "So, gravity isn't just about how strong it is at one point."
"Correct."
"It's also about how it changes across space."
"Yes."
"And that's curvature."
"Now you're getting it."
Trevor smiled. "Well, that's one theory down."
The lights flickered. All three flinched. The lights went out.
For one second there was complete darkness. 
Then they came back on. The clocks were still there.
The chairs were still there. The table was still there. 
But the narrow corridor outside had changed.
Derek stepped out. He counted once under his breath before trusting the number aloud. There were fourteen doors.
Trevor counted. "Fourteen."
Penny counted. "Thirteen."
Derek counted. "Fourteen."
They stared at each other. 
Trevor aimed a finger at Penny. "You're doing it again."
Penny grinned. "Apparently."
Derek watched the corridor. "Something has changed."
"What?"
"The geometry."
Trevor grimaced. "How can you see geometry?"
"You don't see it directly."
"Then how do you know?"
"Because measurements don't fit together the way they should."
Penny agreed. "That's the important bit."
She walked to the end of the narrow corridor. 
Then back. "Forty-seven metres."
Derek measured. "Forty-seven."
Trevor measured. "Forty-seven."
Derek slowly surveyed the doors. "So, the length hasn't changed."
"No."
"The number of doors has."
"Yes."
"The clocks have changed."
"Yes."
"The gravitational gradient has changed."
"Yes."
Trevor's shoulders crept up around his ears. "And you're saying all of this can happen because spacetime is curved?"
Derek confirmed. "That's what General Relativity permits."
"But not arbitrarily."
"Correct."
"The geometry has to satisfy Einstein's equations."
"Yes."
"So, this isn't magic."
"No."
Trevor pointed at the fourteen doors, counting under his breath. "It looks remarkably like magic."
Derek smirked. "That's because you haven't seen enough mathematics."
Penny laughed. They continued. At the far end of the corridor was a wall.
No door. No sign. Nothing.
Derek touched it. Solid. 
Penny knocked. Solid. 
Trevor knocked. Solid.
Derek stepped back.
"Move."
Trevor did. Derek took three steps backwards. 
Then ran towards the wall.
Penny shouted: "Derek!"
He hit the wall. Except he didn't. He went through it.
Penny leaned in. 
Trevor stared. 
Derek disappeared.
There was silence. Trevor regarded Penny. "He's gone."
Penny confirmed. "Yes."
"Should we be worried?"
"No."
"Why not?"
"His office door was closed."
Trevor conceded. "Ah."
A voice came from the other side of the wall.
"Come through."
Penny walked towards the wall. She touched it. Her hand went through.
She smiled. "Interesting."
Trevor took a step back, horrified. "I don't think that's a property walls normally have."
Penny examined him. "Nothing does until it does."
She walked through.
Trevor sighed. Then followed. 
On the other side was another strange corridor. 
Much longer.
Much darker. 
And, unlike the first strange corridor, it contained no doors at all. At the far end was a station platform.
A sign read:
COLD PLATFORM 7
Derek stood beside it. 
Trevor joined him. "Where are we?"
Derek took in the room. "Underground."
"We were already underground."
"Yes."
"So?"
Derek gestured upward. They could hear rain. 
"We're beneath London."
"Yes."
"And it's raining above us."
"Yes."
"How can you hear it?"
Derek grinned. "Good question."
Penny inspected the floor. Water was running along the edge of the platform. 
She crouched. "It isn't just rain."
Derek joined her. "What?"
"The water."
She indicated it. It was flowing towards the drain. 
Then it stopped. Completely. Not slowed. Stopped.
A drop hung at the edge of the drain. 
Trevor crouched. "Is that normal?"
"No," Derek responded.
The drop moved backwards. 
Then forward. Then sideways.
Then fell into the drain. Trevor's mouth twitched. "Naturally."
Derek stood. "The local geometry is doing something strange."
Penny considered him. "Can we distinguish that from a coordinate effect?"
"Not yet."
"How?"
"Find something invariant."
Trevor glanced around. "The cucumber."
Derek and Penny turned to him in unison.
He shrugged. "You've used it before."
Penny's mouth curved. "Good thinking."
She took the cucumber from her bag. 
"You brought it?"
"Obviously."
"Why?"
"You never know."
Trevor allowed. "That resolves it, then."
They placed the ordinary cucumber on the platform. Length thirty-one centimetres — until it wasn't. Local SR still worked; position-dependent length meant the corridor was not flat. The cucumber had become the control with a worldline.
Penny measured it. Exactly thirty-one centimetres. Trevor measured it.
Thirty-one centimetres. 
Derek confirmed. "Stationary."
"Yes."
"Now let's move it."
He placed it on a small wheeled platform. 
They pushed it along the track. Penny measured again.
Thirty centimetres. 
Trevor's brow creased. "Length contraction."
"Exactly."
"Special Relativity."
"Yes."
Derek pushed it faster. Twenty-eight centimetres. 
Trevor smiled. "Still Special Relativity."
"Yes."
Then they stopped. The cucumber returned to thirty-one centimetres. Perfectly ordinary.
Penny grinned. "Excellent."
Derek nodded. "Now we know the cucumber works — joke, clue, experiment, evidence, and whatever Penny is carefully not saying aloud. One object, two behaviours, depending on the frame. If Case 1047 is the same trick with a corpse-shaped label, we're done hunting killers."
Trevor blinked twice. "What does that mean?"
"It obeys relativity."
"Of course it does."
Derek glanced at the empty station. "Now we need to find out whether the geometry itself is changing."
He placed the cucumber at one end of the platform. Then another.
The measured lengths changed slightly depending upon position. 
Trevor ran a finger down the numbers. "That's not length contraction."
"No."
"Because the cucumber isn't moving."
"Correct."
"So, the spatial geometry is changing."
"Yes."
Penny frowned at the track. "Could the gravitational field be responsible?"
"Possibly."
"The tidal field?"
"More likely."
Trevor took in the room. "How much curvature would we need?"
Derek let himself look pleased, briefly. "More than London normally provides."
"That's reassuring."
"Unfortunately..."
Derek gestured down the track. 
The rails were bending. Not physically.
They remained straight to the eye. But the measured distance between them was changing. 
Penny went quiet. "That's curvature."
Derek agreed. "Yes."
Trevor swallowed. "So, the Underground has become a General Relativity experiment."
"Apparently."
"Is that safe?"
Derek considered it. "Probably."
Trevor waited. "Probably?"
Derek smiled. "Would you prefer 'possibly'?"
"No."
"Then probably."
The station lights flickered again. 
The cucumber began to tick. 
Trevor looked at it. "That can't be good."
A train appeared in the tunnel. No headlights. No sound.
It simply suddenly appeared. 
Penny stood. "Where did that come from?"
Derek studied the rails. "That's the wrong question."
"What is the right one?"
"Which worldline did it follow?"
Trevor considered this. "The train has a worldline?"
"Everything physical does."
"I know that," Trevor said. "I mean have we measured one continuous train — or several appearances we've decided to call the same train?"
Derek went quiet for a moment. "That's the better question."
"Even the cucumber?"
"Yes."
Penny watched the ordinary cucumber, thinking, 'peeled and pre-warmed might be nicer'.
Then she confirmed, "Even the cucumber."
Trevor conceded slowly. "Splendid. Moving on."
The train approached. It stopped. The doors opened.
Nobody got off. Nobody got on. Inside, the carriage was completely empty.
Except for a single seat. And on the seat was another cucumber. Derek stared at it.
Penny paled at the sight of him. 
Trevor swallowed hard. 
Nobody offered anything.
Then Derek grinned. "Well."
Penny folded her arms. "Don't."
"I haven't offered anything."
"You were about to."
Derek surveyed the second cucumber. "All right. Two cucumbers. Or one cucumber under two labels. I am beginning to hate that sentence."
He stepped into the carriage. 
Penny followed.
Trevor looked, briefly, entirely done with this.
Then followed them. The doors quietly closed. 
The mysterious train began to move.
Behind them, the station disappeared. 
Ahead, the tunnel curved. 
And somewhere between London and somewhere that London had apparently forgotten to put on the map, the clocks began moving again.
11:18. 11:19. 11:20.
He smiled. "There. Progress."
The distant train accelerated into the darkness. 
And for the first time, Derek began to suspect that the strangest thing about the empty station was not that its geometry was curved. 
It was that something appeared to be curving it on purpose.

Rule: A station that isn't quite there is still curving something on purpose.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 14 - The Train Arrived Before It Left
Chapter 15
The Train Arrived Before It Left
The narrow corridor ended at cold Platform Seven. Derek stopped. "Of course."
Penny regarded the sign.
PLATFORM 7
"There isn't a Platform Seven."
Trevor examined the timetable. 
"The early bird gets the warm," Trevor observed. 
"The early bird gets the worm." Penny corrected. Trevor nodded. "Yes. Obviously. I was testing the bird."
"Excellent."
"Why is that excellent?"
"Because if there were a Platform Seven, this would be considerably less interesting."
Penny looked sharply down the platform. Before she could say anything, a woman's voice came from behind them.
"If you're planning to take that train, I'd recommend knowing where it goes first."
All three spun around. 
The woman at the end of the lonely platform radiated a calm too complete for a place that should not exist. For one unproductive second, Derek wondered if she was the woman from the Underground puddle, a lifetime — several days, technically — ago. She was not. That one hadn't had a dog, or, as far as he knew, a brother.
She was unhurried in a way Trevor had never quite managed, dark hair pulled back with the same practical impatience Sophie applied to hers, and dressed for weather rather than effect. Where her brother went still when he was thinking, Tabitha went quiet in an entirely different register — the quiet of someone who had already finished thinking and was simply waiting for everyone else to catch up.
The woman didn't so much as glance at the board. She had clearly already priced in stranger.
A small dog's head, no larger than a fist, emerged from the collar of her coat, took in the impossible platform with the same composure as its owner, and withdrew again.
Trevor's head snapped up. "Tabitha?"
She grinned. "Hello, little brother." She murmured it as if the relationship were still under review.
Derek studied Trevor. "Your sister?"
"Unfortunately," Trevor opined.
Tabitha raised an eyebrow. "I heard that."
Derek glanced at the coat. "Was that a dog?"
"Gucci," Tabitha announced, as though this settled the matter. "He doesn't care for platforms that shouldn't exist. Frankly, neither do I, but one of us has to be professional about it. He also won't touch dog food — he'll only eat if it comes hand-fed from my plate, which I'm told is a personality disorder and he's told is a lifestyle. He'll finish anyone's leftover tea, given the chance, except Earl Grey, which he regards — correctly, in my opinion - as an affectation. He also once got into a plate with Marmite on it and was sick for an hour, which I consider a reasonable response. The vomiting, I should say, was not instantaneous — there was a delay, rather like light taking its time to arrive from somewhere unpleasant. Cause and effect, several minutes apart, both entirely his own fault."
"You were meant to."
She considered Derek. "So, you're Derek."
"Yes."
"Trevor maintained you were the sensible one."
Derek raised an eyebrow at Trevor. "He has never affirmed that to me."
"Good," Tabitha confirmed. "I'd hate to begin with a false impression."
Penny looked from Tabitha to Derek. She remained silent. She was very good at saying nothing in ways that made Derek nervous.
Derek found himself noticing that Tabitha was watching him. Then she let the moment pass without comment. She had the calm of someone who could leave at any moment and be late for something better. 
He decided not to think about that. The decision proved only partially successful, which was becoming a pattern.
Which was, in retrospect, the first sign that this was going to be a problem.
He remembered, belatedly, that she had once asked a single question about simultaneous events at a Bureau function and left before anyone could answer it properly. 
He suspected, uneasily, that she had already worked out the answer herself.
"How did you get here?" Derek asked.
Tabitha held up her phone. "Trevor sent me a message."
Trevor grimaced. "I didn't."
Tabitha scanned the screen.
"Apparently someone using your number did."
Silence. Derek held out his hand. "What did it say?"
Tabitha showed him.
DON'T LET WEINSTEIN GET TO THE STATION FIRST.
Derek's expression changed.
"When did you receive that?"
"Eleven minutes ago."
Trevor's brow furrowed. "Weinstein is in Munich."
"Apparently not," Derek insisted.
Tabitha slipped the phone back into her pocket.
"So," she emphasised, looking at the train, "which part of this is supposed to be normal?"
Derek smiled. "We're still looking for that part."
Tabitha grinned back. "Good. I was worried I'd missed it."
There was, in fact, a distant, mysterious train waiting. This was unfortunate. It was an entirely ordinary Underground train.
Grey. Damp. Slightly depressing.
The sort of train that looked as though it had been designed by a committee whose principal objective had been to discourage tourism.
Except for one small problem. 
There was no driver.
There were no passengers. 
There was no conductor. 
There was no staff.
And there was no record of the train on the timetable. Trevor checked the timetable again. Nothing.
He checked his phone. Nothing. 
He checked the transport app. Nothing. 
He stared at Derek. "Perhaps it's cancelled."
Derek gazed at the mysterious train. "It's here."
"Yes."
"Therefore it isn't cancelled."
Trevor considered this. "That's not how cancellations work."
"It is if the train exists."
"Does it?"
Derek smiled. "Excellent question."
Tabitha stopped them. "One moment."
She indicated the destination board. "London Terminus."
Derek confirmed. "Yes."
"Is that actually a destination?"
Trevor peered at the sign. "Apparently."
Tabitha shook her head. "No." She tapped a destination word that refused to finish spelling itself. "I'm not boarding first."
Derek gave her a measured look. For a moment he remained silent.
"That's rather good."
Tabitha grinned. "I was hoping you would say that."
Penny walked towards it. 
The destination board read:
LONDON TERMINUS
She stopped. "Where is London Terminus?"
Derek watched the sign. "Everywhere."
Trevor sighed. "That isn't helpful."
"It wasn't intended to be."
Penny surveyed the mysterious train. "There's no such station."
Derek confirmed. "Correct."
"Then why does the train say it's going there?"
"Perhaps it's optimistic."
The doors carefully opened. Nobody moved. Derek stepped inside.
Penny followed. Tabitha followed Penny, already looking as if she belonged on the train more than the rest of them. Trevor remained on the lonely platform.
Derek faced them again. "Coming?"
Trevor hung back from the empty carriage. "Do you know where it goes?"
"No."
"Do you know who is driving it?"
"No."
"Do you know why it exists?"
"No."
Trevor sighed. "Fine."
He stepped aboard. 
Tabitha regarded him. "You know, for someone who asked whether it has a driver, you seem remarkably willing to get on it."
"I am beginning to suspect that refusing to board would be less safe."
"Good reasoning."
The doors quietly closed. The mysterious train moved. There was no announcement.
No warning. 
No reassuring strange voice telling them to mind the gap. It simply accelerated.
"A shilling short and a fortnight late," Derek remarked. "That is becoming the Bureau's unofficial motto."
Derek watched the carriage. "Acceleration."
Trevor agreed. "We can feel it."
"Yes."
"The carriage is lying about stillness."
"Correct."
Penny looked out of the window. "The station is accelerating away from us."
Derek allowed himself a smile. "Depending on your frame."
Trevor got there first. "Depending on the frame. Yes, I know."
The mysterious train continued into the narrow tunnel. Derek closed the notebook without writing.
Trevor examined him. "That's unusual."
Derek indicated two disagreeing watches. "Measure. Don't baptise."
He wrote:
INERTIAL FRAME
Then:
LORENTZ TRANSFORMATION
Then:
PROPER TIME
Trevor read it over his shoulder and didn't argue with any of it — which, for Trevor, was practically applause. "The station has another proper time too, presumably."
"Exactly."
Trevor conceded. "Fine."
The mysterious train stopped. The doors opened. They stepped onto the cold platform.
Trevor surveyed the room. 
His expression changed. "This is Platform Seven."
Derek confirmed. "Yes."
"But..."
Trevor gestured down the tunnel. "We just left here."
"Yes."
"And now we're back."
"Yes."
Trevor checked his phone. 11:18. 
Derek checked his watch. 11:18. 
Penny checked hers. 11:18.
Trevor leaned in. "So, the station clock hasn't advanced."
"No."
"But our clocks have."
"Yes."
"And we were moving relative to the station."
"Yes."
Trevor considered Derek. "Time dilation."
"Possibly," Derek said.
Derek's hand was still on the clock-face. It was warm—the friction from hours of ticking had leached into the metal. Tabitha glanced at the empty station clock. "Why do you keep treating that one as the correct clock?"
Derek faced her. "Because it stayed at the station."
"So?"
"It is the stationary reference clock."
Tabitha smirked. "Stationary relative to what, exactly?"
Trevor stopped. Penny stared at the clock. "She's right." Derek's hand went still on the clock. Derek found the observation more interesting than he had expected. More unsettling.
Derek went quiet, transfixed by the empty station clock. 
Tabitha tapped the station clock. "That one stayed. Ours didn't. That's the whole sentence."
Trevor's expression changed.
"So, there isn't a privileged timekeeper."
"Exactly," Tabitha said. "The universe doesn't keep a master clock. It only keeps local ones. People keep inventing the master clock. The universe never asked for one." 
Derek, for reasons he chose not to examine, found himself watching Tabitha rather than the clock. Her presence made the situation feel slightly more manageable, which he decided was an unhelpful development. 
He filed the observation under 'later,' which was, by now, a considerably overstuffed drawer. 
The universe was consistent on this point as well. 
Consistency, Derek had noticed, was not the same as kindness.
Penny continued. "And if we want to compare the clocks properly, we have to compare the paths they actually took through spacetime."
Derek grinned. "Proper time."
Trevor gestured at the clocks. "So, the difference isn't evidence that one clock is wrong."
"Correct," Derek stated.
Tabitha considered the tunnel's dark mouth. "Then perhaps the train isn't carrying us through someone else's time. Perhaps it is carrying us along a different path."
Silence. Derek gazed at her. "Tabitha."
"Yes?"
"You may have just made this considerably worse."
She smiled. "I was afraid of that." 
Derek discovered he was not entirely sorry. The realisation that he was more comfortable with her presence than without it was, he concluded, going to be a problem for another day — preferably one that had not yet been invented. 
He filed this under Processed as well, from long habit, before noticing that the drawer no longer seemed to close properly. It occurred to him, distantly, that some cases got reopened not because new evidence arrived, but because someone finally looked at the label twice.
He suspected it would not remain someone else's for long. 
Trevor, for his part, had gone quiet for a while, plainly recalculating several assumptions at once. "Why possibly?"
"Because we've only observed a difference in clock readings. We still need to understand the geometry of the situation."
Trevor said nothing. He wrote it down instead.
Penny indicated the tunnel. "The train is coming back."
The same train was approaching. 
Trevor frowned. "But we just got off it."
"Yes."
"It hasn't had time to go anywhere."
Derek watched the train approach. "That depends on what you mean by time."
Trevor softly sighed. "I knew you were going to say that."
The mysterious train stopped. The doors carefully opened. A second Derek stepped out.
Trevor went completely still. Penny's mouth opened and shut without sound. Derek stared at Derek.
The second Derek met their eyes. Then at his watch. Then at the station clock.
"You're late."
The first Derek's brow furrowed. "I haven't gone anywhere."
The second Derek grinned. "I know."
Trevor's head snapped between them. "Which one of you is Derek?"
Both Dereks faced him. "Me."
Trevor closed his eyes briefly. "There are two of him. Splendid. I'll need a bigger notebook."
Penny eyed the second Derek.
"Where did you come from?"
"The train."
"Where was the train?"
"Here."
"It wasn't."
"It was."
Penny insisted, "You just arrived."
"Yes."
"From where?"
"Here."
Penny blinked slowly. Derek smiled. "He's right."
Trevor opened his eyes. "Don't encourage him."
The second Derek watched the first. "You're still trying to describe the whole thing with one clock."
Derek confirmed. "Yes."
"Stop."
Derek considered this. "Fair."
The second Derek gestured towards the empty station. "Stop asking when. Ask what connects."
Derek nodded. The second Derek studied him. "For now."
Then he vanished before anyone could ask for definitions. Trevor jumped.
"Where did he go?"
Derek regarded the empty platform. "Virtuality."
Penny conceded. "Obviously."
"I am beginning to dislike that word."
Tabitha checked her watch, decided it disagreed with several things she'd rather not think about, and drew the obvious conclusion. "I have a dog, a brother, and a strong personal policy against boarding the same train twice in one afternoon." She kissed Trevor's cheek, told Derek to try not to die again before the weekend, and walked back towards the ordinary end of the platform — the one with a working ticket machine and no interest in leaving before it arrived. Nobody stopped her. Nobody, on reflection, could have named a good reason to.
The train doors closed. The train departed. This time Derek paid closer attention.
He took measurements. Speed. Acceleration.
Distance. Clock readings. 
Everything suddenly appeared ordinary.
Then the train emerged from the tunnel. 
They were above ground. 
Cold rain streaked across the windows.
London suddenly appeared. 
Grey buildings. Grey sky. Grey people. Grey everything. 
Trevor looked sharply relieved. "Back to normal."
A raindrop hit the window. Derek watched it hesitate mid-fall, hang there a moment too long, then reverse. Then stop.
Then moved sideways. Then went upward. Against every law Derek understood. Against the geometry that had governed his entire life.
Trevor paused. "No."
Penny leaned closer. "Interesting."
Derek grinned. "Very."
The droplet split into two. One part moved down. The other moved up.
Trevor examined Derek. "That isn't normal."
"No."
"Coordinate effect?"
"Possibly."
"Geometry?"
"Perhaps."
"Train?"
"Maybe."
"Rain?"
"Possibly."
Trevor sighed. "You're enjoying this."
"Immensely."
The mysterious train slowed. 
Derek checked his watch. 11:18.
The mysterious train clock: 11:18. 
Penny's phone: 11:18. 
The station clock outside: 12:04.
They stopped. The doors quietly opened. Everyone stepped out.
The cold rain was falling normally again. 
Trevor inspected the ancient station clock. 12:04.
He checked his phone. 11:18. 
Then back at the clock. 12:04.
"Now what?"
Derek allowed himself a smile. "Now we have a problem."
Penny confirmed. "Good."
Trevor's eyebrows shot up. "Why is that good?"
"Because we know the discrepancy is not just between two clocks."
Derek continued. "The station itself is assigning different temporal coordinates."
Trevor's forehead creased. "So which time is correct?"
Derek glanced at him. "Both."
Trevor conceded. "Obviously."
Derek indicated a bench. A newspaper lay there. He picked it up.
The headline read:
RELATIVITY PROVED WRONG
Trevor nodded. "Finally."
Derek turned the page. Underneath was another headline:
RELATIVITY PROVED RIGHT
Penny laughed. "That's more like it."
Derek turned the page again. A third headline:
RELATIVITY PROVED BOTH
Trevor's whole body stilled. "That's impossible."
Derek didn't even blink. "No."
Trevor waited. Derek smiled. "No. It's journalism."
Penny laughed. They sat on the bench. 
Derek folded the newspaper. "Never be reassured too early."
They stood. The train had disappeared.
There was no record of it on the empty station board.
No destination. No arrival. No departure. Nothing.
Trevor turned to Derek. "Where did it go?"
Derek watched the rails. "I don't know."
Penny grinned. "That's a first."
Derek ignored her. He crouched beside the track. There was something on the rail.
A small brass plate. He picked it up. It was engraved with a single number:
17
Trevor leaned closer. "Again."
Derek confirmed. "Again."
Derek tipped his head towards her. "What else could it be?"
"An event label. Death. Departure. Arrival. One geometry, three names." She stopped. The rest of the sentence sat between them, unspoken.
Derek stopped moving. Something about Tuesday morning's email rearranged itself behind his eyes. He did not say it aloud.
Trevor waved a hand between them. "So which clock is correct?"
"Wrong question," Derek corrected. "Which description is allowed — and which action turns a label into proper time."
Trevor angled his phone so the graphs filled the room. "Clocks are data. Data first."
Penny pocketed the brass plate and turned her phone face-down. Trevor's graphs reflected in the glass; she did not look.
Trevor rubbed his eyes. "I still want a person. Persons can be arrested."
Something crossed Derek's face that wasn't quite a smile. "Then we may be hunting the wrong kind of noun."
They stood silently for a moment.
Then Derek looked at the sky. The cold rain had stopped.
That was unusual. Very unusual.
Penny noticed it too. "London has stopped raining."
Trevor craned his neck back. "That's worse than the train."
Derek allowed. "Much worse."
A single drop of water fell from the sky. It stopped halfway down. Then slowly moved upward.
Then sideways. Then disappeared.
Penny said, very quietly, "If the midpoint was right — one event, three labels — then boarding the wrong train doesn't solve anything. It finishes something."
Derek regarded her. He thought of the photograph. Of Sophie's school bag. Of Penny's hand on his sleeve. Of becoming the face on the floor because he had insisted on treating a coordinate like a corpse — or of erasing a version of Sophie by refusing the geometry that contained her. He understood, finally, what the sacrifice looked like: it was not stopping the murder. It was accepting that he might be standing in the doorway too, somewhere the paperwork could still see him.
Derek put his hand on the carriage door and did not open it. In the window he saw the doorway from the photograph — empty, for now. "We board as people who can still refuse," he declared. "Not as a filing."
Penny didn't look at the photograph again. "You can't choose whether the drawer opens."
"No."
"You can choose what walks through it."
That, Derek thought, was the whole of it. The rest was just paperwork.
Trevor grimaced. "Are you sure?"
Derek took a slow breath. "No."
Trevor conceded. "Case not closed. Probably."
According to the railway timetable, it had never existed. Which, Derek thought, was probably the most reliable information of all — unless the timetable was another label.
He opened the door.
The train did not so much depart as agree, briefly, to have already left. There was no lurch, no whistle, no sensation of gathering speed — only the platform sliding away in a manner that felt less like motion than like punctuation, one paragraph ending so the next could begin.
Trevor sat down anyway, out of habit, and immediately regretted it: the seat was there, and also, for a disorienting half-second, not there, the way a chair looks in a photograph taken a moment before someone pulls it out from under you.
Penny didn't sit. She watched the window instead, where London went past in the wrong order — the same red bus passing twice, five seconds apart, going in opposite directions.
"Are we still counting doors?" Trevor asked.
"No," said Derek. "We're counting whether we get off."
Nobody answered that, on the grounds that it wasn't actually a question.
The doors opened again before any of them had decided the journey was over. Grey London daylight. The particular smell of instant coffee and old carpet. Trevor looked out and recognised the corridor outside the Bureau, with the lurch of meeting his own front door from the wrong side of it.
"That's our corridor," he said.
"Yes," Derek agreed, stepping off.
"We didn't go anywhere."
"We went exactly as far as we needed to." Derek didn't look back at the platform, which was already declining, politely, to have been there in the first place.
Trevor considered this for the length of the corridor. "I'm filing 'refused to board a train, then boarded it anyway' as a philosophical achievement."
"Denied," said Penny, already unlocking the office door.

Rule: Stop asking when. Ask which action finishes the filing.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 15 - The Dolphin and Final Geometry
Chapter 16
Sherlock and the Final Geometry
Weinstein was having a perfectly ordinary morning. This meant, naturally, that the universe was about to become ridiculous. He was sitting in his quiet office in Munich, staring at a sheet of paper on which he had written the same equation three times.
He had crossed it out three times. The fourth version looked promising. The fifth was worse.
The sixth looked suspiciously like the first.
Weinstein wearily sighed.
General Relativity had reached the tea stage: every answer bred two better questions. Weinstein crossed out the equation again. The sixth version was, once again, indistinguishable from the first.
It still worked. That was the insult.
He wrote CASE 1047 under the crossed lines and underlined DEAD as if the word might confess.
And yet Weinstein had the persistent feeling that he was looking at the description rather than the thing being described.
He wrote:
WHAT IS SPACETIME ACTUALLY MADE OF?
Then underneath:
AND WHY DOES CASE 1047 THINK DEREK IS ALREADY DEAD?
He read the words back to himself. Then came a noise. Not a knock.
Not a telephone. Not the sound of someone opening his empty office door. It was a voice.
Inside his head — and it did not arrive the way a thought arrives, or the way a memory does. It arrived the way sound arrives underwater: rounded at the edges, faintly compressed, carrying with it an impression of great unhurried distance, as if whatever was speaking had all the time in the world and had simply chosen, generously, to spend a little of it here. "Good question."
Weinstein froze. He looked around. Nobody.
The familiar office door was quietly closed.
Therefore somebody was definitely in there. Obviously.
Weinstein stood. "Who commented that?"
Silence. Then:
"I did."
Weinstein silently stared at the wall. "Who are you?"
A pause. "Sherlock."
Weinstein frowned. "Sherlock who?"
Another pause.
"Sherlock."
"That's not a surname."
"No."
"Are you a person?"
"No."
Weinstein asked, "A computer?"
"No."
"An artificial intelligence?"
"No."
"Then what are you?"
The answer arrived calmly.
"A dolphin."
Weinstein sat down. "Of course."
There was another pause.
"You don't believe me."
"I am receiving a telepathic communication from a dolphin called Sherlock."
"Yes."
"In Munich."
"Yes."
"Inside my head." Weinstein said it slowly, the way a man tests a floorboard before trusting it.
"Yes."
Weinstein considered this at some length. "So much for that, then."
Sherlock seemed pleased.
"Good."
"Why are you contacting me?"
"Because London is about to file the wrong conclusion."
"About what?"
"The murder that isn't one."
Weinstein sat upright. "You have a solution?"
"I have a geometry — and what the Bureau did with it."
Weinstein leaned in. "Go on."
Sherlock began. Not with an answer. With a question. "The note on the corpse-file. Three words. Death. Departure. Arrival. How many events does that describe?"
Weinstein's expression tightened. "Three, obviously."
"Wrong. Say it again, slower."
Weinstein said it again. Then stopped. "...One event. Three labels for the same coordinate, depending which frame is reading it."
"Correct. Now the drawer. Mrs Marsh keeps one marked Pending Geometry. Nobody uses it. Why?"
"Because everyone assumes an event that looks like a corpse belongs in Murder."
"And an event that looks like nothing yet — a train that hasn't arrived, a child in a doorway that doesn't exist — where does that belong?"
Weinstein wrote it down before he'd finished thinking it: PENDING GEOMETRY.
"Now the file," Sherlock explained. "It kept rewriting itself. Not because a ghost was typing. Because the case was open at both ends — unresolved in exactly the way an interval is unresolved until you fix a frame. The file wasn't haunted. It was uncommitted."
Weinstein exhaled. "Like the cucumber."
"Exactly like the cucumber. One object, two descriptions, neither one a lie. Cut it end to end, it's a line. Cut it across, it's a circle. Nobody murdered the cucumber into being both."
"So Case 1047 — "
" — is not a corpse with three witnesses disagreeing about a killer. It is one event — Death, Departure, Arrival are the same word said in three coordinate systems — filed under the wrong heading because London insisted a coordinate needed a culprit."
Weinstein found his voice again first. "That is a generous use of the phrase 'coordinate system.' Death happened in an office. Departure and Arrival happened on a railway platform. Those are not two number-sets for one location. They are two different places."
"They are," Sherlock agreed. "Which is exactly why this was never an ordinary relabelling — the kind where a door stays where it is and only the numbers beside it change. Nobody is asking you to accept that trick twice, somewhere it doesn't belong. What London built, without meaning to, is closer to the holography Julius keeps threatening you with: two descriptions that look like they live on separate stages, until you learn to read one of them as the boundary of the other. The office and the platform were never two rooms sharing a coincidence. They were one event, described from its two ends, and nobody had written down the dictionary that translates between them — until today."
Weinstein was quiet for a moment. "Julius is going to be unbearable about this."
"He already is," Sherlock said. "He's on the other line."
Somewhere across London, in the Bureau's quiet office, Trevor and Penny and Derek heard the same voice inside their own heads, walking the same four steps.
Trevor's attention quickened. "If this is another lecture — "
"It isn't," Penny corrected. "It's arithmetic. Listen."
Derek got there first, half a second ahead of the voice in his own skull: "One event. Three labels. We've been arresting a coordinate, not a killer."
On Derek's screen the category field flickered, one step behind Sherlock's argument: MURDER... DEPARTURE... ARRIVAL... PENDING GEOMETRY. Mrs Marsh's stamp appeared in the margin without a hand attached. The photograph's caption blanked, then returned as EVENT — UNSORTED.
The brass clock stayed at 11:03. The office clocks kept counting. Nobody confused them again.
Penny remembered asking, days ago, whether "departure" meant the train, and being told only "perhaps" — as if a correct guess still had to wait its turn behind the evidence.
Tuesday's email had been sitting on the desk the whole time — not a warning. Evidence, for a filing nobody had made yet.
Trevor managed, "I saw blood."
"You saw a filing," Sherlock clarified. "Three labels, one event, the wrong drawer. Not Murder. Not a killer waiting in your future. Just Pending Geometry, waiting to be corrected." Derek's hand stopped shaking.
Trevor wasn't finished. "A filing doesn't bleed. I saw a photograph — real light, on a real sensor, of a wound that isn't in this room. Where does blood come from if nobody was stabbed?"
"The same place the cucumber's circle comes from when you slice it the wrong way," Sherlock said. "Not from a lie. From a projection. A photograph is a boundary reading of an event, the same as a length is a boundary reading of a journey. Slice a journey badly enough and the length can tell you almost anything, including things that never happened along the path you actually took. Your camera didn't lie about what it read off that boundary. It only failed to say which boundary it was reading."
Trevor considered this with visible suspicion. "That is a deeply unsatisfying answer."
"Most true ones are," Sherlock said.
Trevor demanded, "Then why did the system name me? PERPETRATOR. My name. Not a guess — my name." It came out smaller than he meant it to.
Sherlock replied, "Because a form asked for one. A field marked Perpetrator does not stay empty just because nobody committed anything. The system reached for whoever's worldline sat closest to the misfiled coordinate at the moment it was opened. You were in the room. That is the entire crime you're guilty of."
Trevor sat with that. "I'd have preferred a better reason."
"Most people would."
Weinstein rubbed his eyes. "And Derek standing up. Dead, and standing. I watched it happen. I am not owed a hallucination, Sherlock."
"You weren't given one," Sherlock continued. "You watched the label change — Death to Departure to Arrival — faster than your eye could sort it, and your eye did what eyes do with a discontinuity: it filled the gap with a body getting up. You didn't see a corpse stand. You saw a frame boundary, and flinched it into a story."
Weinstein said nothing for a moment. Then, quietly, as if filing it away for later: "I would still have sworn to it in court."
"Most witnesses would."
Weinstein didn't say anything else for a while. For a man who had spent forty years trusting his own careful eye over everyone else's careless one, watching it fail him this completely was going to cost more than an afternoon to file away properly.
Derek asked it plainly. "And the man on the train. The one wearing my face."
"The same discontinuity, in a longer coat," Sherlock said. "Not a second Derek waiting in the wings — a worldline the geometry hadn't finished labelling yet. Departure, dressed as a person, because that's what a mind does with an event it can't file. You didn't meet him. You watched an unlabelled coordinate for long enough that it blinked."
Derek made himself ask the only question that had actually kept him awake. "And Sophie. In the doorway, behind me, in the photograph. Why was she there at all?"
"She wasn't," Sherlock said. "Not in any event that occurred. The filing pulled her in the way a bad exposure pulls in a smear at the edge of a frame — because Departure and Arrival both needed a witness on file for that address, and the system reached for the nearest worldline it had. Yours. Hers. The same drawer, the same mistake, twice over. She was never standing in that doorway. She was misfiled into it, exactly the way Trevor was misfiled into Perpetrator."
Derek thought of her clock, then - the one on his study windowsill, four minutes fast for as long as anyone could remember. "Her clock. The one that runs fast. Is that -"
"Not the same fault," Sherlock said. "That one really is just a clock that runs fast, and nobody has filed a single form about it in eleven years. Not everything in this house is a symptom. Some of it is just a clock."
Derek felt something in his chest loosen and tighten at once. Relief and loss. There was no killer to catch. There was only a daughter he'd saved by refusing to finish their filing. But somewhere in the geometry, he was still lying face-down on the floor, and he'd never know if that version of himself had been trying to save her too. "So there was no murderer."
"Pending Geometry," Sherlock finished. "And a daughter in a doorway it should never have contained."
Penny whispered, "Pending Geometry."
Weinstein, in Munich, wrote without looking at his hand:
GEOMETRY IS RELATIONAL
Then:
TIME IS RELATIONAL
Then:
A CLOCK CAN LIE BY TELLING THE WRONG KIND OF TRUTH
Derek understood before he liked it.
A CLOCK CAN LIE BY TELLING THE WRONG KIND OF TRUTH.
Trevor sat down hard. "So I didn't kill you."
Derek turned the photograph face-down. His thumb stayed on Sophie's doorway a second too long. "Not with a knife," he answered. "Not at all — if we refuse to finish their filing."
Weinstein, quiet: "The joke was thinking murder was the noun that mattered."
Penny looked at Derek — cousin, colleague, the face that had been on the floor in a picture that was not a photograph. "Does that mean you're safe?"
Derek didn't answer straight away. There was no one to blame for the photograph, which meant there was also no one to forgive — not the system, not Trevor, not the version of himself still lying face-down in a geometry that hadn't finished deciding what it was. He'd spent every day of this case wanting a villain badly enough to nearly build one out of the man who made his tea, and the case had simply declined to supply one. That was going to take longer to feel like good news than it currently did.
Across the desk, Penny watched him think it through, patient in the specific way she only managed when she already suspected the answer wouldn't be simple.
There was a worse thought underneath it, and he let himself have it now, in the privacy of a question nobody had actually asked aloud. If spacetime was the whole of reality — what physicists rather flatly called a block universe — if Death, Departure, and Arrival really were one event sitting still in four dimensions, waiting for someone with the wrong coordinates to stumble across it — then that photograph hadn't been a warning at all. It had already happened, the way Wednesday had already happened before he'd lived through it.
Trevor had never really had a choice not to leave that scene behind him; there had simply never been a version of the block in which he did. Derek turned that over and found, to his own surprise, that it changed almost nothing. A cause you can't blame is still not a cause you have to forgive twice.
He'd spent a week investigating a filing error as though it were a crime, and apparently the only sensible response to a fixed event was the same one he gave every ordinary Tuesday: get up, put the kettle on, and try not to make it worse. Free will, he decided, was a local variable at best — but it was the only one he had jurisdiction over, and he intended to keep filing under it regardless.
"I think so," he admitted, eventually. "I'm not sure 'safe' is the right unit for it, but yes."
Penny didn't ask again. Her breath caught as his hand hovered over STATUS, then landed.
Weinstein wrote one more line:
CASE STATUS: NOT A MURDER. EVENT MISFILED. 
PENDING GEOMETRY.
Sherlock sent approval, and then — because dolphins have priorities — an image of a cucumber.
"That's ridiculous."
"Yes."
"Why is it in the theory?"
"It isn't. Penny thinks experiments should be edible. The cucumber proved two descriptions can be one object without either description being a lie. Your filing cabinet failed the same test, loudly."
Weinstein laughed once. Outside, Munich continued being Munich.
In London, Derek closed the Case 1047 window. His hand did not quite shake.
"Status?" Trevor asked.
Derek typed carefully, as if the keys themselves might misfile him.
CASE STATUS: NOT A MURDER. EVENT MISFILED. PENDING GEOMETRY.
Penny exhaled. It was not quite relief. Relief assumed the story had ended.
"Well, Sherlock," Weinstein began.
"Yes?"
Sherlock replied:
"No."
Weinstein raised an eyebrow. "What now?"
"The sequel."
Weinstein stared at the wall. "Of course."
And somewhere in London, Penny was taking the cucumber home.
The cucumber had been purchased for personal use.
Nobody cared to ask what sort of personal use.
The canteen was serving meat with mint sauce, chocolate sauce, a third unlabelled bottle that was not soy sauce but had clearly attended the same finishing school as Maggi, and a small jar of Marmite left out for reasons nobody would take responsibility for — a combination several members of staff quietly agreed was a more serious violation of natural law than anything in Case 1047. Weinstein, calling from Munich and catching sight of a photograph Penny had unwisely sent him, wanted to know what, precisely, Marmite was, and why it was black, and why anyone would do that to toast on purpose. Nobody could produce an answer that satisfied him, largely because none of them had ever considered the question settled enough to need one. Mrs Marsh ate hers without comment, Marmite included, which everyone found more alarming than the sauces.
The universe, meanwhile, continued to curve.
As it had every right to. Obviously.

Rule: Pending Geometry is not a punchline. It is the correct drawer.

-> Lesson for this chapter: 16 - Capstone: The Clock Was Lying
EPILOGUE
The Relativistic Investigation Bureau Discovers That 
Solving One Mystery Is Merely an Invitation to Acquire Another

The Bureau had closed Case 1047. Looking back, the email, the triad, the seventeens, the cucumber, and Marsh's drawer had been aiming at the same drawer all along.
He recognised the shape of it, finally: he had spent seventeen years doing to his own feelings exactly what Mrs Marsh's system had done to Case 1047 — filing an unresolved event under the wrong drawer and calling it closed.
Derek still saw his face on the floor when he blinked — and Sophie in a doorway he had never let her open. He did not know which memories had been edited to keep her out.
Penny kept the photograph in her bag, face-down—not to hide the image, but to keep Sophie from seeing herself in a doorway she never opened. Some evidence must remain sealed to stay evidence.
Mrs Marsh knocked once, entered without waiting for a reply, and set a single sheet of paper on Derek's desk.
"The singularity," she reported, "is still not registered to my building. I checked twice. Also, Case 1047 is in Pending Geometry where it always belonged — Exhibit A, not a punchline. The cucumber can remain Exhibit B. I am not asking what Exhibit C was for."
She left before anyone could thank her. Mr Pendleton followed almost immediately, clipboard in hand. "Whichever form is required," he announced, "it is not my form."
"Which form is it, then?" Derek asked.
"Unknown. But it is definitely not mine."
He left, apparently satisfied.
The frosted glass door caught Derek's eye on his way past. NO MURDERS BEFORE 10 A.M. WITHOUT AN APPOINTMENT. Case 1047 had arrived at 9:17. He decided the sign had been correct after all — there had never been a murder before ten, because there had never been a murder at all.
The telephone rang once more.
Trevor answered, listened, then held it away from his ear. "Julius Barbarian," he reported, "declines to comment, on the grounds that comment implies a timeline, and he is not prepared to commit to one."
He hung up. Nobody was surprised.
Derek therefore allowed himself one dangerous thought. Perhaps it was over.
He sat for a while without doing anything useful, which counted.
That evening Sophie rang about homework and complained that time was drawn as an arrow in her textbook. Derek talked too long about nothing useful. Then, almost as an afterthought, she mentioned the notebook — the one she'd started for the clocks, the one with a number plate written under the four-minute mark in her own careful hand. "The car's stopped coming," she remarked, in the tone of someone reporting a mildly interesting weather change. "The one that used to park by the gates. I looked it up, by the way. It's registered to the Bureau." Derek went very still. "You looked it up." "Obviously. You weren't going to tell me." He considered lying, decided the year had used up his quota, and admitted that yes, he'd asked Penny to have someone watch the school, quietly, the week the photograph arrived, and hadn't told her because he hadn't wanted her to know how frightened he'd actually been. Sophie was quiet for a moment. "You should have just said." "I know." "I'd have put it in the notebook properly, instead of guessing." That, Derek reflected afterwards, was as close as either of them had come all year to saying something and meaning the harder thing underneath it. After she hung up he sat with the silent brass clock and did not write a word — afraid a sentence might edit her.
He thought about never would, and hadn't happened yet — close enough to bruise.
The mystery had been a noun in the wrong drawer. This story could only have happened here.
He left the thought alone.
For three days, nothing happened. Derek found he could live with that.
On the second day, Trevor stood. "Tea?"
"Yes."
"Milk?"
"No."
"Sugar?"
"One."
Trevor walked towards the kitchen. Derek did not call after him. One sugar remained one sugar. It was the same order, word for word, as the morning this had all begun — except that morning he'd changed his mind halfway to the door and called out "Two," because a man who thinks he might be dead by lunchtime is entitled to a second sugar. Today he let one stand. Nobody's death was scheduled for tomorrow. It was, Derek reflected, the most ordinary exchange the Bureau had produced in a fortnight, and he intended to enjoy every unremarkable second of it.
Penny, meanwhile, was planning a holiday, which after the week they'd had she considered less a luxury than a filing requirement. She hadn't told Derek the actual reason, and didn't intend to: since Tuesday she had carried a photograph of his corpse face-down in her own bag, had rehearsed, more than once and in more detail than she'd admit, what she would say to Sophie if the geometry ever stopped cooperating — and she had done all of it standing up, in the office, on schedule, because somebody had to be the one who didn't fall apart while the men argued about reference frames. She wanted Greece specifically because it was as far from a filing cabinet as she could get without actually resigning: whitewashed houses leaning companionably into blue, a bed and breakfast with a terrace and no view of London whatsoever, retsina she fully intended to pretend to enjoy, and enough grape leaves and olives to constitute a personality. "And technically," she added, showing Weinstein the itinerary with the satisfaction of someone who had done the maths purely to annoy him, "the flight itself will leave me very slightly older than everyone who stayed home. Not enough to notice. Enough to mention." Weinstein confirmed, with visible reluctance, that this was correct — a few tens of billionths of a second, the going rate for spending a week rather further from the ground — and complained that of all the people in his life to grasp general relativity unprompted, it had to be the one planning to spend a week doing nothing with it. "I'm not doing nothing," Penny said. "I'm doing nothing, further up than you are, which technically counts."
Tabitha had rung that morning, ostensibly to ask whether Gucci had left a lead at the Bureau. She did not ask, in as many words, whether Derek was still alive, but she stayed on the line four minutes longer than a lost lead required, and Derek let her. "Tell Trevor the dog misses structure," she said eventually, in the tone of someone changing the subject before the subject could change her. "And tell yourself whatever you need to, about why you kept me talking." She rang off before he could answer that, which he privately considered good tactics. He hadn't had an answer ready.
Trevor noticed, with the weary resignation of a man updating a spreadsheet he had hoped to retire, that Derek's office door had started closing rather more often than the paperwork strictly required. The evidence sat outside it: a small dog, patient as a filed report, waiting on the corridor carpet. "Gucci's here again," he told Penny, in the tone of a man logging an observation rather than announcing a discovery. Penny didn't look up from her itinerary. "Door open or closed?" "Closed." "Then you know the rule." Trevor knew the rule. Derek had written it. He did not knock, did not call out, and did not stand in the corridor shouting anyone's name, on the grounds that the Bureau had tried all three before and none of them had ever once worked. He did, however, resolve to buy the dog a proper bed, on the grounds that even a voluntary stakeout deserved better furniture.
The Louvre guard was never identified. Weinstein made a few enquiries, of the sort that involved knowing someone who knew someone, and reported back that the museum's own staffing records showed no such person on duty that day, in that uniform, at that hour. Derek filed this exactly where it belonged — next to the singularity not registered to Mrs Marsh's building — and declined, on principle, to lose sleep over a worldline that had already demonstrated it kept its own schedule.
Nobody ever worked out who had sent Tabitha that message from Trevor's number, or how they'd known to send it eleven minutes before a train that, officially, did not exist pulled into a platform that also did not exist. Trevor checked his phone's sent folder obsessively for a week and found nothing. Penny suggested filing it under Pending Geometry, alongside the singularity and the cucumber. Derek suspected, without being able to prove it, that this particular drawer was going to need reopening sooner rather than later.
Nor did anyone establish what would actually have happened had Weinstein reached the empty station first — only that two separate warnings, arriving by two entirely different routes — one buried in a case file, one sent from a phone that swore it hadn't — had gone to some trouble to make sure he didn't. Weinstein found this considerably more alarming than the geometry that had produced it, and said so, at length, to colleagues who had stopped finding it alarming several near-misses ago. Derek's own theory — that somewhere a version of the case existed in which nobody had listened, and the whole point of the warning was to make sure they never met it — was, he privately admitted, unprovable, uncomfortable, and almost certainly correct. He filed it under Pending Geometry and left the drawer shut.
Derek never worked out how a note in his own handwriting had ended up inside Case 1047, warning him to keep Trevor away from a clock neither of them had found yet, in a hand he had no memory of using. He filed the question next to the others, on the grounds that a man who already owned one photograph of himself face-down on a floor was not obliged to go looking for a second mystery involving his own handwriting. Trevor, for his part, remained unconvinced he'd been fairly represented, and spent the rest of his professional life approaching every clock in the building with the particular caution of a man who had once been warned off one by name.
Nor could he explain, even to himself, the man he'd glimpsed sitting at his own desk that first week — old, grey-haired, gone the second time he looked — beyond noting that the chair had definitely been occupied and was then definitely not, which was, by that stage of the case, barely worth mentioning. He filed it under the same heading as the singularity and the Louvre guard, and didn't mention it to Trevor, on the grounds that Trevor already had one clock-based grievance and did not require a second.
The woman on the Underground platform went unidentified too — she'd known, correctly and without being told, that he was doing both kinds of relativity at once. He looked for her, in the loose, deniable way a man looks for someone he has decided not to be looking for, and never found her again. He filed her, eventually, next to the old man in the dark suit — not because the two cases had anything in common, but because both drawers were labelled Probably Irrelevant, and he had learned, by then, exactly how much that label was worth.
Weinstein's own missing minute — his Munich clock had stopped at 11:16, a full sixty seconds before the Bureau's did in London — never received a tidier explanation than the general shape of the geometry itself. He filed it as a data point rather than a separate mystery, on the grounds that a physicist who chased down every disagreeing clock would simply never leave the house.
The cucumber that had appeared in Derek's own hand on the platform — conjured from nowhere, its provenance never established — joined the same drawer. He ate it, eventually, on the grounds that evidence with no ongoing investigative value was, strictly speaking, lunch.
Sophie's own brass clock — the one in her father's study, permanently and precisely four minutes fast — never made it into any official filing at all, mostly because nobody thought to ask a twelve-year-old for her evidence. She kept testing it anyway, on the grounds that someone in the family should keep a proper notebook, and filed the omission under the same heading her father would have used, had he known to: Pending Geometry.
One small matter had, admittedly, been resolved. The initials on the clock — LGV — turned out, when Penny finally thought to ask a Frenchman, to mean nothing more mysterious than Ligne à Grande Vitesse: the stamp of an ordinary French high-speed railway line, most likely off a piece of decommissioned rolling stock. Penny was smug about it in person, at length, over the phone to Munich. She had, after all, guessed "a railway designation" within the first five minutes; Trevor had guessed Local Gravitational Vector, and nobody had let him forget it. "A railway designation," Weinstein repeated, with the particular weariness of a man who had built an entire theoretical framework around three letters that turned out to mean nothing more than fast train. "That's it. That's the whole geometry." "That's it," Penny agreed, not unkindly. Trevor, overhearing, announced that he stood by Local Gravitational Vector as the more interesting answer, correctness notwithstanding, and nobody had the heart to argue with him twice in one week.
He filed actual, ordinary cases under actual, ordinary categories, and none of them concerned him personally. Sophie texted twice about nothing in particular, which he had learned to recognise as the highest compliment she had available. Penny stopped watching him sideways when she thought he wasn't looking. He allowed himself, cautiously, to believe it might simply be over.
On the Friday, Penny ordered curry for the whole Bureau, on the grounds that a team that had spent a week arguing about whether the dead could still receive post deserved at least one meal that didn't come out of a vending machine. They ate it straight from the containers, at desks still faintly haunted by chalk diagrams of spacetime. Derek discovered, somewhere around his second popadom, that he was extremely and uncomplicatedly glad to be alive — not in the abstract, philosophical way the case had spent every day of that week making him consider it, but in the specific, practical way that meant he was eating a chicken jalfrezi instead of, as had at various points seemed likely, pushing up daisies. He mentioned this to no one, on the grounds that saying it aloud would have obliged someone to make a joke about it, and he wanted, just this once, to enjoy being not dead in silence.
Weinstein, predictably, ruined the silence. He remarked, through a mouthful of naan, that the curry had taken forty minutes to arrive by the delivery driver's watch and thirty-nine by the Bureau's, on account of a set of traffic lights that had briefly convinced two clocks they disagreed about the order of events. Trevor asked, not unreasonably, whether the delivery firm could simply hire a tachyon and skip the traffic altogether. Weinstein said that a tachyon would arrive before it had been dispatched, refuse the order retroactively, and leave the Bureau owing a refund to a version of itself that had never placed one — which, he added, was a worse system than the Northern line, and that was saying something. Nobody found any of this as fascinating as Weinstein did. Derek found it, on reflection, exactly fascinating enough — the universe was still out there disagreeing with itself about details, same as ever, and for once none of the disagreements involved anyone's death. He decided he could live with a universe like that.
Later that week, Derek took Sophie shopping for school shoes, on the grounds that fatherhood, like the universe, occasionally demanded ordinary maintenance. The department store's lift stuttered between the second and third floors, held, and dropped an inch with a lurch that emptied both their stomachs at once. Sophie gripped the handrail. "Are we falling?"
Derek considered this with more care than the question strictly required. "Technically," he said, "there's no experiment you could run inside this lift that would tell you whether we're falling freely or just floating in a lift far from any gravity at all. It's called the equivalence principle. Chapter Six of my life, roughly." The lift shuddered and resumed its climb, gravity reasserting its usual bureaucratic authority. "Turns out it also can't tell you whether the culprit is a cable or a motor that hates Tuesdays."
Sophie considered this with the weary patience of a twelve-year-old whose father had spent a week investigating a filing error. "Dad. It's a lift."
"Everything's a lift, if you corner it correctly."
"That's not a real sentence."
"It's a real sentence. It's just doing several jobs at once." The doors opened onto Ladies' Footwear. Derek stepped out first, mostly to confirm the floor was still there.
Trevor took to walking home through Holland Park after dark, on the theory — unproven, and never to be tested again — that a man who had personally witnessed the collapse of simultaneity ought to be able to handle an unlit footpath in Notting Hill. Somewhere near the bandstand, a stranger on a bicycle whistled at him and pedalled off before Trevor could construct a suitable retort. By the time the reply arrived, fully formed and reasonably witty, the stranger had already left whatever light cone might have delivered it to him — which Trevor decided, generously, made it a problem of physics rather than wit. He settled, a full block later, for muttering it to the empty park instead. "I'm flattered, obviously," he informed a duck, "and also extremely cold." The duck did not dignify this with a response, which Trevor felt was probably fair.
Three days later, the brass clock reappeared on Derek's desk, exactly as before, exactly on time by nobody's clock but its own. Nobody reached for a notebook. After the week they'd had, some things no longer needed writing down; they had simply become family shorthand — the kind their grandmother would have recognised, and approved of, in her own alarming way. Derek looked at the clock for a while. Then he did not open a file. He put the kettle on instead.
That evening Sophie rang about nothing in particular. He was starting to trust that most, now — not the calls with news in them, but the ones without. The next morning, Trevor asked if he wanted tea. "One sugar," Derek said, and meant it, and did not change his mind.
Rule: Pending Geometry is the correct drawer. Some cases end not with an arrest, but with a kettle.

-> If you skipped the course: Lesson 16 - Capstone: The Clock Was Lying


COURSE
Spacetime for Investigators
A Short Course from the Relativistic Investigation Bureau
This is not decorative back matter. It is the training course the Bureau pretends its staff have already passed. 
By the end you should be able to explain the central ideas yourself — allegedly — and see why Case 1047 could only happen in a universe with reference frames, proper time, and causal structure. The novel was the exam. This is the worked solution, with jokes still attached.
Prerequisite: curiosity, arithmetic, a little algebra, and the willingness to let 'obviously' lose an argument.
How each lesson works: 
Learning objective — what you should be able to do. 
Teaching — the physics under the joke, in ordinary language. Lesson checkpoint 
Keep this / Common trap / Quick test. If you can answer the Quick test cold, you learned something rather than merely surviving the chapter.
Course rule: separate what an observer measures from what the theory requires is invariant. When a metaphor helps, keep it. When the metaphor starts making predictions the equations forbid — throw it away. 
Quick definition, since the word does a lot of work from here on: invariant means unchanged when you switch coordinate systems. What an observer measures can change from frame to frame; an invariant quantity does not.
The cucumber is an excellent laboratory. It is also food.
Three more course rules, while we're being honest:
