A shared now is a convenience, not a fact. If a plot point requires everyone in London and Munich to mean the same Tuesday, the plot point is Newtonian, and the book has already said so.
v > c in a transform is a failed assumption about events, not a discovery about light. Arrest the premise.
Where this book says "not settled," it means Barbarian, holography as cosmology, and emergent spacetime. It does not mean the interval, the light cone, or the equivalence principle are waiting for a committee.

WORKSHOP
The Twin Paradox
 (do this before Lesson 1)
Workshop problem: One twin boards a very fast spaceship, travels out and back near the speed of light, and returns to find their sibling considerably older. Both twins, the objection runs, saw the other one moving — so shouldn't each expect the other to have aged less?
It cannot, and it isn't. The two twins do not take the same route through spacetime between their parting and their reunion — only the travelling twin's path bends, out and back, while the twin on the sofa travels a straight, unaccelerated line between the same two events. 
That is the entire resolution: proper time depends on the path taken, not merely on relative motion, and the straight path between two events always racks up more of it than any bent one. Turning around is what makes the paths different — it is not, by itself, what makes the travelling twin younger. There is a genuine fact about which twin aged less, and it belongs to whoever took the bent path.
This is the same idea as the invariant spacetime interval you are about to meet in 
Lesson 2, wearing a different coat. Proper time, like ordinary distance, depends on the path you actually take. 
Drive the direct road between two towns and you burn less petrol than the driver who takes the scenic route, even though both of you started and finished in the same two places. 
Nobody finds that paradoxical. 
The twin paradox runs on the same idea, but backwards: it's the scenic-route twin — the one who peels off toward the speed of light and comes back — who logs the fewer years, the way the direct-road driver logs the less petrol. The twin who stays on the direct, unaccelerated road through spacetime is the one who arrives having racked up more time, not less. 
Workshop result: proper time depends on the path taken. 

The same idea reappears as the invariant interval in Lesson 2, and as the other Derek problem in the novel. 

Trevor still wants it filed under Pending Geometry. Mrs Marsh still refuses to stamp the form. 

Proceed to Lesson 1.


Lesson 1
Special Relativity Is Rude
Module A · Foundations of Special Relativity · Case link: Chapters 1-3. Learning objective: State Einstein's two postulates and explain why constant c forces space and time to transform together. The first thing to understand about Special Relativity is that it does not begin with the statement:
"Everything is relative."
That is a popular summary and is also rather misleading. Einstein's Special Relativity rests on two principles: The laws of physics are the same in all inertial reference frames.
The speed of light in vacuum is the same for all inertial observers. It's the second one that causes all the trouble. Suppose you are sitting on a distant train.
You throw a ball forward at 10 m/s. Someone standing on the platform sees the ball moving at roughly: train speed + 10 m/s.
Perfectly ordinary. Now replace the ball with light.
Classical intuition replied that the person on the platform should measure:
train speed + speed of light. 
Nature responds:
No.
The light still travels at exactly c. 
This forces us to abandon the old assumptions about space and time. And that is where things become interesting.
Here is the classic way to see why — not just that it is true, but why it comes out as an actual number. 
Imagine a clock built from a single photon of light, bouncing straight up and down between two mirrors a fixed distance apart. Each round trip is one 'tick.' 
Nothing exotic: light, two mirrors, geometry.
Now put that clock on the mysterious train, moving past a platform at speed v. 
From Trevor's point of view, standing on the platform, the light doesn't simply go straight up and down — the train is moving, so the photon has to travel diagonally to keep up with the departing mirror, tracing a longer zigzag path for each tick.
Here is the part that actually causes the trouble. The speed of light does not change for Trevor either. 
Longer path, same speed, more time. That is the whole derivation. One tick of the train's clock, measured from the platform, simply takes longer than one tick measured on the train. 
Do the geometry properly — a right triangle, Pythagoras, nothing worse — and out drops exactly the same γ you are about to meet properly in the next chapter, without asking anyone to take the mathematics on faith.
Call one round trip of the train's own clock Δτ — proper time, measured by the clock that is actually there. With the mirrors a distance L apart, light covers 2L each tick, so:
Δτ = 2L / c
From the platform, that same tick takes longer — call it Δt — because the photon travels the diagonal, not the vertical. By the time the photon reaches the top mirror — half the round trip, at time Δt/2 — the train has advanced by vΔt/2, while the photon has climbed the full height L. Pythagoras supplies the hypotenuse:
(cΔt/2)² = L² + (vΔt/2)²
Substitute L = cΔτ/2, and multiply both sides by 4:
c²Δt² = c²Δτ² + v²Δt²
Move the v²Δt² term to the left and factor out Δt²:
Δt² (c² — v²) = c²Δτ²
Divide both sides by (c² — v²):
Δt² = c²Δτ² / (c² — v²)
Divide top and bottom inside the fraction by c², then take the square root:
Δt = Δτ / √(1 — v²/c²)
Which is to say, Δt = γΔτ, where:
γ = 1 / √(1 — v²/c²)
One geometric fact — light does not speed up just because you are already moving — and Pythagoras does the rest. Nobody had to take γ's word for it.

The photon takes a longer path in the clock but still travels at speed c — so the tick takes longer.
This is why time dilation is not a story about clocks getting confused. It is what geometry does when one side of the triangle refuses to change length no matter how fast you are moving.
→ Glossary: Time Dilation
None of this is hypothetical. 
The satellites that make a phone's map work are moving fast enough, and sitting high enough out of Earth's gravity well, that both effects discussed in this book — Special Relativity's time dilation from speed, and General Relativity's time dilation from gravity — apply to their onboard clocks simultaneously, pulling in opposite directions. 
Engineers correct for it deliberately, every single day, or a phone's position would drift by several kilometres a day. The Bureau finds this less remarkable than most people do. The Bureau finds most things less remarkable than most people do.
The Bureau's brass clock is not a satellite. It is doing a ruder job: displaying a label as if it were a tick.

Lesson checkpoint
Special Relativity starts with two postulates, and the constancy of light forces space and time to transform together.
"Relative" does not mean "anything goes." The spacetime interval and other invariant quantities remain the same.
→ Glossary: Spacetime Interval

Exit Form
If two inertial observers disagree about simultaneity, must one of their clocks be broken? No.
→ Glossary: Simultaneity

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 2


Lesson 2
Lorentz Finally Gets Involved
Module A · Foundations of Special Relativity · Case link: Chapter 3. Learning objective: Use the Lorentz transformation to relate coordinates between inertial frames and name at least one invariant.
→ Glossary: Lorentz Transformation
The mathematical machinery connecting two inertial observers is the Lorentz transformation. An inertial observer moves at a constant velocity without accelerating.
Let A describe an event using position (x) and time (t). B describes that same event using (x′) and (t′). If B moves to A's right at speed (v), and their coordinate origins coincide when both clocks read zero, then:
x′ = γ(x — vt)
t′ = γ(t — vx/c²)
Here c is the speed of light in vacuum, and γ is the Lorentz factor.
Do not panic. γ is merely a number that becomes increasingly annoying as your speed approaches the speed of light. The important thing is that space and time transform together.
Look at the time equation: B's time depends on both A's time and A's position. That position term is why two events happening at the same time in A's frame can happen at different times in B's.
This explains the diagram. Each coloured line shows what one observer considers to happen at the same time as Event 1.
A's teal line passes through both events. A says they happen at the same time.
B's red line passes through Event 1, but Event 2 lies below it. B says Event 2 happened earlier than Event 1.
Event 2 does not belong on the red line because B does not consider it simultaneous with Event 1. The coloured lines represent equal times, not paths that the events must follow.
This disagreement is not simply about light taking time to reach someone's eyes. It remains after each observer accounts for light travel time.
Different observers can therefore disagree about distances, elapsed times, and whether two separated events happened simultaneously.
Drag the velocity yourself and watch the red and teal lines tilt apart at the simultaneity demo.
They do not, however, disagree about everything.
A key invariant is the spacetime interval. For the one-dimensional motion used here:
→ Glossary: Spacetime Interval
s² = c²Δt² — Δx²
Here Δt is the time between the two events, and Δx is their separation in space. All inertial observers calculate the same value of s² for the same pair of events, provided they use the same sign convention.
The individual space and time measurements can change. This combination stays the same.
s² is what stays the same when the stationery changes. Case 1047 kept rewriting its fields because the Bureau was editing labels and calling the edits news. The interval does not care which drawer you stamp.
This chapter uses the timelike-positive convention — one of two equally valid ways to assign plus and minus signs to space and time in the interval formula. A later chapter uses the equally valid mostly-plus convention, where those signs are simply flipped throughout.


The first major lesson of relativity is this: 
Coordinates are descriptions of reality. The events they describe do not change when we change coordinates.
And that distinction will become increasingly important.


Lesson checkpoint
Lorentz transformations tell you how space and time coordinates change between inertial frames.
Do not add velocities using the old Newtonian rule. Ordinary velocity addition is only an approximation at low speeds. When speeds become a significant fraction of c.
<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 3

Exit Form
What stays the same for all inertial observers?
The speed of light in vacuum, and the spacetime interval between the same two events.
speed of light

Lesson 3
Time May Be Less Fundamental
Module A · Foundations of Special Relativity
Case link: Chapters 5 & 15. Learning objective: Distinguish coordinate time from proper time along a worldline; reject universal Newtonian now.
→ Glossary: Proper Time · Worldline
Proper time: two worldlines between the same two events accumulate different amounts of elapsed proper time. Fly an atomic clock on a plane and keep an identical one on the ground, and the difference is measurable, not hypothetical. Two effects act on the airborne clock at once, though, and they pull in opposite directions: its speed slows it, its extra height in Earth's weaker gravity speeds it up. Which one wins depends on the flight.
→ Glossary: GPS Correction
Julius Barbarian's claim that time does not exist is deliberately provocative. But there is serious physics hiding underneath the joke. In relativity, there is no single universal time shared by the entire universe.
Different observers can experience different amounts of proper time between events. Proper time is the time measured by a clock travelling along a particular worldline. 
So, instead of asking:
"What time is it everywhere?" 
relativity encourages us to ask:
"How much proper time elapsed along this particular path through spacetime?"
That is a much better question. 
And General Relativity goes further. 
Gravitational fields affect the rate at which clocks run relative to one another.
A strange clock deeper in a gravitational field can accumulate less proper time than one farther away. So, clocks do not merely measure some universal substance called Time. 
They measure something associated with their physical histories.
But notice what kind of claim that is. Relativity denies a single shared now across the universe. That part is mainstream, uncontested physics, settled since 1905. Barbarian is reaching for something further along the same road, and the two should not be filed under the same drawer.
His actual claim, spelled out in full, not the tea-time version: a clock does not measure a flowing substance called time. It compares one physical process against another — the swing of a pendulum against the turn of the Earth, the tick of a balance wheel against the fall of an apple. Take away every process in the universe, and there is nothing left over called "time" for a lone clock to still be measuring.
That is why, in Chapter 5, he does not answer Derek's question about the apple with a clock. He answers it with a second configuration. Two arrangements of the same table, and a relationship between them — not because he is being difficult, but because on his account that relationship is the only thing that was ever there. Call each arrangement a Now. On the relational view, reality is not a river of such moments; it is closer to a vast collection of them, most bearing no special relationship to their neighbours — a landscape, not a timeline.
If that is right, an honest question follows immediately: why does it feel like a river at all? Barbarian's answer is not that your memories are lies. It is that some configurations happen to contain something that looks exactly like a record of others — a photograph, a scar, a notebook Sophie kept for seventeen weeks. A configuration rich in such records is experienced as a moment with a past, whether or not anything actually flowed to get there. The Bureau, of all institutions, should recognise the danger in that: a record that looks like memory and a record that looks like evidence file the same way. Case 1047 opened because a filing system trusted a record over the geometry it was supposedly reporting on. Barbarian would say that was the mistake, not the murder.
None of this is settled physics the way proper time is settled physics. It is a live, disputed research programme, not a conclusion in a textbook. But the core move — stop assuming a flowing background and start asking what the honest relationships between configurations actually are — is the same move Special and General Relativity already made once. Barbarian just made it twice.
Unfortunately, he also has an alarming tendency to explain it while eating biscuits.
Lesson checkpoint
Relativity removes universal Newtonian time; each clock measures proper time along its own worldline.
1. Each observer measures the other's moving clock as ticking slower — a relationship between frames, not one clock being objectively slow.
2. A clock at lower gravity, e.g. on an aeroplane at 10 km flight height, gains compared to a clock on the ground — though its own speed cuts into that gain; the net result depends on both.
3. Relativity's "no universal now" and Barbarian's "time is not fundamental" are different claims — the first is settled physics, the second is a live research programme.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 4
Saying 'time is not real' goes beyond what relativity itself establishes. However, there are reasons to claim that.

Exit Form
Can two clocks follow different paths through spacetime and accumulate different proper times?
Yes.


Lesson 4
Enter General Relativity
Module B · Gravity as Geometry · Case link: Chapter 4. Learning objective: Explain gravity as spacetime geometry and state what the equivalence principle does and does not allow. Special Relativity deals with inertial frames and flat spacetime. But gravity causes a problem.
→ Glossary: Equivalence Principle
Einstein noticed something wonderfully simple:
If you are inside a freely falling lift, you cannot locally distinguish falling freely in a gravitational field from drifting weightlessly in empty space, far from any gravity. This is the equivalence principle. 
That suggested something profound: 
Perhaps gravity isn't fundamentally a conventional force. 
Perhaps it is geometry.
General Relativity makes precisely that leap. 
Matter and energy affect spacetime geometry. 
Objects then move through that geometry.
The famous slogan is: 
Matter tells spacetime how to curve; 
Spacetime tells matter how to move. 
That is considerably more accurate than blaming gravity on the apple.
Although the apple remains available for consultation.

Imagine dropping a ball inside a lift. You hold the ball at some height and release it.
Everything is normal if the lift is at rest.
If the ball and the lift are both falling together, at the same rate, relative to the observer, the ball simply floats.
Now imagine the lift is far away from any gravitational field and accelerating upward. The ball now moves downward towards the floor — just as it would in an ordinary gravitational field.
Notice the phrase "far away from any gravitational field" in that last sentence. It is doing a great deal of work.
Real gravity — the kind that comes from an actual planet — is not perfectly uniform. It points towards the centre of the Earth, which means "down" at Heathrow and "down" in Munich are very slightly different directions. Two balls dropped a mile apart do not fall on exactly parallel paths; they fall very slightly towards each other, both aiming at the same distant centre. An accelerating lift cannot reproduce that. Acceleration pushes everything inside it in exactly the same direction, with exactly the same strength, everywhere in the lift. Real gravity does not.
That difference is the tell, and it has a name: a tidal effect. Locally — in a lift small enough, for a short enough time — acceleration and gravity are genuinely indistinguishable, which is the whole content of the equivalence principle. Stretch the region large enough to notice the two balls converging, and the disguise fails.
→ Glossary: Tidal Force
This is why General Relativity cannot simply be "acceleration, but everywhere." A single accelerating frame can cancel gravity inside one small lift. No single frame cancels it in every lift on Earth at once, because the leftover — what no accelerating frame can absorb — is the actual curvature of spacetime. Chapter 14 puts Derek in a corridor built entirely out of that leftover: two clocks agreeing perfectly nearby and disagreeing badly forty-seven metres away, for exactly this reason.
→ Glossary: Curvature
Pound and Rebka, 1960, Harvard. A gamma-ray source and an absorber separated by 22.5 metres of tower. The frequency shift matched gravitational time dilation to about ten percent. Height is cheap. Disagreement between honest clocks is not. The Bureau's anomaly is larger than a tower. That is the interesting part, not a licence to invent a new theory before lunch.

  

Lesson checkpoint
General Relativity replaces flat spacetime with a dynamical geometry that responds to matter and energy.
<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 5

The equivalence principle is local.
A sufficiently small freely falling laboratory can mimic an inertial frame, but tidal effects reveal curvature over larger regions.

Exit Form
What tells you that a gravitational field cannot be removed everywhere just by choosing an accelerating frame?
Tidal effects.

Lesson 5
The Apple
Module B · Gravity as Geometry
Case link: Chapter 6.
Learning objective: Contrast Newtonian force with geodesic free fall
→ Glossary: Geodesic

Say when Newton remains an excellent approximation.
Newton vs. Einstein: the same falling apple, explained as a force or as motion along a geodesic.
Newton answered: There is a gravitational force.
Einstein eventually answered: The object is following a natural path through curved spacetime. 
That natural path is called a geodesic. 

On the surface of the Earth, an orbiting spacecraft is actually in free fall.
It is not being "held up" by a mysterious absence of gravity.
It is following its geodesic. 
The astronauts inside feel weightless because they are falling together with the spacecraft. 
The surface of the Earth prevents us from following our natural free-fall path.
The floor pushes upward on our feet. 
Which is why standing still is, in a relativistic sense, rather more strenuous than orbiting Earth.
The floor is what keeps you off your geodesic. Derek's lift with Sophie, later, is the same joke told at department-store volume.
The apple was therefore not really falling because gravity "pulled" it downward in the Newtonian sense.
It was following the geometry. 
Newton wasn't wrong. 
He had simply discovered an extraordinarily good approximation to something deeper.
Watch a particle trace its geodesic around an adjustable mass, with no force pushing it anywhere, at the curvature demo.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 6

Lesson checkpoint
Newtonian gravity is an extraordinarily successful approximation; General Relativity gives the deeper geometric description.
Do not say Newton was simply wrong. Newtonian gravity remains an excellent approximation when relativistic corrections are small.

Exit Form
Why do astronauts feel weightless in orbit?
They and their spacecraft are in free fall.


Lesson 6
Singularities
Module B · Gravity as Geometry
Case link: Chapters 7-8.
Learning objective: Define a singularity as a breakdown of the classical spacetime description, not merely 'very strong gravity.'
→ Glossary: Singularity

Here Derek makes an important distinction. A singularity is not simply a place where gravity is very strong. 
That would be too easy.
A singularity represents a breakdown of the classical spacetime description. One useful way of expressing this is through geodesic incompleteness. Normally, you should be able to keep extending that path. At a singularity, the classical theory can tell you that the path simply cannot be continued in the expected way. 
→ Glossary: Geodesic
Imagine following a freely falling observer's worldline. 
→ Glossary: Worldline
 
Singularity: a worldline that cannot be extended — a breakdown of the classical description, not a place.

This tells us something profound: The theory has reached its limit. 
This is why physicists don't normally say:
"There's a really strong gravitational field over there, so that's a singularity."
They ask whether the mathematical structure of spacetime itself becomes pathological. 
And that distinction matters enormously when we discuss black holes and the early universe. 
A singularity is not necessarily an object sitting somewhere waiting to be photographed.
It may be telling us that General Relativity is incomplete at that point. 
Which is physicist-speak for: 
"The equations have stopped being helpful. Someone should invent some better ones."
If the Underground appears to end a path, the honest question is whether the description failed — not whether London has installed a black hole between stations.
Two related ideas are worth naming here, though neither is this story's actual diagnosis: a white hole is a black hole's time-reverse, a region that can only emit and never absorb, and a wormhole is a hypothetical tunnel connecting two distant regions of spacetime. Both are solutions on paper. Neither has ever been observed.


Lesson checkpoint
A singularity is best understood as a failure or incompleteness of the classical spacetime description. Black holes contain singularities (classically) hidden behind event horizons. White holes are their time-reverse: regions that can emit but not absorb. Wormholes are hypothetical tunnels connecting distant regions of spacetime.
→ Glossary: Event Horizon · Wormhole

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 7

In this story, the Underground's stranger geometry occasionally behaves as if it were flirting with all three: 
a breakdown of the classical description (singularity-like), 
a one-way refusal to let certain paths return (white-hole-like), and 
a shortcut that should not exist (wormhole-like). 
The Bureau's official position is that the Underground is merely having a bad day.



Exit Form

What should a singularity make you suspect?
That the classical theory has reached its limit.


Lesson 7
Why SR and GR Don't Fight
Module B · Gravity as Geometry
Case link: Chapters 8 & 11
Learning objective: Explain SR as the local freely falling limit of GR; identify when frame-stitching, not physics, is the problem.
Special Relativity as the local limit of General Relativity: any sufficiently small freely falling patch looks flat.
General Relativity contains Special Relativity locally. In a sufficiently small region of spacetime, a freely falling observer can construct a local inertial frame. Within that small region, physics looks like Special Relativity.
So: SR = local flat-spacetime physics while
GR = the larger theory describing curved spacetime and how those local frames fit together. This is why the theories can coexist perfectly well.
Here is the intuition without the mathematics. Stand on a football pitch and the ground looks flat. Look at the whole planet from orbit and it is obviously a sphere. Nothing about the Earth changed between those two views — only how much of it you were looking at. A small enough patch of a curved surface always looks flat, to whatever precision you happen to be measuring with. Spacetime behaves the same way: zoom in on a small enough patch, for a short enough time, and General Relativity's curved geometry is indistinguishable from Special Relativity's flat one. Zoom back out and the curvature reappears — not because SR broke, but because the patch was never the whole pitch.
The cold rain going sideways is the book refusing to stay in the small patch. That is a tidal hint, not a licence to throw SR away.
→ Glossary: Curvature
This is the same fact Lesson 4 called a tidal effect, seen from the other direction: local flatness is what you get before the tides show up.
→ Glossary: Tidal Force

The Underground was therefore not going to discover that SR was "wrong."
It was more likely to reveal that Derek had chosen a sufficiently inconvenient coordinate system. This is an important distinction.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 8



Lesson checkpoint
Special Relativity is the local limit of General Relativity in freely falling frames.
GR does not make SR obsolete. It contains local SR while describing how local frames fit together in curved spacetime.

Exit Form
Why can a small freely falling laboratory behave approximately like Special Relativity?
Because curvature effects become negligible over a sufficiently small region.


Lesson 8
The Door
Module C · Labels, Paths, Experiments
Case link: Chapter 9. 
Learning objective: Treat coordinates as labels; decide whether two number-sets describe one event or two.

Coordinate-dependent descriptions: the same door, the same physics, described differently by two coordinate systems.
It is also an excellent metaphor for something real. Physics is full of boundaries between descriptions. One observer assigns an object one set of coordinates; another assigns different coordinates to the same object. The numbers change because the observers organise their descriptions differently. Nothing has to happen to the object itself.
The picture makes this concrete. Derek chooses one place as his zero point and labels the marked location at the door "four metres." Penny chooses a zero point two metres closer and labels that same location "two metres." Both labels work. Neither person has misplaced the door. They have simply started counting from different places.

The underlying physics doesn't change merely because the description changes: the door's position label depends on the chosen origin, but whether that same door is open at the event being described does not.
If changing coordinates could change the door itself, Derek could quite legitimately say:
"The door is open in my coordinate system."
And Penny could reply:
"Yes, but it's closed in mine."
Trevor would then require a slap.
But changing coordinates cannot do that. It cannot turn the handle, swing the hinges, or put a wooden panel across an empty doorway. Those are physical changes. They require something to happen to the door, not merely something to happen to the numbers used to describe it.
Keep that distinction in reserve. Nothing in this lesson says two events already known to be separate can be merged into one by relabelling them — and Case 1047's eventual resolution is not a relabelling of one door. It is a claim that two apparently separate events were never two to begin with. That is a far stronger, and far rarer, claim than anything in this lesson, and when it arrives, it will need to earn it.
The same discipline applies when the coordinates describe time as well as space. Before comparing two labels, we must ask what each label means and which event it identifies. Different labels can identify the same event. A disagreement between labels is not, by itself, a disagreement about what physically happened.
Derek and Penny can therefore agree completely about the door while writing different numbers beside it. Once they explain their coordinate choices, they can translate between the descriptions.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 9

The description changes; the physical situation does not.

Lesson checkpoint
Coordinates are descriptions. Changing coordinates does not by itself change the physical situation.
A metaphor about doors, maps or languages is not itself a coordinate transformation.

Exit Form
If two coordinate systems assign different numbers to the same event, does that automatically mean two different events occurred?
No.


Lesson 9
Relativistic Travel
Module C · Labels, Paths, Experiments · Case link: Chapter 10. Learning objective: Compute the idea of proper-time difference along unequal worldlines (twin paradox logic). The Lorentz transformation connects the space and time
→ Glossary: Lorentz Transformation · Worldline
coordinates of the same events in the Earth and spaceship frames.

Picture a spaceship travelling at relativistic speed. Its clock measures its own proper time. An observer on Earth measures a different elapsed time between the same events.
→ Glossary: Proper Time

The Lorentz factor, γ, stays extremely close to 1 at everyday speeds and becomes large as speed approaches c — the speed of light. The transformation describes time dilation and length contraction along the direction of motion.
→ Glossary: Time Dilation
But remember:
Nobody experiences their own clock as running slowly. Your own clock always appears normal to you. Different worldlines between the same departure and reunion events can accumulate different amounts of proper time.
If Derek flies to another star at enormous speed and returns, he might have aged considerably less than people who stayed on Earth. His watch did not malfunction. It correctly measured his proper time.
Put a number on it. At a steady γ = 5/3 (roughly 0.8c, chosen because the arithmetic behaves), a ten-year round trip by Earth's clocks costs Derek only six years of his own proper time. The missing four years are not lost, stolen, or hidden in a drawer marked Pending Geometry — they simply never accumulated, because his path through spacetime between the same two events was shorter in exactly the sense that matters.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 10

And this is why booking Ryanair is not actually an established method of achieving relativistic time travel.
The airline has yet to update its terms and conditions accordingly.
Hafele and Keating, 1971: caesium clocks flown east and west around the world, compared with clocks that stayed home. Special and general corrections both showed up. The travelling clocks were not confused. They had taken different paths. Derek's other-Derek problem is this experiment wearing a raincoat.

Lesson checkpoint
Time is what a clock actually accumulates along its worldline; different worldlines can contain different amounts of proper time.
Time dilation is not a malfunction of the travelling clock, and the traveller does not watch their own clock run abnormally slowly.

Exit Form
If Derek travels fast and reunites with Earth, can he have aged less than someone who stayed behind?
Yes.


Lesson 10
The Cucumber
Module C · Labels, Paths, Experiments
Case link: Chapters 11-12 & 14
Learning objective: Apply length contraction correctly; use an ordinary object as a control experiment between frames.

Length contraction: a moving object's measured length shrinks along its direction of motion; its rest length does not.
The ordinary cucumber is useful because it is boring. Boring objects are excellent physics experiments.
If a cucumber moves rapidly relative to an observer, Special Relativity predicts that its measured length along the direction of motion is contracted by a factor of 1/γ. The observer measures both endpoints at the same time in their own frame.
The cucumber itself does not feel squashed. Its length measured in its own rest frame remains unchanged.

Again:
Different observers assign different measurements. 
But the underlying relativistic structure is consistent. This is precisely why the cucumber makes such a useful running joke.
It is an ordinary object. The same laws of relativity apply to it as to everything else.
The cucumber has, however, declined to comment.
Length contraction is a statement about two ends measured at the same time in your frame. If you measure the front now and the back later, you have not measured a length. You have measured a muddle. The Bureau made that muddle with clocks and called it a murder.
This is probably for the best.
<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 11


Lesson checkpoint
Length contraction affects the measured length parallel to relative motion.
The moving cucumber does not discover itself physically squashed.
Its rest-frame length remains L₀.

Exit Form
If the ordinary cucumber is 20 cm long in its rest frame and γ = 2, what length does an observer measure along the direction of motion due to contraction?
10 cm.


Lesson 11
The Hologram
Module D · Dual Descriptions
Case link: Chapter 12
Learning objective: State the area-law clue for black-hole entropy and what holographic duality does and does not claim.

Derek's filing cabinet was full. This was an established fact, supported by the drawer that no longer shut.
Ordinarily, the remedy would be a larger cabinet. Double its width, height and depth, and there would be eight times as much room for files. Trevor would fill it in a fortnight, but that was a staffing problem.
Black-hole physics suggests a stranger kind of accounting. When physicists calculate a black hole's entropy, the quantity that matters is the area of its horizon. That unexpected result helped inspire the holographic principle: a theory describing a region with gravity may have an equivalent description on a boundary with one fewer spatial dimension.
→ Glossary: Holographic Principle
The filing cabinet is an analogy. Please do not attempt the experiment with the Bureau's furniture. 
What the black holes tell us: A black hole's event horizon is the boundary beyond which light cannot escape to distant observers. It is not a solid shell.
→ Glossary: Event Horizon
Bekenstein and Hawking connected the horizon's area to entropy. Here, entropy measures how many microscopic possibilities are compatible with the black hole's large-scale properties. More precisely, it depends on the logarithm of that number (roughly: how many digits the number has, not the number itself, which is why entropy grows far more slowly than the raw count of possibilities). It can also be expressed as an amount of information in bits.
For a spherical horizon, area is 4πR². Double the radius, and the area becomes four times as large. The Bekenstein-Hawking entropy therefore becomes four times as large too.
An ordinary ball with twice the radius has eight times the volume. This contrast is the clue: the black hole's entropy follows an area rule.
Penny would describe this as being charged for storage by the size of the wrapping paper. Derek would ask whether the wrapping paper was included.
What information means here: "Information" does not mean that someone has printed a list of everything that fell in on the outside of the black hole. It concerns the microscopic distinctions needed to specify a physical state.
Think of the difference between knowing that a room contains warm air and specifying the detailed state of that air. Many microscopic arrangements can fit the same broad description. Entropy quantifies that multiplicity.
The area rule helped motivate the idea that gravity limits the independent information needed to describe a region in an unexpectedly economical way. Turning that insight into a general rule requires care about which surfaces and regions are being compared.
It does not give Trevor permission to store the accounts on the office wallpaper.

The holographic principle: information about a volume can be encoded on its lower-dimensional boundary.
How to read the picture
The upper panel shows the black-hole clue. The larger horizon has twice the radius and four times the entropy. Its grid is a drawing aid, not a photograph of tiny storage compartments.
The lower panel shows the next idea. The bulk is the higher-dimensional interior, described using gravity. The boundary carries a quantum theory that describes the same system without its own dynamical gravity.
The double arrow means that physical questions can be translated between the descriptions. It is not a beam projecting objects into the interior. The drawn waves suggest field activity; they are not a literal map of individual particles to matching boundary bumps.
The boundary in this lower panel represents the boundary used in a holographic duality. It should not be confused with the black-hole horizon pictured above. Time is omitted from the drawing.
It is also not Trevor's Klein bottle, whatever the passing resemblance. His desk ornament has no well-defined inside or outside because of its topology, a fact about the shape of glass and nothing to do with holography. The boundary above has two perfectly ordinary sides; the interesting part is that each side's physics can be translated into the other's.
An example that does some work: holographic duality (often taught under the label AdS/CFT). AdS is Anti-de Sitter space, a particular negatively-curved gravitational geometry; CFT is a Conformal Field Theory, a quantum theory living on its boundary with no gravity of its own. In plain language: a world with gravity in one description can match a world without gravity in another — same bookkeeping, different nouns. Do not marry the acronym. Keep the idea: two descriptions, one structure.
In plain terms: "negatively curved" means space that bends away from itself, like a saddle rather than a ball, and "conformal" means a description that only cares about angles and shapes, not absolute size.
In Special Relativity, different observers use different coordinates. In General Relativity, different coordinate systems can describe the same geometry. In holographic duality, dramatically different-looking theories can describe the same physical system.
That is considerably more useful than saying the universe looks a bit like a hologram. It lets physicists turn a difficult problem into a different problem they may be able to solve. Derek would approve of the method. He would object to being billed for both calculations.
None of this establishes that our own universe has the particular holographic description shown here. It does not make Derek imaginary, prove a multiverse, or provide a mechanism for tomorrow's murder notice to arrive today. Penny's cinema trip may help her ask a better question. It cannot supply the experimental evidence.
<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 12
Neither can Jamie Lee Curtis, despite an impressive ability to survive changes of genre.


Lesson checkpoint
Black-hole entropy scales with horizon area. That helped motivate holographic descriptions in which bulk gravity and boundary quantum physics encode the same system.
The boundary is not a cinema screen projecting an unreal world. 
The claim is an equivalence between physical descriptions in particular theoretical settings.

Exit Form
If a spherical black hole's horizon radius doubles, what happens to its Bekenstein-Hawking entropy?
It becomes four times as large, because the horizon area becomes four times as large.


Lesson 12
Not Actually a Multiverse
Module D · Dual Descriptions 
Case link: Chapter 13
Learning objective: Use multiple descriptions as a thinking tool without confusing metaphor with evidence for a multiverse.

Different coordinates. Same events. Same invariant interval.
Optional detour: You do not need this chapter to understand or solve the murder. It follows a thread from relativity into more speculative territory. Treat it as context, not evidence on the same footing as the Lorentz transformation.
→ Glossary: Lorentz Transformation
Penny has decided that Derek is becoming unbearable.
Not because he is wrong. That would be easier.
She does the sensible thing when confronted with a man who is annoyingly correct: she goes to the cinema. Alone.
The film is Everything Everywhere All at Once.
She intends to spend two hours thinking about something other than Derek. Instead, she becomes absorbed in the film's multiverse, live-texts him an increasingly coherent argument about descriptions of reality, and ends the evening contemplating the cosmic significance of a cucumber.
Somewhere in London, a strange clock stops at 11:17.
Again. So much for a night off.
Between interdimensional laundromat trouble and hot-dog fingers, Penny has stumbled into an unusually productive physics seminar. Jamie Lee Curtis provides the career longevity. Penny provides the notebooks.
The important part begins when she stops taking the multiverse literally.
The film's alternative lives are a storytelling device. They are not a working model of the universe, and they do not explain quantum mechanics. Physicists discuss several quite different ideas under the heading "multiverse"; the film is under no obligation to represent any of them.
Treat it as you would the ordinary cucumber: an excellent aid to intuition and completely inadmissible as evidence.
What catches Penny's attention is a question the book has been asking since Chapter 2:
What can change in a description without changing the thing being described?
Consider a sentence translated from English into German. The words change. The grammar changes. With a good translation, the meaning survives.
Physics has its own versions of this distinction. Coordinates label events. Change the labels correctly, and you have not changed the events.
This does not mean every measured number stays the same. Different observers can measure different distances and durations. The requirement is that the descriptions fit together consistently: once you account for who is measuring what, they must agree on the prediction for the same physical experiment.
Penny types:
Reality should survive a change of stationery.
Derek begins a reply containing the words more precisely.
She puts her phone face down.
You have already seen the principle at work. In Chapter 2, observers moving relative to one another assigned different distances and time separations to the same pair of events. For certain pairs — those too far apart for light to travel between them in the available time — they could even disagree about which happened first.
Yet they agreed on the spacetime interval: a particular combination of spatial and temporal separation.
→ Glossary: Spacetime Interval
Different numbers. One invariant relationship.

There is a distinction worth keeping here. An observable is a quantity associated with a possible measurement. An invariant is a quantity unchanged under the transformations being considered. Those are not interchangeable definitions. A measured speed, for example, can depend on the observer.
Penny's point survives the correction. A change of description must preserve the physical content, even when it changes the numbers used to express it.
The same duality Lesson 11 called AdS/CFT — a gravitational space matched to a boundary quantum theory with no gravity of its own — is doing the same job here: same bookkeeping, different nouns, new context.
This goes well beyond relabelling coordinates. The two descriptions use different theoretical machinery, yet the correspondence identifies their physical content.
It is rather like Weinstein writing his patent notes in German and Trevor recording the same invention in English. Establish the translation, and you can compare what they actually mean.
The choice of language does not get either of them out of the patent dispute.
From row F, Penny notices the resemblance. First, different coordinates describe the same events. Then, in a much more demanding theoretical setting, apparently different theories describe the same physics.
She asks the next question.
What if her own life story is also a description she has mistaken for the thing itself?
This is where she leaves the established argument and heads towards the foyer of philosophy.
A worldline is an object's path through spacetime: its physical history. Changing coordinates changes how that path is represented. It does not give the object a different past.
→ Glossary: Worldline
Penny missing a train and Penny catching it are different histories. They are not the same history written in English and German.
Likewise, the story she tells about her life is not identical to her worldline. It selects events, assigns importance, supplies motives and occasionally edits Derek out.
Still, the analogy has unsettled her. Perhaps the account that feels most natural is not the only useful account. Perhaps understanding something sometimes means finding a description in which a previously hidden relationship becomes obvious.
She picks up her phone.
Stop investigating the clocks. Start investigating the descriptions.
This is excellent advice for a theoretical physicist and questionable advice for someone investigating a murder involving clocks.
Derek continues investigating the clocks.
Without quite meaning to, Penny has also approached the question Sherlock the dolphin will raise properly in Chapter 16: could spacetime itself emerge from more basic relationships, rather than provide the stage on which those relationships occur?
That is a speculative next step. It does not follow automatically from coordinate invariance or from a film about alternative lives. It asks whether some of the structure we take for granted might have a deeper explanation.
Penny has not proved it.
She has found a question worth asking.
And that brings us towards tensors.
→ Glossary: Tensor
A tensor is a mathematical object whose components change according to definite rules when the coordinate system changes. Those rules let us distinguish the underlying object from the numbers used to represent it.
Here is the short version, ahead of the careful one: a tensor is what a vector becomes once it has to track several directions' worth of information at once, and it still has to look sensible no matter which coordinate grid you draw it on.
The components may look different. The object has not changed.
This is what makes tensors so useful in General Relativity. They allow relationships involving spacetime geometry, matter and energy to be expressed consistently across coordinate systems. You can change the mathematical viewpoint without changing the physical claim.
As Chapter 15 puts it, a tensor is what happens when a vector gets promoted and starts having opinions about several directions at once.
Penny sympathises. She came to the cinema with one opinion about Derek and now has several.
For the investigation, keep four things:
1. The film's multiverse is a metaphor. Do not cite Marvel in an exam.
2. Different descriptions can preserve the same physical content. Coordinate changes and theoretical dualities illustrate this in different ways; they are not the same operation.
3. An observable need not be invariant. Measurements can depend on the observer, while valid descriptions remain consistent about what a given experiment will show.
4. Tensors give that consistency mathematical form. Their components transform; the physical relationships they express survive.
<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 13

Penny's question — what if the natural story is only one description — is allowed as a question. It does not license a multiverse as Case 1047's murderer. The invariant interval is still the interval. The film is still a film.
The larger questions can wait for the dolphin.
For now, the clock still reads 11:17, Derek is still annoyingly correct, and Jamie Lee Curtis has once again survived in accordance with the previously established Law of Conservation of Actresses.
Americans.
That settles it.



Lesson checkpoint
The film is a metaphor for thinking about different descriptions, not an interpretation of quantum mechanics and not evidence for a multiverse.
An analogy can illuminate a concept without proving the concept. We have no proof that a Multiverse exists.

Exit Form
Does a film about parallel universes establish a physical multiverse?
No.


Lesson 13
Curved Corridors and Tides
Module D · Dual Descriptions
Case link: Chapter 14
Learning objective: Recognise curvature via tidal effects / relative free-fall paths, not via a single 'downward force.'
→ Glossary: Curvature · Tidal Force

Tidal gravity: two initially parallel free-fall paths converge as they pass near a mass — curvature made visible.
The narrow corridor with twelve or thirteen doors is absurd. The underlying idea is not. In curved spacetime, local measurements can differ from measurements made over larger regions.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 14

The crucial concept is tidal gravity. If gravity were perfectly uniform everywhere, you could locally reproduce its effects simply by accelerating your reference frame. But real gravitational fields vary from place to place. That variation produces tidal effects.
For example, Earth's gravitational field is slightly stronger closer to Earth's centre than farther away. 
Two freely falling objects initially moving together can therefore gradually move apart or together.
Picture two people falling towards the Earth, a few metres apart. Both are falling "straight down" — but down means towards the centre of the planet, and the centre is a single point. Their two straight-down directions are not quite parallel; they tilt very slightly towards each other, the way two people walking due north from different points on the equator slowly converge towards the pole. Nobody pushed them together. The geometry did. Chapter 14 puts exactly this argument in Derek's mouth, with two people rather than two clocks, right before the corridor stops behaving.
That gradual convergence — or, elsewhere, a gradual separation — is what "geodesic deviation" means. Geodesics are the straight-as-possible paths freely falling objects follow. Deviation is the technical word for the fact that two nearby ones do not stay the same distance apart in curved spacetime. Flat spacetime has no such effect; parallel geodesics stay parallel forever, which is more or less the definition of flat. Curved spacetime always has some deviation, however small, and its size and direction are exactly what the curvature tensors from Lesson 15 are built to keep track of.
→ Glossary: Geodesic · Tensor

The narrow corridor joke is really about something quite respectable: 
Curvature reveals itself through how neighbouring free-fall paths behave relative to one another. The narrow corridor did not actually gain a door. Probably.
Forty-seven metres is a joke with a pedigree: it is far enough for a tide to stop pretending to be a uniform field, and close enough that nobody can call it cosmology. Chapter 14 is Lesson 4's leftover, walking.


Lesson checkpoint
Curvature reveals itself through the relative behaviour of neighbouring free-fall paths; this is the physics of tidal gravity.
'Gravity can be transformed away' is only a local statement.

Exit Form
What survives as evidence of curvature when a uniform gravitational field can be mimicked by acceleration?
Tidal effects.


Lesson 14
The Train Arrived Before It Left
Module E · Causality & Capstone
Case link: Chapter 15
Learning objective: Separate spacelike from timelike separation; say when time-order can and cannot reverse between frames.

The light cone: only events inside it can causally influence, or be influenced by, the event at its tip.
→ Glossary: Light Cone

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 15

That sounds like a violation of causality.
But before screaming "time machine", we need to distinguish coordinate descriptions from actual causal structure.
→ Glossary: Causal Structure
For an event, spacetime can be divided into: its causal past, its causal future,
In relativity, different observers can disagree about the temporal ordering of spacelike-separated events. For causally connected events, all inertial observers agree on their temporal order. Relativity does not let an ordinary cause arrive after its effect merely by changing coordinates. The light cone is the key.
and regions that cannot be causally connected to it without exceeding the speed of light. The light-cone structure — what can causally influence what — is invariant. 

If Derek sees something that appears to happen before its cause, we should first ask:
Are those events actually causally connected? If not, the apparent reversal may simply be a consequence of the coordinate system. If they are causally connected and the story really reverses their order, then we have left ordinary Special Relativity and need some additional physics — or a very good explanation from the Relativistic Investigation Bureau.
At which point Derek should probably stop buying train tickets.
Events that are spacelike-separated can change order between frames. Events on a causal journey cannot. A train that "arrives before it left" is either two events wearing one noun, or a coordinate stunt. It is not a ticket to yesterday. Penrose's Andromeda paradox is the same lesson at galactic volume: a tilt of "now" is not a telegram.
→ Glossary: Andromeda Paradox



Lesson checkpoint
Only spacelike-separated events can have their time ordering reversed between inertial frames without contradicting relativity.
Relativity does not allow the departure and arrival of one ordinary causal journey to swap order merely by changing coordinates.

Exit Form
If two events are causally connected, can an inertial observer simply transform them into the opposite temporal order?
No.


Lesson 15
The Dolphin and Final Geometry
Module E · Causality & Capstone
Case link: Chapter 16
Learning objective: State what Einstein's equation relates (geometry ↔ matter-energy), with just enough tensor vocabulary to read the equation, not to compute with it.
→ Glossary: Einstein Equation · Tensor

The Einstein field equation as a loop: matter tells spacetime how to curve, and spacetime tells matter how to move.
And now Sherlock. A dolphin communicating General Relativity telepathically is not established physics. The Einstein field equations are.
Tensors: Vectors' slightly more ambitious cousins. A vector is already rather clever.
It can tell you something like:
"Go three metres east and two metres north."
It has components — for instance, v = (vx, vy, vz) — and those components change when you change coordinates, but the underlying vector itself is the same physical thing. 
A tensor takes this idea one step further. At an introductory level, you can think of a tensor as a mathematical object that describes relationships between several directions at once.
Very roughly:
Scalar: one number.
Vector: directional components.
Tensor: components describing relationships between directions.
So, in the spirit of the Relativistic Investigation Bureau: 
A tensor is what happens when a vector gets promoted and starts having opinions about several directions simultaneously. 
That is a useful analogy. It is not the formal mathematical definition.
A tensor is not simply a "higher-dimensional vector." What matters is that its components obey specific transformation rules when we change coordinates, so that the underlying physical object remains well-defined.
And that is exactly why tensors are so useful in General Relativity. Spacetime can be curved. Coordinates can be awkward.
Observers can accelerate. Someone can be falling into a black hole while someone else is sitting safely in an office complaining about the coffee. We need mathematical objects that behave properly when we change coordinates.
Tensors do precisely that. The most important tensor is called the metric tensor, written gμν. It tells us how spacetime is measured: ds² = gμν dxμ dxν, a combination of regular distances in space plus a time component. 
μ and ν each range over the four spacetime directions — one time, three space — and a repeated index means: add up the matching term for all four. That bookkeeping convention is why the equation looks short. It is quietly doing sixteen additions in shorthand.
→ Glossary: Metric
The metric can vary from place to place.
So physicists combine the changes in all the relevant directions according to the metric, and get the spacetime interval.
→ Glossary: Spacetime Interval
The metric is therefore rather like the instruction manual for measuring spacetime. It tells us about: distances, times, angles, causal relationships, and the geometry of spacetime itself. Once you know the metric, an enormous amount of physics follows.
The next is the stress-energy tensor, written Tμν, describing the distribution and flow of matter and energy. Trevor once called it "the universe's spreadsheet."
→ Glossary: Stress-Energy Tensor
Weinstein did not approve. He did, however, admit that the analogy wasn't completely useless.
Then come the curvature tensors — refined and packaged for the field equation as the Einstein tensor, Gμν. Curvature is not adequately described by a single number; the tensors keep track of how directions fail to stay parallel when carried around spacetime.
→ Glossary: Curvature
"Space is a bit bent over there."
That would be charming but mathematically inadequate. Curvature has structure. It can differ in different directions.
It can affect neighbouring paths differently. It can produce tidal effects. Tensors are the machinery that lets us describe all of this without losing the physics every time somebody changes coordinates.
→ Glossary: Tidal Force
The Einstein field equation answers, in compressed mathematical form, how the geometry of spacetime is related to the distribution of matter and energy: Gμν + Λgμν = 8πG/c⁴ Tμν, where Λ is the cosmological constant, accounting for the universe's own accelerating expansion. (The G on the right, in 8πG/c⁴, is Newton's ordinary gravitational constant — not the Einstein tensor Gμν on the left, despite the shared letter.) 
Stretch that expansion by hand, with recession speed and redshift updating live, at the cosmology demo.

Or, as Derek would put it:
"Matter tells spacetime how to curve, and spacetime tells matter how to behave."
Trevor would then say:
"Well, that narrows it down."
And for once, he would be right.
The equation relates geometry to matter-energy. It does not name a killer. Lesson 16 will say the rest: the brass clock displayed an event coordinate. Case 1047 was a drawer error with excellent production values.
Nobody spoke for a moment. Even Trevor seemed to sense that the equation deserved it.
But Is General Relativity the Final Answer? 
Not necessarily.
General Relativity works extraordinarily well. But it does not appear to provide the complete description of nature at the quantum level. Black-hole singularities and the earliest moments of the universe are obvious warning signs.
→ Glossary: Singularity
This is where quantum gravity enters. Research programmes involving: quantum mechanics,
holography, entanglement, information,
and emergent spacetime raise the possibility that spacetime itself might not be fundamental. 
That is an active research idea, not an established conclusion.

<- Chapter for this lesson: Chapter 16

If a deeper theory eventually explains this, General Relativity would not become "wrong."
It would become something like thermodynamics. 
Temperature is real. Pressure is real.
Neither is a fundamental microscopic object. 
They emerge from deeper physics. Spacetime might conceivably be similar.
And this brings us back to Weinstein's original problem. Perhaps the final theory does not replace General Relativity. Perhaps it would explain why General Relativity works so remarkably well.
That would be a considerably more interesting achievement. And Sherlock, being a dolphin, naturally has no tenure problems.



Lesson checkpoint
Tensors let physical laws be expressed consistently across different coordinate systems; the Einstein field equation relates spacetime geometry to matter and energy.
Emergent spacetime and quantum gravity are active research areas, not completed theories.

Exit Form
What is on the geometric side of Einstein's field equation?
Curvature, packaged in the Einstein tensor plus the cosmological-constant term.


Lesson 16
Capstone: The Clock Was Lying

Module E · Causality & Capstone · Case link: Epilogue / Case 1047. Learning objective (capstone): Distinguish event coordinates from proper time; explain how Case 1047 can look like a completed murder without a murderer.
→ Glossary: Proper Time
The brass clock did not malfunction, and it did not see the future. It occasionally displayed something else entirely: an event coordinate — a label attached to a record — instead of its own proper time, the time it had actually measured by ticking.
The clock displayed the record's label.



11:03:17 identified the event in the Bureau's system. It was not a reading the brass clock had reached by ticking.
Those are not the same kind of thing. Proper time is what a clock reads by doing its job: ticking along its own worldline, one tick after another, at its own pace. An event coordinate is a tag in somebody's description of spacetime — a name for where and when something happens, assigned within a particular coordinate system. 
→ Glossary: Worldline
Mistaking a coordinate for a measurement is exactly the trap this book has been warning about, chapter after chapter.
That distinction explains the case, clue by clue. 11:17 and 12:04 were departure and arrival coordinates for the mysterious train, assigned within whatever description produced the impossible station; 
Derek's own watch, ticking his own proper time throughout, read 11:18 — a completely ordinary clock doing a completely ordinary job. The record itself did not arrive from the future. It arrived through the same anomalous geometry responsible for the extra doors and the impossible station elsewhere in this book, filed according to its own event coordinate rather than Derek's local proper time.
None of this required the Bureau's computer to predict anything. It required only that it confuse two different kinds of "when" — exactly the confusion this entire investigation turned out to be.
The Epilogue's resolution turns on a distinction this book has been building since Chapter 3: coordinates are not reality, they are descriptions of reality.



Lesson checkpoint
An event coordinate is a label within a description of spacetime; proper time is what a clock actually measures along its own path. Confusing the two produces exactly the kind of "impossible" result this book has been full of.
A record that looks like it "knows the future" may simply be labelled with someone else's coordinate rather than your own proper time.

Exit Form
If a clock shows a value nobody's watch actually ticked through, is the clock lying — or is somebody reading the wrong number off it?
The clock isn't lying. Somebody's reading a coordinate as if it were a measurement.
SUMMARY
Final exam (open book, closed superstition): In one paragraph, explain why Case 1047 can present a completed murder photograph without a murderer, using only: event, reference frame, event coordinate, proper time, and causal structure. If your answer works equally well with magic instead of relativity, you have failed the course — retake Module E.
→ Glossary: Causal Structure

Here is what you can now explain, presumably.
Two observers can assign different durations to the same pair of events without either clock being broken. Clocks following different paths through spacetime can also record different elapsed times between reunions.
The speed of light is the same for all inertial observers.
Special Relativity works locally inside General Relativity: in a sufficiently small, freely falling laboratory, its familiar rules apply.
In General Relativity, gravity can be understood in the context of spacetime geometry.
Spacetime curvature produces tidal effects: nearby freely falling objects can converge, separate, or shift relative to one another.
→ Glossary: Curvature · Tidal Force
A singularity marks a limit of the classical spacetime description — not simply a place where gravity is unusually strong.
→ Glossary: Singularity
Coordinate descriptions can differ while invariant physics remains the same.
Holographic duality relates gravitational and lower-dimensional nongravitational descriptions in particular theoretical settings. It does not establish that our universe is literally a cinema screen.
Tensors describe geometric and physical quantities whose components transform consistently under coordinate changes.
→ Glossary: Tensor
An ordinary cucumber might have a role in an experiment. Its contribution to quantum gravity remains unestablished.

Congratulations!
You now understand the essential ideas. You may safely discuss relativity at dinner. Or you can have another piece of cake. The second option is generally shorter and, in Derek's experience, no less illuminating.
The universe, for its part, remains indifferent to both choices.
There is, however, one final question. Derek's question. The question that has been lurking underneath the entire book: If measurements of space and time depend on the observer, if gravity is described by curvature, if spacetime geometry might itself emerge from deeper physics, and if different descriptions can encode the same reality... what, exactly, is reality?
Well. That's the problem.
We haven't finished. 
But the good news is that neither has the universe. Which is rather reassuring.
GLOSSARY
Andromeda Paradox. Penrose's illustration that a small change of velocity on Earth can swing a distant "now"-slice across days on Andromeda. It is a statement about simultaneity for spacelike separation, not a method for sending news to Andromeda.
Block Universe. The view that past, present, and future events are equally part of a four-dimensional whole. Relativity's geometry is friendly to it; it is not a theorem that free will is a clerical error.
Causal Structure. The pattern of which events can influence which others, determined by spacetime's light cones and how they connect. Coordinate systems are negotiable; these causal relationships are not.
Curvature. The departure of spacetime geometry from flatness, revealed by effects such as nearby freely falling objects accelerating relative to one another. Matter and energy influence curvature, but spacetime can be curved even in empty regions. No external dimension is required for it to bend into.
Decoherence. The suppression of observable quantum interference as a system becomes entangled with its environment. It helps explain why everyday objects — including cucumbers — behave classically. By itself, it does not explain why a measurement yields one particular outcome.
Einstein Equation. The central equation of general relativity, usually called the Einstein field equations. It relates spacetime curvature to the energy, momentum, and stresses of matter and fields. Matter influences geometry; geometry guides motion. The mathematics takes somewhat longer.
Einstein Synchronisation. The convention that two distant clocks are set by assuming the one-way speed of light is the same in both directions. The two-way speed is measured; the one-way split is chosen.
Equivalence Principle. In a sufficiently small, freely falling laboratory, nongravitational experiments behave as they would in an inertial laboratory without gravity. Over larger regions, tidal effects can reveal the difference. A foundation of general relativity, and the reason lifts feature so heavily in physics thought experiments.
Event Coordinate. A name for where-and-when inside a chosen description of spacetime. Not proper time. A label, not a measurement of elapsed time along any particular clock's path.
Event Horizon. The boundary of a black hole's region of no escape: once inside, no future-directed signal can reach the distant exterior. Not light. Not a strongly worded letter.
Geodesic. The straightest possible path in a given geometry. Ideal freely falling objects follow spacetime geodesics when no nongravitational forces act on them. 
GPS Correction. The adjustment applied to satellite clocks for both special-relativistic slowing from orbital speed and general-relativistic speeding from weaker gravity. Both effects are real, and they point in opposite directions; skipping either term produces a map that lies.
Hafele–Keating Experiment. A 1971 measurement in which atomic clocks flown around the world accumulated different proper time from clocks that stayed behind, in the direction relativity predicted once both speed and altitude were accounted for.
Holographic Principle. The proposal, motivated by black-hole entropy, that a gravitational region's physical information can be described by degrees of freedom associated with a boundary of one fewer spatial dimension. Holographic dualities give this idea concrete form in particular theoretical settings. Its application to our universe remains an open question.
Light Cone. The boundary between lightlike directions and the timelike or spacelike directions at an event. Its future and past portions show the local limits on where signals can go and where they can come from. Cucumbers must remain within the speed limit.
Lorentz Transformation. A transformation relating space and time coordinates in inertial frames while preserving the spacetime interval. Transformations between frames in relative motion underpin time dilation, length contraction, and the relativity of simultaneity.
Manifold. A space that can be described in small patches using ordinary coordinates, even when its overall shape or connectivity is more complicated. Earth's surface is a familiar example. A manifold provides the underlying space; a metric supplies its geometry.
Metric. The mathematical object that determines local spacetime intervals and allows lengths and elapsed times to be calculated along paths. Its components depend on the coordinates chosen. Complicated coordinates can make even flat spacetime look mathematically unwelcoming.
Pending Geometry. The Bureau's filing category for an event that looks like a crime only if you insist on a shared, universal "now." Not a punchline: the correct drawer for a coordinate label mistaken for a corpse.
Pound–Rebka Experiment. A 1960 measurement of gravitational redshift over 22.5 metres of a tower at Harvard. Honest clocks, a small height difference, and a real, measurable disagreement.
Proper Time. The elapsed time measured by an ideal clock along its own path through spacetime. Observers may use different coordinates, but they agree on what that clock records between two events on its path.
Reference Frame. A systematic way of assigning coordinates to events, usually tied to an observer's state of motion. An inertial frame is one in which free objects keep constant velocity.
Simultaneity. The assignment of the same time to different events. Spatially separated events that are simultaneous in one inertial frame need not be simultaneous in another. Neither frame is mistaken; their definitions of "now" differ.
Singularity. A breakdown or incompleteness of the classical spacetime description, often associated with unbounded curvature. Technically, a key sign is a freely falling or lightlike path that cannot be continued despite reaching its end in a finite path parameter. A singularity need not be an ordinary point within spacetime. The map itself has run out.
Spacetime Interval. The invariant combination of spatial and temporal separation. In special relativity, inertial observers agree on it even when they disagree about distance and duration separately. In curved spacetime, the metric defines the corresponding local interval between infinitesimally nearby events.
Stress-Energy Tensor. The tensor describing energy density, momentum density, energy flow, pressure, and shear stresses. It supplies the matter-and-fields side of the Einstein field equations. It constrains curvature, although it does not by itself determine the entire spacetime geometry.
Tensor. A mathematical object whose components change according to precise rules when coordinates change, leaving the object itself unchanged. Scalars and vectors are simple examples; the metric and curvature tensors are more elaborate ones. Different numbers, consistent physics.
Tidal Force. The relative acceleration of nearby freely falling objects, producing stretching or squeezing. In general relativity, these effects reveal spacetime curvature. Their strength depends on the circumstances.
Time Dilation. Differences in clock rates associated with relative motion or gravitational circumstances. Two inertial observers in relative motion can each assign a slower rate to the other's clock, using their own frame's definition of simultaneity. Gravitational comparisons are different: for stationary clocks around Earth, the lower clock runs more slowly than the higher one. The mutual-slowing argument does not apply to that comparison.

White Hole. The time-reverse of a black hole in the equations: a region from which matter and light can only emerge, never enter. A mathematical solution, not an observed object.
Worldline. The path an object traces through spacetime: where it is and when it is there. Your biography, with fewer adjectives and more coordinates.
Wormhole. A hypothetical connection between otherwise separate or distant regions of spacetime. Some solutions of general relativity contain wormholes, but traversable versions typically require exotic stress-energy. Mathematical availability does not guarantee a usable passage.
About the Author
The alleged author Spezala Genara Relavi (S.G.R.) has never been formally accused of understanding General Relativity, though several of her fictitious colleagues maintain that she understands it hopefully better than she does the Special One. She divides her time between London, Munich, and a rather small pink filing cabinet labelled  
[ PRE-TRANSFORMATION / DO NOT OBSERVE ]
Spezala Genara Relavi is the author of The Relativistic Investigation Bureau series, allegedly. This so-called masterpiece is indeed Exhibit One, apparently. 
Ms Relavi has not yet been murdered, although she understands this might be subject to change without further notice, possibly.


The real author, Lothar J. Musiol, exists in a more conventional sense and is responsible for the nonfiction behind the weird work of fiction.
He is German by birth, American, by choice, and writes in British because he regards it as the most awkward American dialect. For exactly that reason he is a staunch aficionado of British motion pictures, such as Four Weddings and a Funeral.
The choice of preposterous accent, however, was rather inspired by a scene in Love Actually, where a daft sod by the name of Colin Frissell had a conversation in an American bar before he arrived at the true purpose of his travels, quite obviously. The author found that eye-opening and still hopes to get that also one day, if he continues his admittedly naff use of British dialect just long enough.

FURTHER READING

For the standard physics behind length contraction and the distinction between an observer's measured length and the object's rest-frame length, see Stanford's Einstein's Spacetime overview and its Relativity Questions and Answers resource on length contraction.
For the broader structure of Special and General Relativity, the Einstein's Spacetime resource provides a concise overview of Lorentz transformations, local inertial frames and curved spacetime.
ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
Thanks are owed to General Relativity, for holding up long enough to be explained; to Special Relativity, for the same, on a shorter and faster-moving basis; and to the ordinary cucumber, for its continued and largely uncomplaining service to physics education — and then some, as Penny might agree.
I'd call it "mission accomplished" if this book managed to spark some interest — even if, frankly speaking, you couldn't be arsed before.
I'd be rather less pleased, though, if this book managed to make you think, *Now I can't be arsed*, when you used to be absolutely mad about relativity before reading this piece of... er... art.
Anyway, kudos to everyone who read an early version of this manuscript and did not, in fact, ask for their afternoon back. Their forbearance was noted and is filed under Pending Gratitude.
And to you, the reader, thanks for making it this far without once asking the Bureau for a refund. The Bureau appreciates this more than its paperwork will admit.
Coming Next in the Relativistic Investigation Bureau Series
Weinstein was staring at a sheet of paper. It had not been there a moment earlier. At the top was the familiar heading:
RELATIVISTIC INVESTIGATION BUREAU.
Underneath it were written four words:
CASE STATUS: NOT YET OCCURRED.
Below the heading was a date. Tomorrow. Below the date was a location — a railway station that did not exist. Below the location was a final line.
DO NOT ALLOW DEREK GENT TO
BOARD THE MYSTERIOUS TRAIN.
On the back was another strange message.
PLEASE NOTE: THE PREVIOUS WARNING WAS SENT TOO LATE.
Then the quiet office door opened. No one was standing there. Beyond the doorway was a railway platform. There had never been a railway platform there. A distant train waited in the darkness. Its destination board displayed one word.
YESTERDAY.
"Well," Penny observed, "that saves us the trouble of finding the station. The ship has left the barn, apparently."
Derek put the paper in his pocket. "Nobody boards that train."
From somewhere inside the carriage came three soft knocks. A voice followed. "Derek?"
It was his own voice — but not his present voice. This was Derek from somewhere later, somewhere he had already learned something.
"Whatever you do," the voice from the mysterious train said slowly, "do not open the door. You already know what's inside."
