The clocks have stopped agreeing again. Somewhere beneath London, a train that should not exist is running to a timetable nobody has written yet, and Derek Gent has just received a case file dated tomorrow, from a sender who has not been born.
Herbert Matkowski calls it a coordinate problem. 
Alfred Weinstein calls it interesting. 
Penny calls it Tuesday. 
Tabitha, when asked, changed the subject. At home, Gucci had already worked it out and was refusing to discuss it either.
The Relativistic Investigation Bureau returns in Book Two, in which the Bureau discovers that some mysteries do not merely repeat themselves — they insist on it.
Allegedly.
BIBLIOGRAPHY
Einstein, Albert. Relativity: The Special and General Theory. Translated by Robert W. Lawson. Methuen, 1920. Originally published in German in 1916. COMMENT: Einstein's original exposition. Difficulty: High — dense and mathematical, but this is where the real physics lives. Patience required.
Epstein, Lewis Carroll. Relativity Visualized. Insight Press, 1985. COMMENT: Visual approach to relativity. Difficulty: Medium — highly recommended for intuitive understanding, much more accessible than Einstein's original.
Barbour, Julian B. The End of Time: The Next Revolution in Physics. Oxford University Press, 2000. COMMENT: Explores whether time is fundamental or emergent. Difficulty: Medium-High — more speculative, but grounded in modern physics.
Minkowski, Hermann. "Space and Time." In The Principle of Relativity, translated by Meghnad Saha, University Press, Calcutta, 1920, pp. 70 — 88. COMMENT: Minkowski's geometric approach to spacetime. Difficulty: Very High — short but exceptionally dense; mathematical notation can be challenging for modern readers. Only for dedicated readers willing to work through the mathematics.
Mermin, N. David. It's About Time: Understanding Einstein's Relativity. Princeton University Press, 2005. COMMENT: Possibly the most readable relativity book on this list — conversational, minimal maths, written for genuinely curious non-physicists.
Gamow, George. Mr Tompkins in Wonderland. Cambridge University Press, 1940. COMMENT: The original relativity-made-friendly book, decades before the rest of this list existed. Mr Tompkins falls asleep and dreams of a city where the speed of light is walking pace — trams contract, cyclists age slower than pedestrians, and nobody finds any of it as alarming as they should. Difficulty: Low. If you want to know what it might feel like to actually live inside Special Relativity, rather than merely calculate it, start here.
Muller, Richard A. Now: The Physics of Time. W. W. Norton, 2016. COMMENT: A working physicist's attempt to explain why "now" feels so real when relativity insists it shouldn't be universal — which is, not coincidentally, the exact problem the Bureau spent a week mis-filing. Difficulty: Medium. Argues time's flow may be more fundamental than most physicists admit, which Weinstein would have opinions about.
Cox, Brian, and Jeff Forshaw. Why Does E=mc²? (And Why Should We Care?). Da Capo Press, 2009. COMMENT: A patient, readable derivation of the equation everyone quotes and almost nobody can actually get to. Difficulty: Medium. Builds the result up from the same postulates this book keeps joking about, minus the dolphin.
Susskind, Leonard. The Black Hole War: My Battle with Stephen Hawking to Make the World Safe for Quantum Mechanics. Little, Brown and Company, 2008. COMMENT: The physicist who helped found the holographic principle, recounting the decades-long fight over whether information falling into a black hole is truly lost. Difficulty: Medium. Covers the area-law entropy clue and the bulk/boundary picture properly — Derek's filing cabinet is a gentler analogy than anything in this book's actual title.
Wheeler, John Archibald. A Journey into Gravity and Spacetime. Scientific American Library, 1990. COMMENT: Wheeler practically invented the modern intuition for curved spacetime, and this is his own attempt to hand that intuition to a general reader — geometry and pictures doing the work equations do everywhere else on this list. Difficulty: Low-Medium. The natural next stop after Lesson 5's apple and Lesson 13's tidal corridor, from the physicist who saw GR most clearly in pictures.
Carroll, Sean. Spacetime and Geometry. Addison-Wesley, 2004. COMMENT: For the reader who finishes Lesson 15 and wants the tensors to stop being metaphors. Difficulty: High. Not required for Case 1047.
Geroch, Robert. General Relativity from A to B. University of Chicago Press, 1978. COMMENT: Almost no tensors; almost all geometry. Difficulty: Medium. The apple chapter, stretched into a short book.
Norton, John D. Einstein for Everyone. Online lecture notes, University of Pittsburgh. COMMENT: Already linked from Lesson 1's simultaneity page — a bibliography line so the notes survive if a hyperlink rots. Difficulty: Low-Medium. Honest about history and about what the equations do not say.
Penrose, Roger. The Emperor's New Mind. Oxford University Press, 1989. COMMENT: Use the simultaneity illustration; leave the rest of Penrose's argument for another case. Difficulty: Medium, where it stays on now-slices.
Rovelli, Carlo. The Order of Time. Riverhead Books, 2018. COMMENT: A working physicist arguing that time's apparent flow is not fundamental. Difficulty: Low-Medium. File next to Barbour, not on top of him — they are not the same programme.
Schutz, Bernard. A First Course in General Relativity. Cambridge University Press, 1985. COMMENT: The standard next course after this Appendix. Difficulty: High. Weinstein would assign it; Matkowski would collect the problem sets.
Taylor, Edwin F., and John Archibald Wheeler. Spacetime Physics. W. H. Freeman, 1992. COMMENT: The cleanest introductory drill on intervals, proper time, and diagrams. Difficulty: Medium. This is Lesson 2 with homework.

If you enjoyed this book, a short review wherever you bought it helps other readers find the Bureau.
Readers who would like the mathematics behind Derek's cases explained directly can find it in Lothar J. Musiol's nonfiction Physics, Actually series, which covers relativity with equations and considerably fewer fictional clocks.

The clocks remain, for the moment, silent.
Derek had, by then, more or less stopped asking them what he couldn't control. He still asked them what he could.
